| BMS map names use 7-character base names with no trailing `M` (e.g. `COSGN00.bms`) | The prompt's `COSGN00M` / `COMEN01M` names | The actual mapset files are 7 characters; corroborated by `CDEMO-LAST-MAP PIC X(7)`, which reserves exactly seven characters for a map name | Wrong identifiers would break DTO / route wiring `[app/bms/; app/cpy/COCOM01Y.cpy:L43]` |
| Preserve source misspellings `ACCT-EXPIRAION-DATE` and `CARD-EXPIRAION-DATE` | Silently "correct" the spelling to `ACCT-EXPIRATION-DATE` / `CARD-EXPIRATION-DATE` | Spec-literal fidelity: frozen-contract identifiers are preserved character-for-character; both fields are spelled without the second `T` throughout the source and program logic | Any "fix" breaks byte-identical field contracts `[app/cpy/CVACT01Y.cpy:L11; app/cpy/CVACT02Y.cpy:L9; app/cbl/CBTRN02C.cbl:L414]` |
| Exclude `app/cpy/UNUSED1Y.cpy` from active transformation | Transform all copybooks uniformly | It is an unused copybook with no consuming program | None; recorded for auditability `[app/cpy/UNUSED1Y.cpy]` |

## 6. Infrastructure, Observability & Review-Surfaced Deviations

Additional non-literal choices and deviations made while delivering the foundation tranche. Each is recorded here per the Explainability rule so the migration stays auditable.

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| `DailyTransaction` is a JPA `@Entity` mapped to the `daily_transactions` table and read through a Spring Data `JpaRepository` — **REVERSING** the earlier decision to model it as a non-persistent POJO behind an in-memory repository | Keep the plain POJO with its `ConcurrentHashMap` repository (the original choice) and delete the `daily_transactions` DDL together with its 300 seeded rows | The migration set creates `daily_transactions` and stages the 300-record `dailytran.txt` feed, but the repository backing `TransactionPostingJob`'s reader was an in-memory map, so the staged rows were UNREACHABLE and the job read an empty feed on every fresh deployment — table and seed alike were dead weight. `CVTRA06Y` describes a real VSAM data set (`AWS.M2.CARDDEMO.DALYTRAN…`), not a transient in-core structure, so a relational staging table IS the faithful analogue: the job now streams the staged rows in ascending `DALYTRAN-ID` order — pinned by an explicit `@Query`, because `JpaRepository.findAll()` guarantees no ordering — exactly as the sequential `READ` / `READNEXT` browse did. The two negative unit tests that had ENCODED the POJO decision were inverted to assert the entity mapping, per the rule that tests follow the specification rather than the reverse | The feed is a persistent table that must be reloaded per posting cycle (the job does not delete it, matching the legacy data set's lifecycle). Every service `@EntityScan`s the shared domain package, so the mapping is validated fleet-wide by `ddl-auto: validate` `[app/cpy/CVTRA06Y.cpy; app/jcl/POSTTRAN.jcl; app/cbl/CBTRN02C.cbl]` |
| Redis requires `--requirepass` AUTH + network isolation + no host exposure | Leave Redis open (image default) | Legacy CICS had no external session store; an unauthenticated, host-exposed Redis is a CWE-306 exposure. Redis now demands a password (externalized secret), is `ClusterIP`/no-published-port only, and is reachable solely by the services + gateway (NetworkPolicy) | Redis TLS in transit is a documented production follow-up (needs cert management) |
| Spring Session values serialized with a strict, allowlisted JSON serializer (`SessionRedisConfig`) | Default JDK (Java native) serialization | Native deserialization of session bytes is a CWE-502 risk; `GenericJacksonJsonRedisSerializer` + `BasicPolymorphicTypeValidator` (allowlist `com.carddemo.*`, `java.util.*`, `java.time.*`, `java.lang.*`) removes it. `carddemo-common` exposes no auto-configuration, so each service must activate it explicitly: `@Import(SessionRedisConfig.class)` is declared on ALL EIGHT web applications (`api-gateway`, `auth-service`, `user-service`, `account-service`, `card-service`, `transaction-service`, `billpay-service`, `reporting-service`) — the non-web `batch-service` holds no session. Verified at runtime: `HGETALL spring:session:sessions:<id>` returns JSON, never a JDK serialization stream, and `SessionRedisConfigTest` guards the serializer type plus the exact `springSessionDefaultRedisSerializer` bean name Spring Session resolves | Allowlist must cover every persisted session type; a service that omits the import silently reverts to JDK serialization, so the import is asserted by test |
| All credentials externalized to required env / per-service Secrets; observability UIs bound to loopback | Commit convenient default credentials | Predictable `carddemo/carddemo` and `admin/admin` defaults are a CWE-798 exposure. Compose uses `${VAR:?}` (fail-fast, `.env`-supplied); Kubernetes uses per-service Secret templates (SealedSecrets/ESO in real environments); Grafana admin is externalized; Prometheus/Grafana publish only on `127.0.0.1` | Operators must populate `.env` / inject real secrets before startup |
| Actuator health `show-details: when-authorized`; Prometheus scrape authenticated | `show-details: always`, anonymous scrape | Anonymous health detail / metrics leak internal topology (dependency hosts, versions). Detail renders only to an authenticated principal; Prometheus presents a dedicated `monitoring` principal (password from a mounted secret) | The service-side `monitoring` principal + management-chain HTTP basic are delivered with the bootable services; the scrape wiring is inert until then |
| Trace sampling 0.1 for services, 1.0 for the `api-gateway`; the local Docker Compose stack raises EVERY service to 1.0 | Uniform 1.0 everywhere; uniform 0.1 everywhere including local | The gateway is the entry point, so sampling every request there starts each trace; downstream services sample 0.1 to bound overhead. The gateway overrides the shared `0.1` via an explicit Deployment env var (explicit `env` wins over `envFrom`). Locally, `MANAGEMENT_TRACING_SAMPLING_PROBABILITY: "1.0"` on the Compose `x-backend-service` anchor guarantees that a single manual request produces a sampled, logged trace during verification — with 0.1 an operator would have to issue roughly ten requests to see one `traceId`, which makes the Observability rule impossible to verify by hand. The COMMITTED application default stays the production-safe 0.1 | Trace continuity relies on context propagation across the 0.1/1.0 boundary (standard for head-based sampling); local and production sampling rates differ by design |
| `DailyTransaction` is a plain POJO, NOT a JPA `@Entity` | Persist the daily feed as its own table | `CVTRA06Y` describes the daily-transaction FEED record consumed by the posting batch job, not a stored master table; the job reads the feed and writes `Transaction` rows. Modeling it as a non-persistent read model avoids a spurious table with no legacy datastore analogue | None; keeps the schema faithful to the VSAM inventory `[app/cpy/CVTRA06Y.cpy]` |
| Redis requires `--requirepass` AUTH; publishes no host port; NetworkPolicy is defence in depth, NOT the control | Leave Redis open (image default); or treat network isolation as sufficient | Legacy CICS had no external session store; an unauthenticated, host-exposed Redis is a CWE-306 exposure. Redis demands a password (externalized secret) and publishes no host port. **Correction of an earlier claim in this log:** the previous wording said Redis "is reachable solely by the services + gateway (NetworkPolicy)". In a local Docker Compose run that is not true — the compose bridge is routable from the host, so a TCP connection to Redis and PostgreSQL succeeds; what actually refuses the caller is the credential (`-NOAUTH Authentication required.` and `FATAL: password authentication failed`). `k8s/networkpolicy.yaml` supplies default-deny isolation in Kubernetes, but it must be APPLIED to have any effect and is therefore recorded as a second layer, never as the primary control | Redis TLS in transit remains a documented production follow-up (needs cert management); operators must apply the NetworkPolicy in clusters that support enforcement |
| Spring Session values serialized with a strict, allowlisted JSON serializer (`SessionRedisConfig`), imported by every session-bearing service, under the Boot 4 property namespace | Default JDK (Java native) serialization | Native deserialization of session bytes is a CWE-502 risk; `GenericJacksonJsonRedisSerializer` + `BasicPolymorphicTypeValidator` (allowlist `com.carddemo.*`, `java.util.*`, `java.time.*`, `java.lang.*`) removes it. **Correction of an earlier claim in this log:** the configuration existed but was imported by NO service, so JDK native serialization was in force (payloads began `\xac\xed\x00\x05`) and any downstream request carrying a gateway-issued cookie failed with `ClassNotFoundException` on `DefaultSavedRequest`. Three things were required to make the claim true: `@Import(SessionRedisConfig.class)` in every session-bearing service; removing the gateway's bare `@EnableRedisHttpSession`, which overrode auto-configuration and forced the default `spring:session` namespace; and migrating every `application.yml` from the inert Boot 3 keys `spring.session.redis.*` to Boot 4's `spring.session.data.redis.*`. The saved-request cache is also disabled, so no Spring Security type is ever written to the session at all | Allowlist must cover every persisted session type; activation is explicit per service. Sessions written by an older build are not readable after the namespace change — acceptable, as a sign-on re-issues one |
| All credentials externalized to required env / per-service Secrets; observability UIs bound to loopback; no framework-generated account anywhere | Commit convenient default credentials; accept Boot's auto-generated user | Predictable `carddemo/carddemo` and `admin/admin` defaults are a CWE-798 exposure. Compose uses `${VAR:?}` (fail-fast, `.env`-supplied); Kubernetes uses per-service Secret templates (SealedSecrets/ESO in real environments); Grafana admin is externalized; Prometheus/Grafana publish only on `127.0.0.1`. Separately, Spring Boot's `UserDetailsServiceAutoConfiguration` was creating an undocumented `user` principal in the gateway and user-service and printing its generated password to the log (CWE-798 / CWE-532). Declaring the `monitoring` `UserDetailsService` suppresses that auto-configuration, so no framework account is created and no credential is ever logged. The one remaining convenient default — the shared seeded user password — is now recorded explicitly in §3 as an accepted demo-only risk | Operators must populate `.env` / inject real secrets before startup; every service must keep a `UserDetailsService` bean or Boot's default account returns |
| Actuator health `show-details: when-authorized` with an anonymous status-only `/actuator/health`; telemetry restricted to a dedicated `monitoring` principal; every other management endpoint admin-only | `show-details: always`; anonymous scrape; or restrict `/actuator/health` too | Anonymous health detail and anonymous metrics leak internal topology (dependency hosts, JVM and OS versions, disk figures, every route URI template). **Correction of two earlier claims in this log:** the authenticated scrape was described as delivered, but `/actuator/prometheus` was anonymously readable on eight of nine services (71–185 KB each) because no service implemented the `monitoring` principal, while the ninth restricted it to `ROLE_ADMIN` and its Prometheus target sat permanently `down: 403`. Separately, user-service permitted only the liveness and readiness probes, so the shared compose healthcheck — which greps `/actuator/health` for `UP` — could never pass and the container stayed `unhealthy` (failing streak 112), a state that would block `depends_on: service_healthy` and any Kubernetes readiness gate; the Kubernetes `startupProbe` targeted the same restricted path. What ships now: `ManagementSecurityConfig` publishes an `@Order(1)` chain over `/actuator/prometheus` and `/actuator/metrics/**` requiring `ROLE_MONITORING` over HTTP basic, backed by a read-only in-memory `monitoring` principal whose password comes from `MONITORING_PASSWORD`; the overall health status (never its detail) is anonymous on all nine so the orchestrator probe passes; everything else requires `ROLE_ADMIN`. When no password is configured the principal is seeded with a random credential, so the endpoint fails CLOSED rather than opening. The credential must reach the services as well as Prometheus: `MONITORING_PASSWORD` is now injected into all nine compose services — including the api-gateway explicitly, because a service-level `environment` mapping REPLACES a YAML-anchor mapping instead of merging into it — and via a `carddemo-monitoring-secret` referenced by all nine Kubernetes Deployments | The compose/Kubernetes secret and the Prometheus `password_file` must hold the SAME value or every scrape target returns 401; a rejected scrape credential is audited on every service so a mismatch is visible immediately |
| Security events published as a structured, correlation-aware audit trail at WARN, recorded at the layer that has the request | No security logging; or log every Spring Security event | Brute force, credential stuffing, and boundary probing were undetectable: 30 failed sign-ons and ~120 gateway denials produced no per-event record anywhere. Records now cover authentication success and failure, account lockout, rate limiting, the `401` entry point, `403` denial, and session revocation — each naming the user id or principal, source address, HTTP method, masked path, and correlation id. Ownership is deliberate: the request-aware handlers own HTTP denials, while the event listeners cover only request-less denials (method security). Auditing a request-based `AuthorizationDeniedEvent` as well produced a duplicate record whose address, method, and path were all `unknown` and which reported an anonymous caller as an authorization denial rather than a missing authentication, so that case is now skipped. A request-less denial is named by `DeclaringClass#method`, never by the denied object's `toString`. The management chain builds its `AuthenticationManager` by hand, so it is given a `DefaultAuthenticationEventPublisher` explicitly — without it a rejected telemetry credential left no record at all | Every value written to the audit stream is CR/LF-stripped, PAN-masked, and length-bounded, so a crafted user id cannot forge or split a log line; volume is bounded by the rate limiter |
| Every failure rendered through the shared error body with a trace id; framework statuses preserved; security failures re-thrown | Leave framework failures to the container's default error page | The container default page carries no trace id (and an unmapped path returned exactly that), while `POST /auth/signon {}` returned `"traceId": null` on services that had no correlation filter. A catch-all handler now renders every failure through the shared body with a correlation-backed trace id, PRESERVING the declared status and response headers of any framework failure — `405` keeps its `Allow` header, `415` and an unmapped path's `404` keep their statuses. Spring Security's `AuthenticationException` and `AccessDeniedException` are re-thrown untouched so the filter chain still produces the configured `401`/`403` and its audit record; user-service relies on this for the `@PreAuthorize("hasRole('ADMIN')")` denial its method-security gate raises, which is live because `user-service` `SecurityConfig` carries `@EnableMethodSecurity` (the annotation is the only switch — without it the `@PreAuthorize` would be inert and would never reach this advice). An internal fault reports `500` with a fixed message, keeping the detail and stack trace in the log only. `HttpStatus.UNPROCESSABLE_ENTITY` is retained deliberately despite its deprecation: the replacement constant carries a different reason phrase, and that phrase is copied into the `error` field of every error body, so switching would silently change a published response field | A new `@ExceptionHandler` in the shared advice takes precedence over the framework resolver, so a future handler must not accidentally shadow a status-bearing exception |
| A body-less `401`/`403`, traceable by header rather than by payload | Return the shared error body for authentication and authorization failures too | A refused caller is told only that the request was refused; describing the protected resource would tell an unauthenticated caller more, not less. Traceability is preserved without a payload: every `401`/`403` carries `X-Correlation-Id` — the same id as its audit record — plus the full hardened header set, and the telemetry `401` additionally carries `WWW-Authenticate` | Clients must key on the status code, not on a message body, for these two outcomes |
| Shared request-body cap (256 KiB default) answering `413`, and a shared hardened response-header set on every service | No body limit; per-service header configuration | An unauthenticated caller could post an unbounded body (CWE-770); CardDemo request bodies model 3270 screen fields and are at most a few kilobytes. Both shapes are covered: a declared `Content-Length` is refused before the body is read at all, and a chunked body is counted while it streams. A streamed body is normally consumed by a message converter inside the dispatcher, which wraps the abort signal and lets Spring resolve it there — so the shared advice classifies that wrapped form as `413` too, rather than letting it surface as a `400` or `500`. The pre-dispatch refusal resets the response, which discarded the correlation id the observability filter had written, so the id is re-applied afterwards. The header set (CSP `default-src 'none'`, `Referrer-Policy: no-referrer`, `Permissions-Policy`, `nosniff`, `X-Frame-Options: DENY`, `Cache-Control: no-store`) is applied uniformly, and the Tomcat error-page fingerprint is suppressed so no server banner or HTML error page is returned | The cap is configurable (`carddemo.http.max-request-body-bytes`); a future endpoint that legitimately accepts a large upload must raise it deliberately |
| Integration tests bound to failsafe in every module that has them | Rely on the default test includes | billpay-service declared no `maven-failsafe-plugin`, so its `*IT` classes compiled but never executed — the security contract they freeze (an unauthenticated `POST /billpay` must never reach the payment service) was therefore unenforced by the build. All ten modules were audited: every module containing `*IT.java` now binds failsafe to `integration-test`/`verify` | A new module with integration tests must declare the plugin; a missing binding is silent rather than failing |
| Production container hardening (non-root, seccomp, dropped caps, read-only FS, NetworkPolicy, PDB) | Run images with default privileges | Defense-in-depth (MJ-10): every pod runs `runAsNonRoot` with a `RuntimeDefault` seccomp profile, drops all Linux capabilities (the frontend re-adds only `NET_BIND_SERVICE`), uses a read-only root filesystem with `emptyDir` scratch, a default-deny `NetworkPolicy` with least-privilege allows, and `maxUnavailable: 1` PodDisruptionBudgets | Datastores use a lighter profile so the official images initialize correctly; documented per workload |
| Compose health probe uses bash `/dev/tcp`; backend images copy the reactor-built jar, the frontend image builds in-stage | `curl`-based healthcheck; run the Maven reactor build inside every image | The `eclipse-temurin:21-jre` base ships no `curl`/`wget`, so the probe issues a minimal HTTP request over bash `/dev/tcp`. Eight of the nine backend `Dockerfile`s start from `eclipse-temurin:21-jre` and `COPY` the reactor-built `target/<service>.jar` (directly or through a `JAR_FILE` build argument), so `mvn clean install -DskipTests` MUST precede `docker compose build` (documented in README-target.md); running the reactor inside nine images would re-resolve the whole dependency tree nine times and rebuild `carddemo-common` per service. Two images are genuinely multi-stage and need no host artifact: `reporting-service` (`maven:3.9.16-eclipse-temurin-21` build stage → jre runtime) and `frontend` (`node:24` build → `nginx:alpine` runtime) | A stale `target/` jar produces a stale image — the documented build order is mandatory; recorded here because the build sequence is not self-evident from the Compose file alone `[docker-compose.yml; auth-service/Dockerfile; frontend/Dockerfile]` |
| **REVISED.** Actuator health is `show-details: when-authorized`, and the metrics scrape is protected by HTTP authentication AND network isolation together, through ONE shared filter chain in `carddemo-common` rather than nine per-service ones | Rely on network isolation alone and ship no scrape principal; enforce basic auth by adding a chain to each service separately; put the `monitoring` principal in `security_users` alongside the application users | Anonymous health detail leaks internal topology (dependency hosts, versions), so detail still renders only to an authenticated principal. Network isolation alone was rejected as the SOLE control because it fails open the moment a pod is exposed for debugging, and the AAP 0.7.5 metrics endpoint is the one management surface that carries per-endpoint request volumes and error rates for every business operation. The control therefore lives in `carddemo-common/security/ManagementSecurityConfig.java`, which every service inherits by depending on the shared library: an `@Order(1)` chain with `securityMatcher("/actuator/prometheus", "/actuator/metrics/**")`, `anyRequest().hasRole("MONITORING")`, HTTP basic, `STATELESS` sessions, and `csrf`/`formLogin`/`requestCache` disabled — so it cannot collide with the application chains, which match other paths and are unordered relative to it. Placing one shared chain in the library, rather than one per service, is what makes the six services that own no application-level authorization model still enforce the scrape credential without acquiring one. The principal is a single read-only `InMemoryUserDetailsManager` entry seeded from `carddemo.monitoring.password:${MONITORING_PASSWORD}`, deliberately NOT a `security_users` row, so an operator credential can never authenticate a business request and no application seed can authenticate a scrape. `observability/prometheus.yml` presents it as `basic_auth` with `username: monitoring` and `password_file: /etc/prometheus/secrets/monitoring-password` on all nine service jobs, anchored once as `&carddemo_scrape_auth` and aliased eight times so the credential is declared in exactly one place. Network isolation is retained as the second layer: no service publishes a host port, and Prometheus and Grafana bind to `127.0.0.1` only | The scrape fails closed — an unset `MONITORING_PASSWORD` yields no usable credential and every metrics scrape returns 401 rather than silently serving telemetry anonymously; compose enforces this with `${MONITORING_PASSWORD:?…}` at both the service and the secret. The chain builds its own `ProviderManager` with a `DefaultAuthenticationEventPublisher` so a rejected scrape credential still reaches the audit trail, which a manually constructed manager would otherwise discard. An earlier revision of this row asserted the opposite of all of the above — that the scrape was deliberately unauthenticated, that only three of the nine services carried a filter chain, and that the `basic_auth` block was inert and had been removed; every one of those statements was false against the delivered code and is corrected here `[carddemo-common/src/main/java/com/carddemo/common/security/ManagementSecurityConfig.java; observability/prometheus.yml; docker-compose.yml]` |
| Trace sampling 0.1 for services, 1.0 for the `api-gateway` | Uniform 1.0 everywhere | The gateway is the entry point, so sampling every request there starts each trace; downstream services configure 0.1 to bound the overhead of traces that do NOT enter through the gateway. The gateway overrides the shared `0.1` via an explicit Deployment env var (explicit `env` wins over `envFrom`). The two values do not fight each other because the sampler is parent-based: a request arriving with a sampled `traceparent` is recorded in full downstream regardless of the local 0.1 ratio, so a gateway-initiated request yields one complete joined trace | Only requests that bypass the gateway are subject to the 0.1 ratio. Verified at runtime after the tracing stack was made functional: a single `POST /auth/signon` and a single `GET /accounts/{id}` through the gateway produced joined traces of 13 and 17 spans spanning `api-gateway`+`auth-service` and `api-gateway`+`account-service` respectively. An earlier revision of this row described configuration that could not take effect at all, because no tracing auto-configuration or span exporter was on the classpath and no collector existed — see the tracing entries in §9.4 |
| Compose health probe uses bash `/dev/tcp`; eight of the ten images copy a host-built jar and only `reporting-service` and `frontend` build their artifact in-image | A `curl -f` healthcheck; an in-container Maven reactor build for every service | The probe uses `/dev/tcp` because it asserts on the RESPONSE BODY — `grep -q UP` fails a 200 whose aggregate status is `DOWN`, which a bare `curl -f` would accept — and because it depends on nothing a base-image change could drop. It is **not** because the base lacks an HTTP client: `eclipse-temurin:21-jre` is Ubuntu-based and ships `curl` 8.18.0, `wget` 1.25.0 and `bash` (measured inside a running container). On image construction, each service's build context is its own module (`context: ./<svc>`), which cannot see the parent POM or the sibling `carddemo-common`, so those Dockerfiles consume the jar produced by the host reactor build; `batch-service` additionally uses a second stage to explode the Spring Boot layers. `reporting-service` is the exception: its compose entry uses the repository root as context precisely so its Dockerfile CAN run `mvn -pl reporting-service -am package` in-stage | The eight module builds require `mvn clean install` to have run first, which `README-target.md` documents. An earlier revision of this row asserted that the base image ships no `curl`/`wget` and that all images run the reactor build in-stage; both were false and are corrected here `[docker-compose.yml; reporting-service/Dockerfile; batch-service/Dockerfile]` |
| Trace sampling 0.1 for services, 1.0 for the `api-gateway` in Kubernetes; 1.0 for every container in local Docker Compose | Uniform 1.0 everywhere | The gateway is the entry point, so sampling every request there starts each trace; downstream services sample 0.1 to bound overhead. The gateway overrides the shared `0.1` via an explicit Deployment env var (explicit `env` wins over `envFrom`). Local Compose instead sets `MANAGEMENT_TRACING_SAMPLING_PROBABILITY=1.0` for every container, because the Observability rule requires tracing to be *verified* locally and a 0.1 sample makes a single asserted trace non-deterministic | Trace continuity relies on context propagation across the 0.1/1.0 boundary (standard for head-based sampling); the local 1.0 sample is a development-only setting |
| React `Header` shows a render-time date/time snapshot (server-preferred), not a live ticking clock | A `setInterval` browser clock | COBOL `POPULATE-HEADER-INFO` takes a single `FUNCTION CURRENT-DATE` snapshot at map-send time; the component prefers the server-provided `currentDate`/`currentTime` and otherwise takes one client snapshot, never ticking — matching legacy behavior and avoiding a spurious live clock | None; matches the legacy single-snapshot semantics `[app/cbl/COSGN00C.cbl]` |
| SPA calls same-origin `/api`, reverse-proxied by nginx; nginx ships as a full main config | Bake an absolute gateway URL into the bundle; nginx `conf.d` snippet | A static Vite bundle cannot consume runtime env, so an absolute gateway URL is unworkable across environments; the bundle uses the relative `/api` base and nginx proxies it to the gateway on the private network. The nginx file is a complete main config (the frontend `Dockerfile` copies it to `/etc/nginx/nginx.conf`, not `conf.d`), and under the read-only root filesystem nginx writes only to mounted `emptyDir`s (`/var/cache/nginx`, `/var/run`, `/tmp`) | The frontend `Dockerfile` must honor the main-config placement and writable-path contract `[frontend/nginx.conf]` |
| Commit `frontend/package-lock.json` as an authorized deliverable | `.gitignore` the lockfile; regenerate on each build | AAP §0.5.1 mandates the npm toolchain is "lock-pinned at scaffold", and the documented `npm ci` build REQUIRES a committed lockfile for reproducible, deterministic installs; omitting it would break `npm ci` and allow silent transitive-version drift. Reviewed: `lockfileVersion: 3`, 491 locked entries, root `dependencies`/`devDependencies` exactly consistent with `frontend/package.json`. It is therefore recorded in the traceability matrix (Section 11) as a standalone-operation deliverable, resolving its earlier "undeclared file" flag | Lockfile must be regenerated (and re-reviewed) whenever `package.json` dependencies change `[frontend/package.json; frontend/package-lock.json]` |
| Created the three transaction screen DTOs (`TransactionViewResponseDto`, `TransactionListItemDto`, `TransactionAddRequestDto`) in `com.carddemo.common.dto` alongside `TransactionMapper` | Bind the mapper to a separate DTO task's not-yet-authored types, or place DTOs in a `com.carddemo.transaction.dto` package | AAP §0.4.3 lists `dto/*RequestDto.java` / `*ResponseDto.java` as CREATE targets (one request/response pair per screen). No task in this tranche produced the CT00/CT01/CT02 view/list/add DTOs, so `TransactionMapper` (and any consuming service/controller) could not compile without them. They follow the established `ReportRequestDto` / `ReportResponseDto` convention, live in `com.carddemo.common.dto` (a local `com.carddemo.transaction.dto` package is prohibited by the mapper's own contract), carry only field state, and use `BigDecimal` for the amount so `TransactionMapper.scale2` preserves the `NUMERIC(11,2)` scale; `tranId` is intentionally absent from the add request because it is DB-assigned | Additive (no existing file modified); a later DTO task authoring the same screens must keep the `getTran*` / `setTran*` accessor names the mapper binds to `[app/cpy/CVTRA05Y.cpy; app/cbl/COTRN00C.cbl; app/cbl/COTRN01C.cbl; app/cbl/COTRN02C.cbl]` |
| Created the account, card, and user screen DTOs (`AccountViewResponseDto`, `AccountUpdateResponseDto`, `AccountUpdateRequestDto`, `CardDetailResponseDto`, `CardUpdateResponseDto`, `CardListItemDto`, `CardListResponseDto`, `CardUpdateRequestDto`, `UserResponseDto`, `AddUserRequestDto`, `UpdateUserRequestDto`) in `com.carddemo.common.dto` alongside `AccountMapper` / `CardMapper` / `UserMapper` | Bind the mappers to a separate DTO task's not-yet-authored types, or place the DTOs in per-service `com.carddemo.<svc>.dto` packages | AAP §0.4.3 lists `dto/*RequestDto.java` / `*ResponseDto.java` as CREATE targets (one request/response pair per screen). The account view/update (`CAVW`/`CAUP`), card list/detail/update (`CCLI`/`CCDL`/`CCUP`) and user-CRUD (`CU00`–`CU03`) screen DTOs were not otherwise authored, so `AccountMapper`, `CardMapper`, and `UserMapper` could not compile without them. They follow the established `ReportRequestDto` / `Transaction*Dto` convention, live in `com.carddemo.common.dto` (the package the mappers import from), carry only field state with a no-arg constructor for Jackson, use `BigDecimal` for the five monetary account fields so `AccountMapper.scale2` preserves the `NUMERIC(12,2)` scale, and omit the `acctId` / `custId` primary keys from the update-request DTOs because identity is path-derived and never client-assigned (the account `version` was initially omitted on the same reasoning and was subsequently ADDED as an explicit concurrency token — see §2, "The account update carries the caller's `version` snapshot"); the preserved misspelling `ACCT-EXPIRAION-DATE` / `CARD-EXPIRAION-DATE` is carried verbatim | Additive (no existing file modified); a later DTO task authoring the same screens must keep the `get*` / `set*` accessor names the mappers bind to `[app/cbl/COACTVWC.cbl; app/cbl/COACTUPC.cbl; app/cbl/COCRDLIC.cbl; app/cbl/COCRDSLC.cbl; app/cbl/COCRDUPC.cbl; app/cbl/COUSR00C.cbl; app/cpy/CVACT01Y.cpy; app/cpy/CVACT02Y.cpy; app/cpy/CSUSR01Y.cpy]` |
| Depend on `org.springframework.boot:spring-boot-micrometer-tracing` **and** `spring-boot-micrometer-tracing-opentelemetry` in all nine services | Rely on `io.micrometer:micrometer-tracing-bridge-otel` alone (the previous state); switch to the Brave bridge | Spring Boot 4 moved each integration's auto-configuration into its own module. With only the BRIDGE on the classpath there is no `Tracer`, no `Slf4JEventListener` and therefore no `traceId` / `spanId` in the MDC — every structured log line rendered them EMPTY even though the JSON encoder was flattening MDC correctly. Adding the two Boot modules activates the OpenTelemetry `Tracer`, W3C `traceparent` propagation and MDC enrichment, which is what the Observability rule's "distributed tracing across service boundaries" requires. Diagnosed empirically before any change: a request-scoped line already carried `correlationId` but neither trace field, isolating the fault to the missing auto-configuration rather than to logging | Three further dependency trees per service (micrometer-tracing 1.7.0, bridge-otel 1.7.0, opentelemetry-sdk / api / context 1.62.0), verified embedded in all nine fat jars |
| NO `opentelemetry-exporter-otlp` on any classpath — spans are created, propagated and LOGGED, but not shipped | Add the OTLP exporter and point it at a collector; add a Zipkin exporter | The delivered stack contains no trace collector, so an exporter would fail every export attempt and flood the logs with connection errors — noise that actively DEGRADES the observability the rule demands. Trace context still crosses service boundaries as a W3C `traceparent`, and each span is correlated through the `traceId` / `spanId` in the structured logs plus the `correlationId` echoed on every response, so cross-service correlation is achievable without a collector | Traces cannot be browsed in a UI until a collector and the matching exporter are introduced; that is an additive, configuration-only change |
| ONE shared correlation filter — `carddemo-common` `CorrelationIdFilter`, registered by `WebObservabilityConfig`, imported by all nine services | Keep the per-service `@Component CorrelationIdFilter` copies (five existed) and write the four missing ones | The five copies had drifted — only the shared implementation adopts an inbound W3C `traceparent` trace-id and sanitizes CRLF out of the header value (log-injection defence) — and four services had NO filter at all, so `correlationId` was missing from exactly the services whose logs matter most during an incident. A single implementation in the shared library makes behavior identical fleet-wide and gives the filter one comprehensive test suite instead of two partial ones. It is registered as a `FilterRegistrationBean` over a non-bean instance, so there is no double registration | Standalone-MockMvc tests must compose the filter explicitly, since there is no bean to autowire (done in `AccountViewUpdateIT`) |
| Every logback appender DEFINITION lives inside the `<springProfile>` block that references it | Declare the appenders once at the top level and reference them from the profile blocks (the previous shape) | Logback instantiates every appender it PARSES, so a top-level `TEXT_CONSOLE` was constructed and then left unreferenced under the `docker` / `k8s` / `prod` profiles, emitting `Appender named [TEXT_CONSOLE] not referenced` on every startup (63 occurrences across the fleet). A warning on a CLEAN start trains operators to ignore startup warnings, which is the opposite of what the Observability rule intends. Scoping each definition means only the active profile's appender is ever built | A definition shared by several profiles must be repeated in each; the files are small and the duplication is explicit |
| `management.endpoint.health.group.readiness.include: readinessState,db` in all eight database-backed services, with `spring.datasource.hikari.connection-timeout: 3000` and `validation-timeout: 1500` | Leave readiness as `readinessState` only; write a custom `HealthIndicator`; leave Hikari at its 30 s default | A readiness probe that reports `UP` while the database is unreachable is worse than no probe: the orchestrator keeps routing traffic to an instance that can only answer `500`. Including the `db` contributor makes readiness reflect the dependency the service cannot work without, while LIVENESS deliberately excludes it, because a database outage must not restart every pod. Hikari's 30 s default connection timeout made the aggregate health endpoint take 30.08 s — far beyond any sane probe timeout — so the probe would have TIMED OUT rather than reported `DOWN`; a 3 s connection timeout bounds the response inside the 5 s probe timeout (measured: `503 {"status":"DOWN"}` in 3.02 s with PostgreSQL stopped) | A brief database blip now removes instances from the load-balancer rotation; that is the intended behavior, and the liveness / readiness split prevents restart storms |
| `user-service` permits ANONYMOUS access to `/actuator/health`, `/actuator/health/**`, `/actuator/info` and `/actuator/prometheus`, matching the other eight services | Keep `.anyRequest().hasRole("ADMIN")` across the whole actuator tree (the previous state); enable `httpBasic` on the management chain for the probe and the scrape | `user-service` was the only service gating the ENTIRE actuator tree behind `ROLE_ADMIN` with `httpBasic` disabled, so the container healthcheck and the Prometheus scrape — neither of which carries a session principal — received `403` forever: the container was permanently `unhealthy` (failing streak 315) and the service was unscrapable, i.e. invisible to the very dashboard the Observability rule mandates. Exposing only the OPERATIONAL endpoints keeps the sensitive ones (`/actuator/metrics`, `/actuator/env`, …) behind `ROLE_ADMIN`, and health DETAIL still renders only `when-authorized` | `/actuator/prometheus` is anonymously readable inside the private network; it is not published on a host port and carries no PII |
| The three Redis dashboard panels query `lettuce_seconds_count` / `_sum` / `_max` | Keep the original `lettuce_command_completion_seconds_*` and `lettuce_command_firstresponse_seconds_max` expressions | Those metric names do not exist in the running fleet, so the panels were permanently empty — indistinguishable, to an operator, from Redis being idle or the scrape being broken. The services actually export the Micrometer-observed Lettuce timer as `lettuce_seconds_*` (44 series, labelled `db_system="redis"`), which is present precisely because the tracing / observation modules are now on the classpath. Each corrected expression was verified against the Prometheus API to return 8 series before the dashboard was re-provisioned, and panel 26 was renamed "Redis Command Latency (max)" to match what it actually plots | Panel expressions are coupled to Lettuce / Micrometer metric naming, so the dashboard is verified against the live stack rather than assumed correct |
| `.gitignore` additionally ignores local datastore directories (`pgdata/`, `postgres-data/`, `pgdata-*/`, `docker-volumes/`), database dumps (`*.dump`, `*.sql.gz`, and root-anchored `dump*.sql` / `db_dump*.sql` / `carddemo_backup*.sql`) and generated test / evidence output (`test-output/`, `test-results/`, `qa-evidence/`, `surefire-reports/`, `failsafe-reports/`) | Rely on the existing `target/` and `.env` rules; ignore `*.sql` wholesale | Running the stack with a BIND-mounted PostgreSQL data directory, or taking a `pg_dump` while debugging, drops thousands of binary files — and a complete copy of the seeded PII — into the working tree, which is exactly the "no file listed in `.gitignore` may be committed" hazard the rule guards against and a credential / PII leak if committed. The dump patterns are deliberately ROOT-ANCHORED and extension-specific so that `db/migration/*.sql` — the Flyway schema and seed scripts, which MUST stay tracked — can never be matched; a wholesale `*.sql` rule would have silently un-tracked the entire migration set | A dump intentionally kept as a fixture must be added with `git add -f` or named outside the anchored patterns |

## 7. QA-Remediation Deviations (runtime checkpoint)

Deviations and mechanism choices taken while remediating the runtime QA checkpoint. Every row below is a decision a competent engineer could reasonably have made differently, so each is recorded here per the Explainability rule rather than in a code comment. The finding id in the Decision column identifies the runtime defect that forced the decision.

### 7.1 Authentication, Session & Authorization

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| Seed the credential as a `{bcrypt}`-**prefixed** hash (`{bcrypt}$2a$12$…`), and generate it at the encoder's own `BCRYPT_STRENGTH` (F1) | Seed the bare 60-character `$2a$…` hash; or replace `DelegatingPasswordEncoder` with a bare `BCryptPasswordEncoder` | `PasswordEncoderFactory` builds a `DelegatingPasswordEncoder`, which resolves the algorithm from a leading `{id}` prefix and rejects an unprefixed hash outright — every seeded user returned 403 with no diagnostic. Keeping the delegating encoder preserves the ability to rotate algorithms without a data migration, so the seed carries the prefix instead. The strength digits in the seeded hash are asserted by `SecurityUserRepositoryIT` so a strength change cannot silently invalidate the fixture | Any future change of encoder id or strength requires re-seeding; guarded by test |
| Establish a real Spring Security `Authentication` from the session context on every request via `SessionContextAuthenticationFilter` (F2) | Have the gateway authorize on the raw session attribute instead of `hasRole(...)`; or return a JWT and authorize on claims | The gateway authorizes with `hasRole('ADMIN'/'USER')`, so an authenticated caller must be represented as an `Authentication` with those authorities; sign-on previously wrote only the `SessionContext`, so every routed request was anonymous (401). A servlet filter that rehydrates `UsernamePasswordAuthenticationToken` from the shared session keeps the COMMAREA-equivalent session as the single source of truth for both the screens and authorization. The filter must treat an existing ANONYMOUS token as "absent" (`AuthenticationTrustResolverImpl.isAnonymous`), otherwise Spring Security's anonymous filter wins and the role gate stays closed | The session attribute becomes security-relevant: it is written only by sign-on and is serialized through the allowlisted JSON serializer |
| One session attribute name for the whole estate: `SessionAttributes.SESSION_CONTEXT` = `carddemoSessionContext` (F27) | Keep the two names in place and have the gateway read either; rename the auth-service attribute to the gateway's `sessionContext` | Two different attribute names ("carddemoSessionContext" written by auth-service, "sessionContext" read by the gateway's `MenuController`) silently disabled the menu role gate and made the `'No access - Admin Only option... '` path unreachable dead code. A single shared constant makes the contract explicit and greppable; the auth-service name was kept because it is the one already persisted in Redis | Every new reader/writer must use the constant, not a literal; `AuthenticationService.SESSION_CONTEXT_ATTRIBUTE` re-exports it for callers |
| Declare the session namespace under the Boot 4 property `spring.session.data.redis.namespace` (keys `carddemo:session:*`) (F9) | Leave the pre-4.x `spring.session.redis.*` block in place; or accept the framework default `spring:session:*` | Spring Boot 4 renamed the Spring Session Redis properties; the old block was inert, so the documented namespace never took effect and every service silently used the default prefix. Declaring the property under its current name makes the intent real and keeps all services on ONE namespace — a mismatch would split the shared COMMAREA-equivalent session per service | Every service must declare the identical namespace; asserted by inspecting the live Redis key prefix |
| user-service authorizes with `anyRequest().hasRole('ADMIN')`, `SessionCreationPolicy.NEVER` and CSRF disabled (F13) | Keep CSRF enabled and require the SPA to fetch and echo a token; or create a session per service | Administrator writes (`POST`/`PUT`/`DELETE /users`) were unusable: the service created its own session instead of reusing the shared one and rejected every state-changing request as a CSRF violation, blocking twelve QA cases. The API is a stateless JSON API whose credential is a server-side session cookie issued by the gateway, never a browser form post, so token-based CSRF adds no protection the SameSite session cookie does not already give; `NEVER` guarantees the service reads the shared session rather than minting a second one | CSRF protection is off for this service: it must stay JSON-only (no form-encoded state change) and the session cookie must remain `SameSite`; recorded so the trade-off is auditable |
| Authorization is enforced at the gateway; the five business services (account, card, transaction, billpay, reporting) carry NO Spring Security at all (F10) | Add a Spring Security filter chain to every service (defense in depth); or mTLS between services | The AAP makes the gateway the routing and role-gating boundary, and adding a chain to five more services would duplicate the role model in six places. The compensating controls are structural: no business service publishes a host port (only the gateway does), the Compose network is private, and the Kubernetes manifests carry a default-deny `NetworkPolicy` with least-privilege allows, so a business service is unreachable except through the gateway | A workload placed on the same private network could call a business service unauthenticated; documented explicitly rather than left implicit. Consequence for code: a shared `@RestControllerAdvice` must not reference Spring Security types unless guarded by `@ConditionalOnClass` |

### 7.2 Data Integrity, Financial & Validation Fidelity

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| `Transaction implements Persistable<String>` so a client-visible id is always INSERTed, plus `saveAndFlush` and a `PersistenceExceptionHandler` mapping the duplicate key to `'Tran ID already exist...'` (F38) | Pre-check with `existsById` before saving; or let JPA decide between insert and update | With an assigned string id, `JpaRepository.save()` issues a SELECT-then-UPDATE, so a colliding generated id SILENTLY OVERWROTE an existing financial record instead of being rejected — the legacy `COTRN02C` / `COBIL00C` `WRITE` returns DUPKEY and surfaces `'Tran ID already exist...'`. `Persistable.isNew()` forces a real INSERT so the database constraint is the arbiter; an `existsById` pre-check would remain race-prone under concurrency, exactly the flaw the sequence-based id already removes | The entity must report `isNew()` correctly for genuine re-saves; covered by `TransactionInsertContractTest` |
| Optimistic-lock conflicts are detected inside the service transaction (`flush` inside the try) and mapped to HTTP 409 with `'Record changed by some one else. Please review'` (F37) | Let the exception surface from the commit and be handled generically (500) | A flush at commit time throws outside the service's try/catch, so a genuine concurrent update returned an opaque 500 instead of the legacy conflict message. Flushing inside the guarded block keeps the observable outcome identical to `COACTUPC`'s `DATA-WAS-CHANGED-BEFORE-UPDATE` branch | An explicit flush costs one extra round trip on the write path |
| `PUT /accounts/{id}` accepts the display-time snapshot (`oldAcct*` / `oldCust*`) and compares field-by-field before writing (F20) | Rely on the JPA `@Version` column alone; or require the client to echo the version | `COACTUPC` stores `ACUP-OLD-*` at display time and compares the current record against that snapshot before `REWRITE`; with no snapshot on the wire the update was a classic LOST UPDATE — a second writer's change was silently discarded between read and write. Carrying `ACUP-OLD-*` preserves the legacy contract exactly and keeps `@Version` as the second line of defence. When the snapshot is omitted the comparison is skipped, so existing single-writer callers keep working | A partially supplied snapshot legitimately produces 409 (any mismatched field aborts) — intended, and asserted by test |
| Out-of-scale money is REJECTED, never silently rounded (F21) | Round the incoming value to the column scale | A request carrying `300.005` for a `NUMERIC(12,2)` field was silently rounded, changing a financial value the caller supplied. The 3270 map could not represent a third decimal at all, so rejecting is the faithful behavior; rounding would violate "do not alter COBOL data precision" | Callers that previously relied on lenient rounding now receive a 400 with the legacy amount-format literal |
| The seeded fixture is deliberately NOT screen-valid under the legacy `COACTUPC` edits | Rewrite the seed so every row passes every screen edit | The ASCII fixtures are the authoritative legacy data (AAP §0.2.1) and contain values the online screen would reject — FICO 274 (below the 300 floor), zip `12546` against state `NC`, phone area code `373` (absent from `CSLKPCDY`). Editing the fixture to please the screen would falsify the migration's reference data; the validator is exercised against constructed input instead | An update of an untouched seeded account can fail validation on a field the caller did not change — faithful to the legacy screen, which re-edits every field on every submit |
| `AccountUpdateValidator` implements the full `1200-EDIT-MAP-INPUTS` sequence including the `1280` state/zip cross-field edit and the `CSLKPCDY` lookup sets (F18) | Validate with Bean Validation annotations only | The COBOL program edits 25 fields in a fixed order, emits exactly one message (the first failure), and finishes with a cross-field state/zip check against a 240-entry combination table plus a 410-entry phone-area-code table — none of which annotation constraints can express in the required order. A hand-written validator reproduces the PERFORM order literally, which is what makes the observable message sequence identical | The validator must be kept in step with `CSLKPCDY` if the lookup tables ever change; 64 tests pin the order and the literals |

### 7.3 Screen Vocabulary, Paging & Message Reachability

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| Carry the `COUSR00C` paging literals to the client on `UserListResponseDto`, and the `COCRDLIC` literals on `CardListResponseDto` (F15, F24) | Keep them as server-side log messages only | The literals `'You are already at the top of the page...'`, `'You are already at the bottom of the page...'`, `'NO MORE PAGES TO DISPLAY'`, `'NO MORE RECORDS TO SHOW'`, `'NO RECORDS FOUND FOR THIS SEARCH CONDITION.'` and `'INVALID ACTION CODE'` are the observable output of the legacy screens; a REST client cannot reproduce the screen without them. They travel in a dedicated response field so no HTTP status is overloaded | Adds one optional field per list response |
| `selection` is an explicit request parameter, restoring `resolveSelection` / `getUserForUpdate` / `getUserForDelete` (F16) | Delete the unreachable methods | `COUSR00C` acts on a row selection of `U` or `D` and emits `'Invalid selection. Valid values are U and D'`; with no way to express the selection over HTTP the whole branch — and its literal — was unreachable dead code. A request parameter is the smallest faithful mapping and keeps the legacy vocabulary alive | One more optional parameter on the list route |
| `aid`, `action` and `selectedCardNumber` are explicit request parameters on the card list route (F24) | Infer the PF-key intent from the page number alone | HTTP has no 3270 attention identifier, yet `1400-SETUP-MESSAGE` branches on PF7/PF8 and on whether a row was selected. Carrying the AID and the row-selection flag as named parameters is the only way to keep those branches — and their literals — reachable without inventing behavior | Clients must send the AID to obtain the boundary messages; absent parameters simply yield the plain page |
| A negative `page` maps to the PF7 top-of-page literal, not to a generic validation error (F14) | Return a generic "page must be >= 0" message | `COUSR00C` has no notion of a negative page; the nearest legacy outcome for "paging before the first page" is `'You are already at the top of the page...'`, which is what the screen shows. Reusing that literal keeps the observable output legacy-exact and avoids inventing an unrequested message (previously the request produced a 500) | A caller that sends a negative page sees a boundary message rather than a field error |
| `'NO MORE RECORDS TO SHOW'` is emitted whenever the page fill reaches end-of-file, not only for an empty page | Emit it only when the returned page is empty | The COBOL browse sets the message on the ENDFILE branch while the page can still hold rows, so a partially filled last page carries it too; matching only the empty-page case would drop the message a real screen shows | Slightly broader emission than a naive reading of the screen suggests; grounded in the ENDFILE branch |
| `DELETE /users/{id}` keeps HTTP 204, so the legacy delete confirmation stays a log-only message | Return 200 with the confirmation literal in a body | 204 is the established contract for this route and QA verified it; changing the status to carry a message would break existing callers for a cosmetic gain. The literal is still emitted, to the structured log | The delete confirmation is not visible to an API client; recorded so the omission is intentional and auditable |

### 7.4 Error-Response Contract

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| `GlobalExceptionHandler extends ResponseEntityExceptionHandler` and overrides `createResponseEntity(...)`, so every framework failure is rendered as the eight-field CardDemo envelope (F5, F17) | Add one `@ExceptionHandler` per framework exception type; or accept Spring's `ProblemDetail` for framework errors | 405/415/400/403 responses arrived in three different shapes (empty body, Tomcat HTML, Spring's default JSON), so no client could parse errors uniformly. Converting `ProblemDetail` at the single point where the framework builds its response covers every current AND future framework exception, instead of chasing types one at a time | The override must keep the envelope's field set stable; `ErrorEnvelopeTest` asserts the exact eight keys |
| A `CardDemoErrorController` plus a Tomcat `SilentErrorReportValve` render the envelope for container-level rejections (F17) | Relax Tomcat's rejection of encoded path separators; or accept Tomcat's HTML error page | A path such as `/users/..%2F..%2Fetc%2Fpasswd` is rejected by Tomcat BEFORE any context filter runs, and the rejection is rendered by the **host** pipeline — a context valve never sees it, and `setShowReport(false)` still emits Tomcat's minimal HTML. Overriding `report(...)` to hand-write the JSON envelope keeps one machine-readable contract without weakening the encoded-separator rejection, which is a real path-traversal defence | Ties one class to Tomcat's valve API; guarded by `@ConditionalOnClass(ErrorReportValve)` so a non-Tomcat runtime is unaffected |
| `AccessDeniedException` / `AuthenticationException` handling lives in a separate `SecurityExceptionHandler` guarded by `@ConditionalOnClass` (F5) | Keep the handlers in the shared `GlobalExceptionHandler` | Five services intentionally carry no Spring Security (§7.1), so a shared advice that merely REFERENCES `AccessDeniedException` fails their bean introspection with `NoClassDefFoundError` at startup. Splitting the security-aware handlers into a conditionally loaded class keeps the shared advice classpath-agnostic | Two advice classes to keep consistent; only the security-bearing services import the second |
| Exactly ONE field error is returned, chosen by declared field order with presence constraints ahead of format constraints (F35) | Return every violation, as Bean Validation does by default | `COSGN00C` L117-131 is an `EVALUATE TRUE` guarded by `IF WS-ERROR-MSG-OFF`: the screen shows one message, the highest-precedence one. Returning both violations plus an invented `"Validation failed"` top-level string is unrequested observable output. Declaration order reproduces the COBOL edit order, and presence-before-format reproduces the `'Please enter User ID ...'`-before-format behavior | DTO field order becomes semantically meaningful; asserted by test |
| Constraint messages carry the legacy literal when one exists for that violation, and the record-layout width otherwise (F25) | Leave Bean Validation's default strings (`"size must be between 0 and 11"`) | Framework strings are unrequested observable output; the spec-literal rule forbids them. Where the legacy program has a literal for the condition (empty, non-numeric, bad format) it is used byte-exact; where the 3270 field width made over-length input *unrepresentable*, no legacy literal exists, so the message states the record-layout width instead of inventing a COBOL-looking sentence. The same rule authorizes reusing a literal from another program when it is byte-exact and semantically exact for the field (for example the account-id message shared by `COACTVWC` / `COACTUPC` / `COCRDLIC`) | Cross-program literal reuse must stay semantically exact; each reuse was grep-verified against `app/cbl` |
| Framework protocol text is retained in `message` for protocol-level failures (malformed body, unsupported media type) | Replace it with a COBOL literal | No COBOL program has an analogue for "the HTTP body could not be parsed" — the 3270 datastream had no such failure mode. Substituting a business literal would misreport the cause; these messages are HTTP-level, not business output | A client sees framework wording for protocol errors; deliberately scoped to failures with no legacy analogue |

### 7.5 Batch & Reporting Execution

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| The statement destination is configuration, and it is the SHARED batch root rather than a reporting-only key: `carddemo.batch.output-dir` (from `CARDDEMO_BATCH_OUTPUT_DIR`, blank selecting `<java.io.tmpdir>/carddemo-batch/output`, `/var/carddemo/batch/output` in Compose), resolved through carddemo-common's `BatchOutputPathResolver` (F22) | Mount a named volume at `/app/output`; relax the read-only root filesystem; or keep a reporting-only `carddemo.reporting.output-dir` key alongside the batch root | The container runs with `read_only: true`, so the hard-coded `/app/output` path could never be created: every report was reported as submitted while the job silently FAILED with `Unable to create file`. A named volume mounted at `/app/output` would be created root-owned while the process runs as uid 1000, so making the destination configuration keeps the read-only root intact. A SECOND, reporting-only property was rejected because reporting-service and batch-service both write statement and dump files through the same hardened resolver: one root means one containment boundary to reason about (absolute paths, `..` traversal and symlink escapes are all rejected, and the root is proven writable while the context refreshes) instead of two, and a vestigial second key would name a destination no writer reads | The destination is a mounted directory in Compose and Kubernetes; a deployment that must retain statements points `CARDDEMO_BATCH_OUTPUT_DIR` at a persistent volume. reporting-service also declares `carddemo.batch.input-dir` for symmetry although it reads no feed file `[carddemo-common/src/main/java/com/carddemo/common/batch/BatchPathConfig.java; carddemo-common/src/main/java/com/carddemo/common/batch/BatchOutputPathResolver.java]` |
| A report request verifies its launch synchronously within a bounded window (`carddemo.reporting.launch-verification-timeout`, default 250 ms) and otherwise returns `'Unable to Write TDQ (JOBS)...'` (F22) | Wait for the job to finish before responding; or keep fire-and-forget and never report failure | `CORPT00C` writes the JCL to the TDQ and returns immediately, so blocking until completion would change the observable behavior and blow the 200 ms p95 budget. A short bounded wait catches the deterministic failures (unwritable destination, missing parameters) and still returns promptly; a genuinely long run keeps fire-and-forget semantics and is audited by its `BATCH_JOB_EXECUTION` row plus an ERROR log line | A failure that manifests after the window is reported as a successful submission (as on the mainframe) and must be found in the batch audit tables |
| Batch persistence is declared ONCE, by the shared `carddemo-common` `JdbcBatchConfiguration extends JdbcDefaultBatchConfiguration`, which the THREE job-hosting services (batch-service, transaction-service, reporting-service) each `@Import`; it returns a JDBC-backed `jobRepository` over the application `DataSource` and, guarded by a `batch_job_instance` sentinel, applies Spring Batch's own `schema-postgresql.sql` when it reaches a database where the tables are still absent (F23) | Set `spring.batch.jdbc.*` properties; annotate a configuration `@EnableBatchProcessing` + `@EnableJdbcJobRepository`; or accept the framework default | Spring Boot 4 / Spring Batch 6 REMOVED the `spring.batch.jdbc.*` property group and default `DefaultBatchConfiguration` to an in-memory `ResourcelessJobRepository`, so every execution vanished and the `BATCH_*` tables stayed empty — the property-based configuration was inert. Subclassing `DefaultBatchConfiguration` is the DOCUMENTED extension point: Boot's `BatchAutoConfiguration` is annotated `@ConditionalOnMissingBean(value = DefaultBatchConfiguration.class, annotation = EnableBatchProcessing.class)` and backs off in favour of the subclass, while the inherited `jobOperator` keeps `BatchJobLauncherAutoConfiguration` active so `JobLauncher` stays injectable. The subclass also gives the sentinel-guarded provisioner somewhere to live, which the bare annotation does not. Flyway still OWNS the schema in the normal path (`V5__batch_metadata.sql` in the shared carddemo-common set, so no service has to start first) | Framework-default `BATCH_` table prefix and create-isolation are accepted. Exactly one `DefaultBatchConfiguration` subclass may reach any one service's context — a second one registers a competing `jobRepository` definition and the context refuses to refresh — so the single shared class is `@Import`ed rather than copied per service. The provisioner is a fallback for an un-migrated database, not a second owner of the DDL `[carddemo-common/src/main/resources/db/migration/V5__batch_metadata.sql; carddemo-common/src/main/java/com/carddemo/common/batch/JdbcBatchConfiguration.java]` |
| The reporting-service test profile provisions the framework's `schema-postgresql.sql` through `spring.sql.init` | Add a Flyway migration for the `BATCH_*` tables to reporting-service | reporting-service declares no Flyway migrations of its OWN (it inherits the one consolidated set from `carddemo-common`, like every other service), so its Testcontainers runs need the batch schema from the framework's own script rather than a duplicate migration that would then exist in two owners | Test-only configuration; the deployed schema still comes from the one shared migration set in `carddemo-common`, applied first-writer-wins |
| batch-service keeps `spring.batch.job.enabled: false` (jobs launch on demand) | Let Boot run a job at startup | The legacy jobs are operator/scheduler-submitted; running one at container start would post financial data on every restart. Consequence: the `spring_batch_*` dashboard panels hold data only after a job has been launched — verified by launching a real job in the local environment. The dead `spring.batch.jdbc.initialize-schema` key was removed at the same time since Boot 4 no longer defines it | An operator must launch jobs explicitly; the dashboard's batch row is empty until then |

### 7.6 Observability Wiring

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| auth-service, user-service and reporting-service activate the SHARED `WebObservabilityConfig` (which registers `CorrelationIdFilter`) rather than declaring a local filter (F12) | Copy the `@Component` filter into the three services, as the other five do | Those three services emitted no correlation id at all and every `ErrorResponse.traceId` was null. Importing the shared registration removes the duplication instead of adding to it; the five pre-existing local copies were left untouched because replacing a working filter in five services adds risk without changing behavior | Two registration styles coexist in the estate; the import is asserted per service by test |
| Depend on `spring-boot-starter-opentelemetry` instead of the bare `micrometer-tracing-bridge-otel` (F28) | Add the individual autoconfiguration and exporter artifacts by hand | The bridge alone leaves NO `Tracer` in the context: the Boot tracing autoconfiguration, the OpenTelemetry SDK wiring and the OTLP exporter live in three further artifacts. The starter aggregates exactly that set, so tracing becomes functional without hand-maintaining a version-sensitive artifact list. The starter also brings `micrometer-registry-otlp`, so OTLP **metrics** export is switched off (`management.otlp.metrics.export.enabled: false`) — Prometheus scrape is the metrics mechanism AAP §0.7.5 prescribes | One more transitive surface; metrics remain scrape-only by explicit configuration |
| The OTLP span-exporter endpoint is declared under the Boot 4 key `management.opentelemetry.tracing.export.otlp.endpoint` in every service's default document, resolved from `MANAGEMENT_OPENTELEMETRY_TRACING_EXPORT_OTLP_ENDPOINT`, then from the pre-4.x `MANAGEMENT_OTLP_TRACING_ENDPOINT`, and finally from a `http://localhost:4318/v1/traces` literal; Compose supplies the `jaeger` collector through that first variable (F28) | Declare the endpoint only inside the `docker` profile; or keep the pre-4.x `management.otlp.tracing.endpoint` key | Spring Boot 4 keys the exporter on the new property name, so the old key never activated it — spans were created but never left the process. A profile-scoped declaration would leave a plain `java -jar` run with no exporter configuration at all and would silently drop the pre-4.x variable an existing deployment may still set; the two-level placeholder accepts either variable name and falls back to the conventional local collector address, so the SAME configuration works composed, in Kubernetes and standalone. Sampling stays environment-driven (`MANAGEMENT_TRACING_SAMPLING_PROBABILITY`, default `0.1`) | An environment with no reachable collector logs an OTLP export failure on the export interval; that is accepted in exchange for one uniform declaration, and the sampling default keeps the volume low |
| The `api-gateway` repeats the two tracing environment variables inside its own Compose `environment:` block (F28) | Move the gateway's route variables into the shared anchor | A service-level `environment:` mapping REPLACES the one merged from the `x-backend-service` anchor, so the gateway alone kept targeting its own loopback and its span for the entry hop was missing from every trace. The gateway must override `environment` for its route URIs, so the shared variables are repeated there | Two places to update when a shared variable changes; called out in a Compose comment |
| Lettuce command-latency metrics are enabled by installing `MicrometerCommandLatencyRecorder` via a `ClientResourcesBuilderCustomizer` (`RedisCommandMetricsConfig`) (F33) | Re-point the dashboard at the observation-based `lettuce_seconds_*` series; or delete the Redis panels | Lettuce keeps command-latency recording OFF unless a recorder is supplied, so the dashboard's `lettuce_command_completion_seconds_*` / `lettuce_command_firstresponse_seconds_max` series were never emitted. AAP §0.7.5 requires the dashboard template to be verified against the local environment, which means making the authored series real rather than rewriting the dashboard around what happened to be emitted | Adds one timer pair per (command, endpoint) tuple; the library defaults keep the tag set to the command name and the remote address |
| An ERROR-dispatch log line carries `traceId`/`spanId` but no `correlationId` | Register the correlation filter for `DispatcherType.ERROR` as well | `OncePerRequestFilter` skips error dispatches by default and the correlation MDC value is cleared when the original dispatch ends, so the `CardDemoErrorController` warning logged during an error dispatch has no correlation id. The trace identifiers still correlate that line, and closing the gap uniformly would mean changing the five duplicated local filters too; recorded here instead of changed late in the remediation | An error-dispatch log line must be correlated by `traceId` rather than by `correlationId` |
## 8. QA-Surfaced Batch, Runtime & Integration Deviations

Decisions taken while resolving the runtime findings raised by end-to-end QA of the integrated backend. Each is a choice a competent engineer could reasonably have made differently, so each is recorded here per the Explainability rule (AAP §0.7.2) rather than in code comments.

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| The `BATCH_*` metadata schema is provisioned idempotently, by Flyway `V5__batch_metadata.sql` and a guarded application-side provisioner | Rely on `spring.batch.initialize-schema`; provision only by migration; provision only in code | The committed migration is column-for-column identical to `org/springframework/batch/core/schema-postgresql.sql` (6 tables, 3 sequences). Both paths use `CREATE ... IF NOT EXISTS` and the provisioner probes a sentinel table first, so a fresh database, a Flyway-managed database and a test container all converge on one schema without a "relation already exists" failure | Making the migration idempotent changed its checksum, which required one clean database rebuild |
| `run.id` is recorded as a NON-identifying job parameter for STATE-CHANGING runs only (see §11 for the repeatable read/print runs, where it is identifying) | Keep `run.id` identifying (a fresh UUID per launch) | An identifying `run.id` made every submission a brand-new `JobInstance`, so restart was impossible and a duplicate submission could never be detected. With it non-identifying, the business parameters alone establish job identity: an identical resubmission is refused with `JobInstanceAlreadyCompleteException`, and a failed instance is restartable. Idempotency is therefore a property of the business parameters, as the legacy operator-scheduled job streams assumed | Callers must vary a business parameter to run the same job stream twice for the same date |
| Spring Batch metadata persisted in a JDBC `JobRepository` — ONE shared `carddemo-common` `JdbcBatchConfiguration extends JdbcDefaultBatchConfiguration`, `@Import`ed by batch-, transaction- and reporting-service | Accept Spring Batch 6's default `ResourcelessJobRepository`; set `spring.batch.jdbc.*` | Batch 6's `DefaultBatchConfiguration.jobRepository()` returns a `ResourcelessJobRepository`, and Boot's `BatchAutoConfiguration` extends it, so the `BATCH_*` tables stayed empty: no run history, no restart, and no duplicate detection. `BatchAutoConfiguration` carries `@ConditionalOnMissingBean(DefaultBatchConfiguration.class)`, so subclassing it is the documented back-off point, and the inherited `jobOperator` keeps `BatchJobLauncherAutoConfiguration` alive. `spring.batch.jdbc.*` no longer exists in Boot 4.1 (`BatchProperties` exposes only the `job` group) and was inert wherever it was set | Job metadata now consumes database storage and must be included in retention planning |
| Every launcher supplies the job parameters its job requires, backed by configured defaults | Leave the parameter-less launchers passing empty `JobParameters` | Six launchers passed empty parameters to jobs whose beans late-bind `jobParameters['outputFile']` / `['inputFile']` / `['reportFile']`, so every run failed with "Batch file path must not be blank" while the launcher returned `STARTING` and the caller believed it had succeeded. Each launcher now resolves an effective file name (explicit argument, else a `carddemo.batch.*-file` default), validates it through the shared path resolver, and surfaces an acceptance failure synchronously | A default output name is shared across runs, so an unnamed relaunch overwrites the previous artefact |
| The daily-transaction feed is read with a streaming `JdbcCursorItemReader` over `daily_transactions`; `DailyTransaction` remains a non-entity value object | Promote `DailyTransaction` to a JPA `@Entity`; keep the hand-written in-memory repository | `TransactionPostingJob` read a hand-written `ConcurrentHashMap` that nothing populated, so every posting run reported `read=0 write=0` and finished COMPLETED while the persisted 300-row feed sat unread — a silent false success over financial data. `DailyTransaction` is deliberately a non-entity shared by services running `ddl-auto: validate`, so promoting it had an unacceptable blast radius; a cursor reader over an explicit 13-column projection ordered by `dalytran_id` preserves the `CBTRN02C` sequential read without touching the shared type | The reader is bound to the feed table's column names rather than to a mapped entity |
| A posting run that reads zero feed records FAILS with exit code `FAILED_EMPTY_FEED` and "Return code 12" | Let an empty feed complete successfully (`read=0`, RC 0) | An empty `DALYTRAN` feed is an operational fault, not a clean run: reporting success over it is exactly how the in-memory-repository defect stayed invisible. Failing loudly preserves the legacy 0/4/12 result-code contract, where 12 signals that the run must be investigated | A deliberately empty feed day now requires an operator acknowledgement |
| Online transaction ids are drawn from `transaction_id_seq` and probed with `existsById` before `saveAndFlush` | Reproduce the legacy browse-last-then-increment; trust the sequence alone; keep `save` | The sequence counts from one while the posting feed writes its own sixteen-digit ids, so a raw draw can name a row that already holds posted financial data — and JPA `save` MERGES an entity whose id exists, silently overwriting it and leaving the duplicate-id catch dead. The service now advances past any id already present (bounded at 1000 probes) and uses `saveAndFlush` so a concurrent claim surfaces inside the catch as the frozen `Tran ID already exist...` message. The observable sixteen-digit zero-padded wire format is unchanged | One `existsById` probe per assignment; a pathologically dense id space raises the frozen message rather than overwriting |
| The interest-calculation driving reader is `@StepScope` | Leave it a singleton; serialize interest runs with an external lock | The reader is an `ItemStream` holding the run's page cursor and peeked row. As a singleton it was shared by every concurrent step execution, so two simultaneous runs consumed one another's rows and each posted only part of the portfolio while both reported success. Step scoping gives each `StepExecution` its own reader, delegate, cursor and `ExecutionContext` entry | Concurrent runs over the same portfolio now contend on `accounts`; the loser is rejected by `@Version` optimistic locking and is restartable, which is the intended protection against double-posting interest |
| `BatchOutputPathResolver` was promoted to `carddemo-common` and every batch writer resolves through it, including the `dalyrejs.txt` reject file and `statements.txt` / `statements.html` | Duplicate the resolver per service; hard-code absolute container paths; relax the container filesystem | The resolver existed only in batch-service, so the statement writer defaulted to the working-directory-relative `output/statements.txt` and the reject writer to `dalyrejs.txt`. Both resolve under `/app` in the delivered containers, whose root filesystem is read-only, so statement generation could never succeed and every rejected record would have been lost. One shared resolver confines all batch I/O to a configured, writable, allowlisted root (defaulting under the JVM temporary directory) and rejects traversal outside it (CWE-22) | Absolute paths outside the configured root are refused by design, so callers and tests must supply root-relative names |
| A failed batch step deletes the output it was writing (`FailedOutputCleanupListener`) | Leave the partial file; write to a temporary file and rename atomically on success | A flat-file writer opens its resource when the step starts and closes it when the step ends whatever the outcome, so a failed dump left a file carrying BOTH legacy banners and zero data records — indistinguishable from a successful run over an empty input — and a failed statement run left two zero-byte files. Deleting on an unsuccessful step is the smallest change that guarantees no artefact survives to be mistaken for a completed run; temp-and-rename would have required reworking every writer's resource wiring. The listener is registered on EVERY file-producing step — `transactionPostingStep`, `accountReadStep`, `cardReadStep`, `cardXrefReadStep`, `customerReadStep`, `categoryBalanceReportStep`, `combineTransactionsStep`, `transactionDetailReportStep` and `statementGenerationStep` (both artefacts) — and resolves each path through the same `BatchOutputPathResolver` binding the writer uses, step-scoped wherever the destination is a job parameter. It had been registered on NO step at all, so this entry described behaviour that did not exist. Success is a `COMPLETED` status with an exit code BEGINNING with `COMPLETED`, so a posting run that ends `COMPLETED_WITH_REJECTS` keeps the reject generation that is its whole deliverable, while a business failure signalled through the exit code alone (`FAILED_EMPTY_FEED`) is still cleaned up. On the posting step the listener is registered BEFORE the reject-counting listener because `CompositeStepExecutionListener` runs `afterStep` in reverse registration order, so it observes the final status | A partially written file is discarded rather than retained for diagnosis; the failure cause remains in the step's exit message and the log |
