| `eslint.config.js` gains a `'src/testing/**'` override extending the Jest-globals block | Add the harness to the existing test-file glob; disable the rule for the file | `src/testing/sessionHarness.ts` is test infrastructure that is not itself a `*.test.tsx` file, so it fell outside the glob that grants Jest globals and its `jest.fn()`-shaped parameter tripped `no-undef`. A path-scoped override keeps the grant narrow; widening the test glob would extend Jest globals to any future non-test file placed there, and a file-level disable would hide genuine undefined references | The override is keyed to the directory, so anything added under `src/testing/` inherits Jest globals `[frontend/eslint.config.js]` |

### 37.1 Findings declined on AAP precedence (end-to-end security gate)

Four findings of this review cannot be closed by changing code, because the change each asks for is
forbidden by the frozen AAP. Each row states the residual exposure plainly, names the AAP section
that governs, and records the mitigations that ARE in force, so the decision is auditable and can be
revisited by whoever owns the AAP. Rows in §19.1 decline the equivalent findings of the previous
checkpoint under the same reasoning; these rows restate the position against this review's numbering
and against the code as delivered today.

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| **AAP PRECEDENCE (finding C‑07).** No `@PreAuthorize`, ownership predicate or session-derived filter is added to `GET`/`PUT /accounts/{id}`, `GET`/`PUT /cards/{cardNumber}`, `GET /transactions/{id}` or `POST /billpay` | Add an ownership predicate deriving the caller's customer id from the session and reject any key not linked to it; filter every repository read by the session principal; annotate each method with a per-resource expression | The legacy programs authorize by SCREEN REACHABILITY, never by row ownership: `app/csd/CARDDEMO.CSD` carries `RESSEC(NO)` and `CMDSEC(NO)` on all eighteen transaction definitions, so CICS performed no per-resource check, and `SEC-USR-TYPE` gates which SCREENS the menu offers rather than which rows a screen may read. AAP §0.6.7 specifies the security model exhaustively — a password encoder and the `'A'`/`'U'` → `ROLE_ADMIN`/`ROLE_USER` mapping — and specifies no entitlement model; §0.7.1 requires validation rules and workflow sequencing to be reproduced exactly, and §0.7.6 forbids modifying business logic beyond the technology conversion. A per-principal entitlement filter is therefore a NEW business rule, not a migration of an existing one | **RESIDUAL, STATED PLAINLY:** any authenticated CardDemo user can read or update any account, card or transaction by supplying its key, and can post a bill payment against any account. This is a genuine IDOR exposure that the delivered code does not prevent. The review's own adjudication is that it requires a human decision on the AAP's internal conflict between §0.7.1 functional equivalence and a general security mandate, and it is NOT within an implementing agent's authority to resolve by editing either the code or the AAP. Mitigations in force: authentication is required on every one of these routes, the routes are reachable only through the gateway, and administrator-only operations ARE enforced at both the URL and — since this pass — the method level. Closing this requires an AAP amendment adding an entitlement model `[app/csd/CARDDEMO.CSD; AAP §0.6.7, §0.7.1, §0.7.6]` |
| **AAP PRECEDENCE (finding C‑08).** The card number continues to travel as a path segment on `GET`/`PUT /cards/{cardNumber}`, and `GET /transactions/{id}` continues to return `tranCardNum` verbatim for the screen to render | Move the card number into a request body or an opaque server-side handle; mask the PAN in the transaction response; truncate to the last four digits everywhere | AAP §0.6.7 enumerates the fields to mask and encrypt — SSN, government-issued id and CVV — and the card PAN is deliberately NOT among them, while `app/bms/COTRN01.bms:L117-118` declares the card number as a displayed field on the transaction-view mapset, so masking it would change a frozen UI contract. §0.7.6 forbids changing data formats for downstream consumers, and the transaction DTO is one such contract | **RESIDUAL:** the PAN appears in the request line of `card-service`, so any component that logs raw request lines — a reverse proxy, an access log, an APM agent — could capture it. Mitigations verified in force: no SPA route carries a PAN (pinned by `frontend/src/api/wireContract.test.ts`), the browser reaches the API same-origin through the nginx `/api` prefix so the PAN is not exposed to a third-party origin, and application log masking is now COMPLETE — the two duplicate unmasked log statements that broke it are deleted in this pass, and `extractPath` has exactly one caller, wrapped in `SensitiveDataMasker.maskPan`. Closing the remainder requires an AAP amendment permitting a wire-shape change `[app/bms/COTRN01.bms:L117-118; AAP §0.6.7, §0.7.6]` |
| **AAP PRECEDENCE (finding C‑09).** The ten seeded `security_users` rows keep ONE shared BCrypt hash of `PASSWORD` | Seed ten distinct random credentials and print them at first start; seed no users and require an operator to create the first administrator | The seeded users ARE the reference data the seventeen UI workflows are exercised against, and the authority for them is the in-stream data of the legacy load job `app/jcl/DUSRSECJ.jcl:L35-L44`, which carries all ten records with the literal password `PASSWORD` and the trailing `A`/`U` `SEC-USR-TYPE` flag (there is no security-user file among the nine `app/data/ASCII` fixtures, so that directory is NOT the source here). Distinct random credentials would make the documented workflows unreproducible; seeding no users would leave the sign-on workflow with nothing to sign on as | **RESIDUAL, and already recorded at §6:** a single shared password across five administrators and five users is a real weakness, ACCEPTED for local and demo profiles ONLY. The credential exists only as a BCrypt hash — never plaintext — and no in-scope test embeds the literal. Any shared, staging or production environment must rotate these credentials before exposure; that instruction is part of the §6 row and is not superseded here `[app/data/ASCII; AAP §0.2.1; §6 of this log]` |
| **RESOLVED, not declined (finding M‑15).** `Card.cardCvvCd` keeps its encrypted-at-rest CVV column; only its DOCSTRING is corrected, because the docstring promised a clearing path the code does not implement | Implement the promised clearing path; drop the column | AAP §0.6.7 REQUIRES the CVV to be masked and encrypted at rest, so retaining the encrypted column is compliance, not exposure, and `CVACT02Y` declares `CARD-CVV-CD` as part of the frozen 150-byte card record so the field cannot be dropped. The defect was purely documentary: a docstring describing behaviour that does not exist is worse than no docstring, because a reader relies on it. It now states only what is delivered — encrypted through `CryptoConverter`, `@JsonIgnore` so it never serializes to a client, and excluded from `toString()` and logs | None outstanding; a future clearing policy must implement the path and the docstring together `[app/cpy/CVACT02Y.cpy; carddemo-common/.../domain/Card.java:L81-L91; AAP §0.6.7]` |

### 37.2 Build-time dependency-vulnerability gate (finding S‑17)

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| The OWASP `dependency-check-maven` gate is declared in a NON-DEFAULT Maven profile (`-P dependency-scan`) rather than bound into the default lifecycle | Bind `dependency-check:check` to `verify` unconditionally; add it only to a CI workflow file; use `mvn versions:display-dependency-updates` instead; rely on the existing manual CVE pins alone | An unconditional binding would make EVERY build depend on the network and on the NVD feed: the plugin is not managed by the Spring Boot BOM, so Maven would have to resolve it before `verify` could run, and the first scan downloads and processes the full CVE datafeed. That turns a reproducible offline `mvn clean install` into a build that fails without connectivity — a regression for a repository whose ordinary build is expected to work from the local repository. Putting the gate only in a CI workflow file would make it unrunnable by a developer and invisible from the POM, which is where a reader looks for the build's quality gates. `versions:display-dependency-updates` reports NEWER versions, not vulnerable ones, so it does not answer the finding. Verified empirically: with the profile inactive the effective POM contains ZERO `dependency-check-maven` plugin entries in `<build><plugins>` (only the inert profile declaration), and `mvn -B -o clean install -DskipTests` succeeds across all eleven modules; with `-P dependency-scan` the plugin appears in the active build bound to `verify` | The gate does not run unless asked for, so a release process MUST invoke `mvn -B -P dependency-scan verify` explicitly — a pipeline that forgets it gets no scan and no warning. That is the deliberate trade-off for keeping the default build offline-reproducible, and it is why the invocation is written into the profile's own comment in the POM `[pom.xml]` |
| The gate fails the build at `failBuildOnCVSS` 7 and skips test- and provided-scope artifacts | Fail on any CVE at all (CVSS 0); fail only on CRITICAL (9.0); scan every scope including test | CVSS 7.0 is the CVSS v3 HIGH boundary, so the threshold is "HIGH and CRITICAL fail, MEDIUM and LOW report" — a line drawn on the published severity scale rather than an arbitrary number. Failing on any CVE makes the gate unusable, because LOW-severity advisories against transitive build tooling are continuous. Failing only at 9.0 would let a HIGH-severity remote-execution advisory through. Test- and provided-scope artifacts never ship inside a service image, so a CVE in Testcontainers or in a servlet-API stub is not a deployed exposure and must not block a release; scanning them would generate failures that cannot be acted on | A MEDIUM-severity advisory in a shipped dependency is reported but does not fail the gate, so the HTML and JSON reports still have to be read rather than merely exit-code-checked. The scanner version is pinned explicitly (`13.0.0`) because the Boot BOM does not manage it, so a scanner upgrade is a deliberate edit `[pom.xml]` |
| `frontend/package.json` gains an `audit` script (`npm audit --audit-level=high`) that is NOT chained into `test` or `build` | Chain `npm audit` into `npm test`; add a `preinstall` audit hook; add a second Node scanner | The frontend has its own dependency graph and its own registry advisory database, so the Maven gate cannot see it; `npm audit` is the first-party tool and needs no new dependency. Keeping it out of `test` and `build` is the same decision as the Maven profile and for the same reason: those two commands must stay deterministic and runnable from the existing `node_modules`, and an advisory published overnight must not turn yesterday's green build red. The `--audit-level=high` threshold matches the Maven gate's CVSS 7 boundary so the two halves of the build hold one standard. Currently reports zero vulnerabilities at that level | Requires network access and must be invoked deliberately as `npm run audit`, exactly like the Maven profile `[frontend/package.json]` |
| The parent POM's surefire/failsafe comment is consolidated from two stacked paragraphs into one | Leave both paragraphs; delete the older one without merging | The block had accumulated two full descriptions of the same test-only PII key, written at different times, which disagreed on emphasis and forced a reader to reconcile them — the same duplicated-doc-block defect the review reported in three Java files, surviving here because the earlier sweep covered Java doc comments and not XML comments. Deleting one outright would have lost the detail each carried that the other did not (the eager startup validation on one side, the enumerated encrypted columns and the surefire/failsafe symmetry on the other), so the two were merged | None; the comment is non-executable and the pom remains well-formed, verified by parse and by a clean eleven-module offline build `[pom.xml]` |

## 38. SPA Entry Point (`main.tsx` checkpoint)

Non-literal choices made while delivering `frontend/src/main.tsx` — the Vite ES-module entry point
that `frontend/index.html` loads, creates the React root on the `#root` node, and mounts the routed
`App` inside the SPA's single browser-history router. Recorded here per the Explainability rule so
the one place the application is bootstrapped stays auditable against its build instruction.

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| `BrowserRouter` is imported from **`react-router`**, not from the `react-router-dom` specifier the entry point's own build instruction names | Reinstate `react-router-dom` (latest 7.18.2) as a second dependency beside `react-router@8.3.0`; downgrade the whole SPA back to `react-router-dom@7.18.x`; hand-roll a history binding over the router's lower-level primitives | `react-router-dom` has NO 8.x release — the package was folded into `react-router` in v8 — and §19.2 removed it repo-wide (finding M‑24) when the fixed 8.3.0 line was adopted for the RSC-mode CSRF advisory, so it is absent from both `package.json` and `node_modules`. Naming it here would fail `tsc --noEmit` and `vite build` outright, and all other router import sites in `frontend/src` already name `react-router`. `BrowserRouter` is exported unchanged by v8, so the render tree the instruction freezes is reproduced exactly and only the module specifier differs | The entry point's specifier differs from the wording of its instruction. Behaviour verified at runtime in Chrome against the rebuilt container: in-app navigation pushed real `window.history` entries (`history.length` 3→4) without re-fetching the document, and browser back/forward both resolved `[frontend/package.json; frontend/src/main.tsx:L13]` |
| The mount reads `document.getElementById('root')!` with the non-null assertion, replacing the `ROOT_ELEMENT_ID` constant and the explicit `if (container === null) throw new Error(…)` guard the previous revision carried | Keep the guard and its `:raises Error:` docstring line; render into a lazily created fallback container; log an error and return without mounting | `frontend/index.html` is a committed deliverable that declares `<div id="root"></div>`, so the node is guaranteed by construction, and the file's instruction declares the assertion intentional under `strict`. Spec-literal fidelity (AAP §0.7.2) freezes the render tree, and the thrown message was observable output the requirement does not ask for. Removing it costs no diagnostics: React's own `createRoot` failure (`Target container is not a DOM element`) names the same fault at the same instant. The project's ESLint gate does not forbid the assertion — `eslint.config.js` extends `recommendedTypeChecked`, not `strict`, and `no-unnecessary-type-assertion` does not fire because `!` genuinely narrows `HTMLElement \| null` | A hand-edited `index.html` that drops the mount node now fails inside React rather than at the SPA's own guard. Verified at runtime: `#root` mounts with 46 descendant elements and the console carries zero application errors `[frontend/index.html; frontend/src/main.tsx:L17]` AMENDED: the entry point now also registers global error telemetry and mounts a root `ErrorBoundary`, so a failure at or below the mount is contained and reported on line 23 inside the terminal frame instead of blanking the document — the class of problem the removed guard gestured at is now handled where React can actually observe it, without reinstating a user-visible throw. See section 39.6. |
| `React.StrictMode` is used through a default `import React from 'react'` rather than the named `import { StrictMode }` the previous revision used | Keep the named import; drop `StrictMode` altogether | The build instruction freezes the render tree as the literal `<React.StrictMode><BrowserRouter><App /></BrowserRouter></React.StrictMode>`, and `esModuleInterop: true` makes the default import valid. Both forms resolve to the same element type, so the mounted tree, the double-invoked development checks and the production bundle are unchanged — this is conformance to the frozen literal, not a behavioural choice | None observable: `tsc --noEmit` (with `noUnusedLocals`), `eslint .`, the 660-test frontend suite and `vite build` are all green, and the built bundle stayed at 383 kB `[frontend/src/main.tsx:L11,L18-22]` AMENDED: the frozen literal now carries one additional wrapper — `<React.StrictMode><ErrorBoundary><BrowserRouter><App/></BrowserRouter></ErrorBoundary></React.StrictMode>` — which adds a failure path without altering any element the build instruction names. See section 39.6. |

## 39. Code-Review Remediation — Launch Surface, API Currency, Financial Truncation, Presentation Containment and Measured Non-Functional Evidence

Non-literal choices made while resolving the acceptance-gate review, plus the deviations that
review found unlogged. Recorded here per the Explainability rule so that every change made in
response to the review is auditable, including the ones that declined the review's own suggestion
and the ones that corrected a defect the review did not report.

### 39.1 Previously unlogged deviations the review identified

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| The account-update transaction writes **customer first, then account**, which is the lock order AAP 0.6.2 mandates (customer → account → card → transaction) and the REVERSE of the order the legacy program used | Reproduce `COACTUPC`'s own order (`REWRITE` the account file at `[app/cbl/COACTUPC.cbl:L4066]`, then the customer file at `L4086]`); acquire an explicit advisory lock before either write | The AAP names a single consistent lock order for the whole system precisely so that two concurrent flows touching the same pair of rows cannot deadlock by approaching them from opposite ends. `COACTUPC` could write in either order safely because CICS serialized the pair inside one task; a service that runs many requests concurrently against a relational database cannot. Where the AAP and the legacy sequence disagree, the AAP governs (its own precedence rule), so customer precedes account. The choice is not observable: both writes commit inside ONE `@Transactional` unit, so no reader can see the intermediate state and the committed result is identical either way | The ordering guarantee holds only while every flow that touches both rows obeys it; a future flow that writes account-then-customer would reintroduce the deadlock this order exists to prevent. `AtomicRollbackIT` pins the all-or-nothing boundary, not the order, so the order is enforced by review rather than by test |
| `frontend/package.json` declares `engines.node: ">=22.22.0"` rather than the Node 24 that AAP 0.5.1 pins | Declare `">=24"` to match the AAP verbatim; leave the previous `">=22.12.0"`; drop the `engines` field | `">=22.22.0"` is not an arbitrary loosening — it is the floor `react-router@8.3.0` declares for itself, so it is the true minimum under which the dependency graph is supported, and it is strictly tighter than the `">=22.12.0"` it replaces. Declaring `">=24"` was rejected because it would make the field FALSE for this repository's own toolchain: the installed runtime is Node 22.23.2, so every `npm` invocation would warn or refuse and the local build would stop working. The AAP's Node 24 pin is honoured where it actually governs the artifact — `frontend/Dockerfile` builds and runs the image on `node:24` — so the shipped container is on the pinned major while the declared engine range stays true of every environment the project supports | The declared range is wider than the image's runtime, so a contributor on Node 22 can build a bundle that was never exercised on 24. The `node:24` build stage in the image is the gate that closes this, and it runs in CI on every image build |

### 39.2 Batch launch surface and per-job authorization

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| `BatchJobController` is DELETED and `BatchController`'s query-parameter contract is kept as the single batch launch surface | Delete `BatchController` and keep the newer `@RequestBody Map` controller; keep both and disambiguate by adding a path segment or a `params`/`consumes` condition | The two classes registered identical `RequestMappingInfo` for `GET /batch/jobs` and `POST /batch/jobs/{jobName}`, so Spring MVC threw `IllegalStateException: Ambiguous mapping` and batch-service could not refresh its context at all — with the whole Compose stack gated on it. Only one contract has a real caller: `BatchJobClient` submits QUERY PARAMETERS and no body, which is what `BatchController` accepts, so keeping the body-based controller would have required changing a working client too. Disambiguating and keeping both was rejected because two launch surfaces for the same nine jobs is exactly the duplication that produced the collision | The deleted class's traceability row was a verbatim duplicate of the surviving one and was removed with it. A context-refresh test (`BatchServiceApplicationConfigurationTest`) now fails fast on any future ambiguous mapping, which is the gap that let this reach a checkpoint |
| Every job-launch route requires `ROLE_ADMIN` except `transactionDetailReportJob`, enforced independently in batch-service's OWN filter chain AND in the gateway | Leave `/batch/**` at `hasAnyRole("USER","ADMIN")`; gate at the gateway only; gate inside the controller with `@PreAuthorize` | `.anyRequest().authenticated()` in batch-service and a blanket `/batch/**` USER-or-ADMIN rule at the gateway let any ordinary user launch `interestCalculationJob`, which rewrites `acct_curr_bal` on EVERY account, plus seven other jobs whose legacy analogues were operator-submitted JCL with no CICS transaction at all — so no legacy user could reach them. `transactionDetailReportJob` is the single exception because main-menu option 9 is a `userType 'U'` option, so an ordinary user legitimately requests that report. Gating in both places is deliberate defence in depth: the gateway is the public entrypoint, but batch-service must not depend on being unreachable directly. The gateway already had this exact precedent for `POST /reports/statements` | Two places now encode the same authority, so they can drift. `BatchLaunchAuthorizationTest` (batch-service) and `BatchLaunchRouteAuthorizationTest` (gateway) assert all nine jobs on both sides — 24 assertions — so drift fails the build |

### 39.3 Spring Batch API currency

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| Deprecated `JobLauncher` / `TaskExecutorJobLauncher` are replaced by `JobOperator` / `TaskExecutorJobOperator`, and `launcher.run(job, params)` by `operator.start(job, params)`, in all three job-launching configurations | Keep `JobLauncher` and widen the `@SuppressWarnings`; wait for a later Spring Batch minor | `JobLauncher` is deprecated for removal in Spring Batch 6.0.4, and `JobOperator extends JobLauncher`, so the replacement is a strict superset reached without changing call semantics. `start(Job, JobParameters)` delegates to `run(...)` unless the job declares a `JobParametersIncrementer`; this codebase declares NONE, verified by grep, so `start` is behaviourally identical here. Suppressing the warning was rejected because the API is deprecated FOR REMOVAL — the suppression would silently become a compilation failure on upgrade | `TaskExecutorJobOperator` requires a non-null `JobRegistry`: `SimpleJobOperator.afterPropertiesSet` asserts "JobLocator must be provided". A hand-constructed `MapJobRegistry` is supplied and deliberately left EMPTY, because these operators are handed the `Job` bean directly and never resolve one by name; a manual instance receives no `ApplicationContextAware` or `SmartInitializingSingleton` callback, so it stays exactly what is registered. Any future call that resolves a job by NAME through these operators would find the registry empty and must register the job first. The launcher beans were renamed (`asyncJobLauncher` → `asyncJobOperator`, `postingJobLauncher` → `postingJobOperator`); these are internal bean names, not frozen legacy contracts |
| `.chunk(int, PlatformTransactionManager)` is replaced by `.chunk(int).transactionManager(...)` in the posting and statement steps | Suppress the deprecation; move both steps to a `CompletionPolicy` | The two-argument overload is deprecated for removal; `StepBuilder.chunk(int)` returns a `ChunkOrientedStepBuilder` that carries `.transactionManager(...)` plus every reader, processor, writer, stream and listener registration these steps already use, so the migration is mechanical and order-preserving. Verified by `javap` against the 6.0.4 jar before editing | `ChunkOrientedStepBuilder.listener(...)` overloads accept `StepListener` or `Object`, so the existing `(StepExecutionListener)` and `(ItemWriteListener<PostingItem>)` casts still resolve through `StepListener` rather than silently binding to `Object` |
| `InterestCalculationJobConfig` KEEPS the deprecated `.chunk(CompletionPolicy, PlatformTransactionManager)` overload, with the suppression narrowed to that method and justified in place | Migrate to `.chunk(int)` and accept a fixed chunk size; drive the control break from a step listener; commit once per record | Spring Batch 6.0.4 offers NO non-deprecated equivalent: the replacement `ChunkOrientedStepBuilder` holds its chunk size as a final `int` and has no `CompletionPolicy` support whatsoever, verified by `javap`. The step's chunk boundary is a per-account CONTROL BREAK — `CBACT04C` accumulates `WS-TOTAL-INT` across all of an account's category balances and rolls the account up once — so a fixed size would split an account's interest across two transactions and a per-record commit would write one roll-up per category instead of one per account. Retaining the deprecated call preserves the legacy unit of work; the review's own wording permits retention with a logged rationale | The call must be revisited when Spring Batch restores a completion-policy equivalent or removes the overload. The suppression is scoped to the single method rather than the class, so no other deprecated usage can hide behind it — which is how the class-level suppression on `batch-service`'s `JobSchedulingConfig` had been masking five `JobLauncher` usages |

### 39.4 Financial truncation and frozen byte contracts

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| All 20 monetary `setScale(2, …)` sites use `RoundingMode.DOWN`, and the six test classes that pinned half-up boundaries were realigned to truncation | Keep `HALF_UP` as the house rounding mode; make the mode configurable; round half-up only in presentation and truncate only in persistence | COBOL `COMPUTE` truncates toward zero unless the statement carries the `ROUNDED` phrase, and no monetary `COMPUTE` in this application carries it — so half-up rounding produces a different figure from the system being replaced, which AAP 0.7.6 forbids ("do not alter COBOL data precision, rounding, or calculation logic"). Tests align to the specification, never the reverse: assertions that encoded `100.005 → 100.01` were encoding the defect, so they now encode `100.00`. `TransactionValidationProcessorTest`'s over-limit fixture had to be RE-DERIVED rather than edited, because under truncation `1000.005` becomes `1000.00`, which is no longer over a `1000.00` limit — the old fixture proved the opposite of what its name claimed | Truncation and half-up agree except when the discarded digits are exactly at the boundary, so a regression here is invisible in ordinary data. Proven at scale instead: 50,000 interest transactions computed from a balance of `1000.40` at rate `15.00` — `(1000.40*15.00)/1200 = 12.5050` — were all written as `12.50` totalling `625,000.00`, where half-up would have produced `12.51` and `625,500.00`. Sites that use half-up merely to PAD a value to scale 2 (no boundary digits) were left alone, and are distinguished by comment |
| The three fixed-width batch sinks pin `.lineSeparator("\n")` explicitly | Rely on the platform default; write the records through a raw `Writer` | `FlatFileItemWriter` defaults to `System.lineSeparator()`, so the record length of a frozen contract would depend on the JVM's host: 431 bytes on Linux and 432 on Windows for the 430-byte `DALYREJS` record, breaking the `RECFM=F LRECL=430` contract `[app/jcl/POSTTRAN.jcl]` and the 80/100-byte statement contracts. Rule 5 requires byte-identical output, which cannot be conditional on the host. Two sibling sinks already pinned it, so this makes the policy uniform rather than introducing it. Full rationale is recorded in section 10.1 | Verified at runtime, not just by unit test: a 50,000-record reject run produced a `dalyrejs.txt` of exactly 21,550,000 bytes — 431 bytes per record, the 430-byte record plus one `LF` |
| `V1__create_schema.sql` was amended IN PLACE twice (comment-only), changing its Flyway checksum | Add a corrective `V9__` migration; use `flyway.validateMigrationNaming`/`ignoreMigrationPatterns` to tolerate the mismatch; leave the misleading comments in place | Both amendments are comment-only — the first reduced an 18-line rationale block that duplicated section 10 of this log (and removed an orphan `--.` fragment), the second corrected a comment that claimed `2000-POST-TRANSACTION` stamps `dalytran_proc_ts` at posting time, which `CBTRN02C` does not do (it opens `DALYTRAN` with `OPEN INPUT` and never rewrites the feed). A new migration cannot amend a comment, and suppressing checksum validation would disable the guard that protects every OTHER migration. Amending in place is safe HERE because no deployed database is being carried forward: the schema is provisioned from empty in every environment this project ships | Any database already migrated by the previous revision will fail `flyway validate` with a checksum mismatch on version 1 and must be rebuilt or repaired with `flyway repair`. This was actually hit during development, and is the reason the development database is torn down and re-provisioned rather than reused. Proven safe from empty: a full reactor build reports 19 Testcontainers contexts each applying 8 migrations with zero validation failures |

### 39.5 Defects found while remediating that the review did not report

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| The three per-service `BatchInfrastructureConfig` classes are DELETED and all three job-hosting services share `carddemo-common`'s single `JdbcBatchConfiguration` | Keep one copy and delete the other two; rename the beans per service; mark the duplicates `@Primary` | The three files were byte-identical and each declared a `jobRepository` bean, so every service that imported both them and the shared configuration had two definitions of the same bean name and could not refresh its context at all. Three copies of one definition is also the condition that let them drift apart unnoticed. One shared definition keeps the create-isolation level and the observation registry identical in all three services, which per-service copies had already been relied upon to do | Replacing Boot's batch auto-configuration wholesale means new Boot batch defaults are not inherited automatically — already recorded in section 9.5 for the shared class itself |
| The overloaded `@Bean` methods in `DataManagementJobConfig` are replaced by distinctly named methods | Keep the overloads and disambiguate with explicit `@Bean(name=…)`; split the class | Two `@Bean` methods sharing a name register ONE definition — the second silently overwrites the first — so a step was wired to the wrong reader. All 18 batch-service integration tests errored on it. Distinct names make the registration one-to-one and the wiring readable at the call site | A duplicate bean NAME is now caught mechanically: `noConfigurationClassOverloadsABeanName` reflects over every `@Configuration` class in the module and fails on any repeated `@Bean` method name, which is the blindness that let both this and the `jobRepository` collision reach a checkpoint |
| `CardService.listCards` pages through a `ScreenWindow` that requests one extra row rather than computing the page slice from the raw page number | Keep the arithmetic and fix the off-by-one in place; ask the repository for a count as well as a page | The shipped arithmetic dropped exactly one card at every page boundary — every eighth card, given the seven-rows-per-page screen — so a card could be invisible in the list while being reachable by direct lookup. `COCRDLIC` decides `CA-NEXT-PAGE-EXISTS` by reading ONE record beyond the screen and discarding it, so requesting `pageSize + 1` and trimming reproduces the legacy paging state directly instead of deriving it arithmetically. A separate count query was rejected as a second round trip the legacy screen never made | The window must trim the probe row before rendering; a caller that forgets would show eight rows on a seven-row screen. The pagination-boundary integration test walks every page of the seeded 50 cards and asserts the union equals the full set, so a dropped or duplicated row fails |
| The card list scopes to an account when a FILTER is supplied, not according to the caller's ROLE | Scope by role (administrators see everything, users see only their session account); scope always by session account | `COCRDLIC` branches on whether the account-id filter field is populated, not on `CDEMO-USER-TYPE`: supplying a filter narrows the browse for anyone, and supplying none browses the ordered card master for anyone. Making the scope role-dependent would let an administrator's unfiltered list differ from the legacy screen's, and would silently ignore a filter an administrator did type | An ordinary user CAN browse the whole card master when it supplies no filter — the same disclosure the 3270 screen allows, accepted on AAP precedence in §19.1 and reconciled in §40.7. Pinned by `CardViewUpdateIT.listScopeFollowsTheSuppliedFilterNotTheCallerRole` and `CardServiceTest.listCards_unfilteredBrowseIsIdenticalForAnOrdinaryUserAndAnAdministrator` (the earlier `nonAdminListScopedToSessionAccount` guard asserted the superseded contract and was removed) |
| `AccountViewUpdateIT` TRUNCATES the seeded `card_xref` table in `setUp` instead of dropping and re-creating it | Keep the drop-and-recreate; recreate the table WITH its constraints; use `@DirtiesContext` | The drop-and-recreate rebuilt `card_xref` WITHOUT `fk_card_xref_cust` and `fk_card_xref_acct`, so it silently removed the referential integrity AAP 0.6.4 requires — and because Testcontainers shares one container across the JVM, it left the schema damaged for every suite that ran afterwards, which is why a sibling repository test failed only when the whole group ran. Truncating touches rows, not structure, so the constraints survive | The four cases that need a cross-reference linkage now insert their own row, matching the eight sibling cases that already did. A future case that forgets will fail on the FK rather than pass against a constraint-free table — which is the point |

### 39.6 Frontend presentation containment

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| A root `ErrorBoundary` wraps `<BrowserRouter>`, so the frozen render tree of section 38 becomes `<React.StrictMode><ErrorBoundary><BrowserRouter><App/></BrowserRouter></ErrorBoundary></React.StrictMode>` | Leave the tree literal and accept that a render-time throw blanks the screen; wrap `App` inside the router instead; use an error-boundary library | Section 38 froze the tree as the literal its build instruction named, but that literal had no failure path at all: a throw during render unmounted everything and left an EMPTY document, which no 3270 screen can do. The boundary is placed OUTSIDE the router deliberately — a throw raised while resolving a route must still be caught, and a boundary inside the router cannot catch that. This adds one wrapper to the frozen literal rather than altering the elements it names, and it is the minimum that restores a failure path | The tree literal in section 38's row is now amended rather than contradicted; the row records the addition explicitly. `ErrorBoundary.test.tsx` pins containment with 11 scenarios, and flipping `getDerivedStateFromError` to a non-failing state was probed and fails exactly the three containment tests |
| The fallback reuses `ErrorBanner` inside a `.screen` frame and displays the verbatim legacy literal `UNEXPECTED ABEND OCCURRED.` | Invent wording such as "Something went wrong"; render the error message or stack; render a bare `<p>` outside the terminal frame | The legacy application already had an answer for an unexpected failure: `COCRDUPC`'s `ABEND-ROUTINE` moves `'UNEXPECTED ABEND OCCURRED.'` into `ABEND-MSG` when no specific message was set, sends it with `ERASE`, and abends `[app/cbl/COCRDUPC.cbl:L1531-L1552]`. Rule 5 forbids inventing observable output where the source supplies it, so the literal is reproduced character-for-character. Reusing `ErrorBanner` puts it on line 23 — where every other message appears — inside the 24x80 frame, and reuses three CSS classes that already exist, so the fallback needs no new styling and no inline styles. Rendering the error text or stack was rejected as unrequested observable output that also leaks internals | The fallback deliberately shows nothing else — no stack, no correlation id, no retry control. A `TreeWalker` over the rendered document found exactly ONE text node, confirming that. Diagnosis therefore depends entirely on the telemetry record, not on the screen |
| `window.onerror` and `unhandledrejection` are registered BEFORE `createRoot`, and emit one `spa.uncaught-error` record carrying the correlation id | Register inside a `useEffect` in `App`; rely on the boundary alone; send the record to a logging endpoint | A failure during the very first render happens before any component effect runs, so an effect-based registration cannot observe the case that matters most. The record carries the SAME correlation id the API client already sends as `X-Correlation-Id`, which is what makes a browser-side failure joinable to the server-side trace it came from — the Observability rule's requirement that correlation crosses boundaries. Posting to an endpoint was rejected as unrequested network behaviour; the record is emitted where a collector can pick it up | `generateCorrelationId` had to be exported from `api/client.ts` and re-exported through the `api` barrel so `randomUUID` keeps exactly ONE call site. Because ESM re-exports are statically linked, `App.test.tsx`'s `jest.unstable_mockModule('./api/client', …)` double then had to supply `generateCorrelationId` too — omitting it failed 74 tests with "does not provide an export named". Any future mock of that module must supply every export the barrel re-exports |

### 39.7 Build-context hygiene and service test parity

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| `reporting-service` gets NO module-level `.dockerignore`; the repository-root file governs it, and an invariant test pins that arrangement | Add a module-level `.dockerignore` matching the eight siblings, as the review suggested; add a BuildKit `Dockerfile.dockerignore`; change the build to a module context | The review's premise was empirically disproved. Docker resolves `.dockerignore` relative to the build CONTEXT, and `reporting-service` is the ONLY image built with `context: .` — its Dockerfile compiles the Maven reactor IN-CONTAINER and must copy the aggregator POM, all ten module POMs and `carddemo-common`, which a module context cannot reach. A module-level file would therefore never be read: dead configuration that reads like a control. Worse, the sibling allowlist (`*`, `!target`, `target/*`, `!target/*.jar`) would flatly contradict an image that builds from source. Proven from a real build: `load .dockerignore` transferred 3.71 kB against a root file of exactly 3671 bytes. `Dockerfile.dockerignore` was rejected because that form REPLACES the root file — trading roughly 4 MB of context for the risk of silently re-admitting `.git` or the secret `.env` | The arrangement is now enforced rather than described: `DockerBuildContextHygieneTest` asserts that every compose `build:` entry has a `.dockerignore` governing its own context, that exactly one image is root-context and it is reporting-service, that a module-level `reporting-service/.dockerignore` does NOT exist, and that the root file excludes `.git`/`.env`/`**/target` while still admitting the reactor inputs. Three controlled experiments confirmed each assertion fails when violated |
| The duplicate-`@Bean`-name guard lives in package `com.carddemo.common.packaging`, not `…​.build` | Use `…​.build`; add a `.gitignore` negation for `**/src/**/build/` | `.gitignore` carries `build/`, which matches a directory named `build` at ANY depth — including a Java package. The file compiled and ran perfectly while being invisible to `git status`, so the loss would have surfaced only after the commit. A negation cannot rescue it either: git cannot re-include a file inside an EXCLUDED DIRECTORY, so the negation would have to precede the directory rule and would weaken the ignore for real build output. Renaming the package costs nothing and removes the trap. `dist` and `target` are equally poisoned as package names; `packaging`, `containers`, `image` and `out` are safe | Any future package named `build`, `dist` or `target` will silently fail to be committed. A tree-wide sweep confirmed no source-like file on disk is currently git-ignored, and Rule 3 verification (`git ls-files -i -c --exclude-standard` empty) is part of the standard validation sweep |

### 39.8 Measured non-functional evidence

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| A k6 harness (`perf/carddemo-online-load.js`) is committed and the measured figures are published in `README-target.md` | Rely on the Grafana SLO panels; measure with JMeter or Gatling; keep the harness outside the repository | AAP 0.7.1 states numeric targets, and the checkpoint states explicitly that dashboard configuration alone is not evidence: the panels satisfy the Observability rule but prove nothing about behaviour at 150 users. A committed harness makes the claim reproducible by anyone, and declaring the target as a k6 THRESHOLD means a run that misses it exits non-zero, so the evidence cannot quietly rot. k6 was chosen over JMeter because the scenario is a JavaScript-authored HTTP flow with a cookie jar and CSRF echo, which needs no GUI-authored plan and diffs as source. It lives outside the Maven and npm builds so the ordinary build stays offline and fast | The harness needs a running stack and is therefore not a unit test; nothing runs it automatically. Measured result at exactly 150 users for two minutes: 33,912 requests, 0 failed, all 33,212 checks passing, global p95 73.44 ms |
| `VUS` is the TOTAL user count, with the update and administration mixes carved OUT of it | Keep `VUS` as the read-mix count and add the other mixes on top | The earlier arrangement ran 150 read users PLUS 5 write users and reported `vus_max` 155, so the run did not hold the figure the target names — it overstated concurrency by 5 users while claiming to measure 150. Deriving the read count as `VUS - WRITE_VUS - ADMIN_VUS` makes `vus_max` exactly 150, which is the number the evidence claims | A caller who raises `WRITE_VUS` or `ADMIN_VUS` above `VUS` would starve the read mix; the derivation floors at 1 |
| Virtual users arrive spread over a `RAMP_MS` window (default 15 s) rather than all at once | Keep `constant-vus`' simultaneous start; use a `ramping-vus` executor | A `constant-vus` scenario starts every user in the same instant, which fires all 150 sign-ons simultaneously against the password encoder. Measured, that turned sign-on p95 into 916 ms — a measurement of a thundering herd, not of the endpoint, since an isolated sign-on completes in 89-106 ms. No population of operators arrives that way. Staggering by user index is the smallest change that makes the arrival pattern representative, and it keeps the steady-state concurrency exactly `VUS`, which a ramping executor would not | The ramp is a harness parameter, so a run with `RAMP_MS=0` reproduces the old artifact. Its purpose is documented at the constant so the figure is not mistaken for a capacity limit |
| `POST /users` at p95 280 ms is REPORTED as exceeding the 200 ms target rather than excused, excluded or tuned away | Lower the encoder's work factor; exclude the endpoint from the threshold set; drive it at a realistic provisioning rate and report only that | The target admits no carve-out, so the number is published with its cause identified rather than assumed: the three slowest endpoints are exactly the three that run the password encoder, all sharing a median of 88-90 ms; measured in isolation the endpoint completes in 85-91 ms and encodes EXACTLY ONCE (`UserService` line 413), so there is no duplicated hash and no N+1 query. The excess is contention between concurrent adaptive hashes, and that work factor is the property that makes the stored credential resistant to offline attack — the whole reason `COSGN00C`'s plaintext comparison was replaced (AAP 0.6.7). Lowering it would trade a security guarantee for a latency figure. The harness also over-drives this path deliberately, provisioning about two users per second where real administrator provisioning happens a handful of times a day | `POST /auth/signon` — the credential path every real user traverses, hashing just as expensively — stays inside budget at 197 ms, so the exceedance is confined to an administrator provisioning screen driven far above its real rate. Both figures are published |
| The account-update mix submits five CORRECTED customer fields instead of echoing the record unchanged | Echo the record verbatim and accept the rejection; skip ineligible accounts; drive the screen's edit path only | Measured against the shipped fixture, NO seeded account can be rewritten unchanged: all 50 are refused — 21 for a FICO score outside 300-850, 22 for a telephone area code absent from the North American lookup table, and the remainder for a state code or state/zip pair the cross-edit rejects. The seeded data is faithful to the legacy customer file, and that file carries values `COACTUPC` itself does not accept, so an operator arriving at any of these accounts must correct the flagged field before the rewrite is taken. Submitting the correction is that operator action, and it is what makes the successful write path — optimistic lock, dual write, one transaction — measurable at all. Echoing and accepting a `400` would have measured only the edit path | The correction MUTATES those five fields on the accounts the update mix owns — `WRITE_VUS` accounts, verified afterwards as exactly 5 — so a fixture-preserving run must re-seed. Verified after a full run: the security table returned to its seeded 10 rows with no harness user left behind, the confirmed add-transaction path appended 115 rows leaving the seeded 300 untouched, and NO batch job was launched |
| Bill payment and the report request are driven on their CONFIRMATION-GATE path, with a blank `confirm` flag | Drive both confirmed; omit both from the harness | This is not a shortcut but the first-ENTER behaviour of `COBIL00C` and `CORPT00C`, which redisplay the screen with a confirmation prompt and mutate nothing until the operator confirms — verified in the services (`ReportService.confirmAndLaunch` returns the prompt when `confirm` is empty and launches no job). It measures the full request path including the account read and balance computation, or the whole date-window validation, while keeping the run repeatable: a confirmed payment zeroes a balance and a confirmed report launches a Spring Batch job PER CALL, neither of which survives minutes at 150 users. Add-transaction, by contrast, IS driven confirmed, because that is where the sequence-backed id generation of AAP 0.6.5 lives | The confirmed branches of those two screens are therefore not covered by the latency measurement; they are covered by the service integration suites, and the batch jobs are timed separately |
| **SUPERSEDED (see section 40.5).** No rate-limit override is needed for a measurement run: the limiter now counts a SIGNED-ON caller per session and reserves per-address counting for callers that hold no session | Keep raising the committed budget for the duration of a run; leave the limit in force and measure whatever it permits; remove the limiter | The original arrangement keyed every request on `request.getRemoteAddr()` at 600 per minute, so a load generator on ONE host shared a single budget that 150 real users would hold 150 of — a 10-VU probe produced 99.68% `429`s, and measuring under it measured the limiter rather than the application. That was treated as a harness problem and worked around with an uncommitted overlay; it was in fact a PRODUCT defect, because 150 operators behind one NAT address share exactly the same single budget. The delivered design counts the session for a signed-on caller and the address only before a session exists, so per-address limiting still blunts credential stuffing where it matters | The workaround and its restoration check are retired. Measured on shipped configuration: 150 of 150 sign-ons from a single source address succeeded, a 150-session gateway workload produced ZERO `429`, and the abuse budget still trips above its limit |
| **REVISED (see section 40.2).** The "memory footprint increase under 10%" target is still recorded as UNVERIFIED against the mainframe, but the footprint itself is now DECLARED, ENFORCED and measured against its declaration | Assert a percentage on the strength of the container figures; omit the target silently; leave the footprint unbounded and report absolute numbers only | The target is a DELTA against the mainframe baseline and no z/OS region is available, so any percentage would be invented — that part of the original entry stands. What did NOT stand was its second half: it excused the figures on the grounds that "no container memory limit is set, so `-XX:MaxRAMPercentage=75` sizes the heap against the whole host and `heap_max` reads in terabytes", which described the defect as a measurement caveat instead of fixing it. Both topologies now declare a 1 GiB per-service limit, all nine images resolve one identical heap policy against it, and the measured working set is 462-618 MiB — 45-60% of the declaration | The 10% figure remains unverified and is stated as such. What replaces it is falsifiable: the published baseline table and its method (README-target.md) make a 10% delta computable against THIS release, and the ceiling is enforced by the container runtime rather than by convention |
| A job interrupted mid-chunk is left reporting `UNKNOWN` and is recovered by an operator, rather than being forced to `FAILED` | Give the metadata writes `PROPAGATION_REQUIRES_NEW`; catch and re-write the status in a listener | Traced into the Spring Batch 6.0.4 sources: `AbstractJobRepositoryFactoryBean` gives `create*` and `getLastJobExecution*` `REQUIRES_NEW` but everything else `REQUIRED`, and `ChunkOrientedStep.doExecute` updates the step's metadata only when the transaction is marked rollback-only — a writer exception that propagates bypasses that guard, so the final status write joins the doomed transaction and is rolled back with it. Forcing `REQUIRES_NEW` would deliberately break the atomicity between a chunk and its own bookkeeping, which is exactly the safeguard that prevents a chunk being counted as written when it was not. Correctness is preferred over a tidier status | The status is a documented operational characteristic with a working remedy: resubmitting the interrupted instance is refused loudly (`HTTP 400`, "already running") so no double-post is possible; marking it `ABANDONED` makes it non-restartable; marking it `FAILED` allows restart. Exactly-once is structural regardless: `tran_id` IS the feed's own `DALYTRAN-ID`, so a re-run of an identical feed is refused by `transactions_pkey` — verified, with the posted count unchanged at 1,000 and zero duplicate ids |
| The batch working directory stays ephemeral (`tmpfs` in Compose, `emptyDir` in Kubernetes) even though that makes a file-producing step non-restartable | Provision a PersistentVolumeClaim or named volume for batch output | Already recorded in section 9.5 for the storage choice itself; what this run established is the CONSEQUENCE. A restart after a mid-job kill was accepted by the job repository but then failed with `ItemStreamException: File is not writable`, because the reject file the step had been appending to was wiped with the tmpfs. So for a job that writes files, ephemeral storage means restart-from-last-committed-chunk is unavailable in the shipped topology, and the job must be re-run from the start | `k8s/deployment-transaction-service.yaml` states in place that a durable deployment substitutes a PersistentVolumeClaim for the `emptyDir`, which is what restores restartability |

### 39.9 Integration-harness and cross-cutting repairs

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| The account-service integration suites let the `test` profile's own Flyway provision the schema instead of driving migrations from a `@BeforeAll` hook | Keep the hook and disable profile Flyway; baseline the container | Two provisioning paths against one container disagree about the history table: the hook applied the scripts and then profile Flyway found a populated schema whose history it had not written. Letting the profile own it means the suites provision the schema exactly the way a deployed service does, which is also what makes the migration set itself part of what the tests exercise | Suites that need a table emptied must truncate rather than re-create it — see the `card_xref` entry in section 39.5 |
| `PiiEncryptionException` declares two constructors (message, and message-with-cause) rather than one | Keep the single constructor and wrap causes in the message string; use a generic runtime exception | Flattening a cause into a message discards the stack that identifies WHICH crypto operation failed, and the encryption chain has several. Preserving the cause keeps the diagnosis available while the message stays the operator-facing text | Both constructors are exercised by the crypto suites, so neither is dead |
| Rationale that had accumulated as multi-line "why" blocks inside source comments was MOVED into this log, leaving a one-line `:note:` pointer that names the section | Leave the blocks in place; delete them outright; duplicate them in both places | Rule 1 places rationale in the decision log, not in code comments, and Rule 4 requires docstrings to state purpose, output and parameters rather than carry commentary. Deleting would have DESTROYED the rationale, and duplicating guarantees drift — so each block moved and left a pointer. Where a pointer named a target that did not exist, the target was written rather than the pointer weakened: section 10.1's record-delimiter row exists because a pointer needed it | A pointer is only as good as its target, so every one is verified mechanically: 78 pointer sites were checked and the two whose parenthetical resolved to nothing now name a section NUMBER instead of a paraphrase. Section numbers are stable anchors; a renumbering would need the same sweep re-run |

## 40. QA Remediation — Card Screens (`COCRDLI` / `COCRDSL` / `COCRDUP`)

Decisions taken while resolving the card-screen findings of the runtime QA pass. Several of
them SUPERSEDE earlier entries in this log; each says so explicitly and names what changed.

### 40.1 INFOMSG is a field of its own, not the line-23 region

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| The card screens render their `INFOMSG` text in a NEUTRAL field of its OWN and never in the shared line-23 region: `COCRDLI` and `COCRDSL` paint it on the body row their map declares, and `COCRDUP` publishes it to the shell's separate `INFOMSG` row (the dedicated channel section 47.3 added, rendered NEUTRAL immediately above line 23) | Keep publishing it to line 23 as a green `role="status"` banner; drop the text altogether | A census of all seventeen mapsets shows that exactly five declare an `INFOMSG` field and every one of the five places it in the BODY: `COCRDLI` at `POS=(20,19) LENGTH=45`, `COCRDSL` and `COCRDUP` at `POS=(20,25) LENGTH=40`, `COACTVW` and `COACTUP` at `POS=(22,23)` — all `COLOR=NEUTRAL`. `ERRMSG` is a SEPARATE field at `POS=(23,1) COLOR=RED`. `3000-SEND-MAP` moves BOTH on every send, so the two are simultaneous outputs on different rows, not two variants of one line. Collapsing them into a single region forced a choice between them and made the row guidance disappear at the moment the operator had a row to act on | Two mechanisms now carry the same field: a body row on the two screens whose `INFOMSG` sits at row 20 with body rows beneath it, and the shell's own `INFOMSG` row on `COCRDUP`, `COACTVW` and `COACTUP` — where the field sits at row 20 or 22 with nothing but the message rows below. Both are NEUTRAL fields distinct from `ERRMSG`, so the decision this row records holds on all five; the residual difference is which row the text paints on, recorded in §40.5 `[app/bms/COCRDLI.bms; app/bms/COCRDSL.bms; app/bms/COCRDUP.bms; app/cbl/COCRDUPC.cbl:L1138-L1163]` |
| A browse that both found rows and hit a paging boundary shows its guidance AND its boundary banner | Suppress the info line whenever an error line is present | With the two on different rows the source has no reason to suppress either, and `1400-SETUP-MESSAGE` sets them independently. The old suppression is what made a successful one-row filter read as a red error | None `[app/cbl/COCRDLIC.cbl:L115-L116]` |

### 40.2 `COCRDUP` is a two-mode screen; validation waits for an AID

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| **SUPERSEDES the two earlier `CARDSID`-is-protected entries.** `ACCTSID` and `CARDSID` are entry fields whose protection follows the screen state: both enterable while no record has been fetched, both protected once one is displayed, and every field protected while the F5 confirmation is outstanding | Keep both keys permanently read-only, as previously logged | `3300-SETUP-SCREEN-ATTRS` moves `DFHBMFSE` (unprotected) over BOTH keys `WHEN CCUP-DETAILS-NOT-FETCHED` and `DFHBMPRF` over both `WHEN CCUP-SHOW-DETAILS` / `WHEN CCUP-CHANGES-NOT-OK`, and over ALL SIX fields `WHEN CCUP-CHANGES-OK-NOT-CONFIRMED` / `WHEN CCUP-CHANGES-OKAYED-AND-DONE`. The earlier entries recorded only the second of those three states and concluded the field was protected outright. That left the screen with no way to key a card at all, which is why a direct visit was a dead end. The wire concern the earlier entry raised — that editing the key would silently retarget the update — no longer applies: the key is submitted in the request body and the record is re-read before anything is written | A key typed over a displayed record cannot retarget an update, because the keys protect themselves as soon as a record is on the screen `[app/cbl/COCRDUPC.cbl:L1168-L1208; app/bms/COCRDUP.bms]` |
| A freshly entered `/cards/update` runs NO edit and shows NO error: it carries `PROMPT-FOR-SEARCH-KEYS` on row 20 with row 23 empty | Validate the routed key on mount, as delivered; carry the card number in the URL so a direct visit always has one | The fresh-entry branch of the source, `WHEN CCUP-DETAILS-NOT-FETCHED AND CDEMO-PGM-ENTER`, performs `3000-SEND-MAP` and returns WITHOUT performing `1000-PROCESS-INPUTS`, so neither `1100-RECEIVE-MAP` nor `1200-EDIT-MAP-INPUTS` runs. Only `WHEN OTHER` — an AID pressed on a screen already presented — reaches the edits. Putting the card number in the URL was rejected outright: it is the exact exposure §40.4 removes | None; a hand-over from the card list still reads immediately, through the `LIT-CCLISTPGM` branch `[app/cbl/COCRDUPC.cbl:L502-L512, L564-L568, L641-L661]` |
| `1230-EDIT-NAME` is implemented, first of the four detail edits, and the `NO-CHANGES-DETECTED` guard short-circuits them | Leave the name unvalidated and always run the edits | The name edit was absent altogether, so a blank or numeric embossed name reached the service. `1200-EDIT-MAP-INPUTS` compares `FUNCTION UPPER-CASE(CCUP-NEW-CARDDATA)` against the old snapshot and, when they match, sets the four detail flags VALID and returns BEFORE the edits — so an untouched screen reports the comparison rather than asking for a confirmation it has nothing to confirm | A test that pressed ENTER on an untouched screen expecting the confirmation prompt now correctly gets `No change detected with respect to values fetched.`; those tests were updated `[app/cbl/COCRDUPC.cbl:L680-L716, L761-L800]` |
| F12 re-reads the displayed record instead of navigating to the card-detail screen | Keep the forward navigation | `CCARD-AID-PFK12` is valid only `AND NOT CCUP-DETAILS-NOT-FETCHED`, and the program answers it by re-reading, so the operator SEES the stored values return with the `CCUP-SHOW-DETAILS` acknowledgement. Navigating away gave no acknowledgement that the edits had been abandoned | The key no longer leaves the screen; `F3=Exit` remains the way out `[app/cbl/COCRDUPC.cbl:L418, L954-L964]` |
| An HTTP 409 on the card rewrite re-displays the record rather than reporting a failed write | Report it as a write failure, as delivered | `2000-DECIDE-ACTION` answers `WHEN DATA-WAS-CHANGED-BEFORE-UPDATE` with `SET CCUP-SHOW-DETAILS TO TRUE`, and `3200-SETUP-SCREEN-VARS` repaints that state from the `CCUP-OLD-*` snapshot. A write failure is the separate `CCUP-CHANGES-FAILED` state, whose `INFOMSG` is `INFORM-FAILURE`. This now matches the account-update screen, which took the same correction | None; `F5=Save` is withdrawn until the operator reviews the conflict `[app/cbl/COCRDUPC.cbl:L988-L1001, L1108-L1116]` |

### 40.3 Only the literals the programs can actually emit

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| The mixed-case `SEARCHED-CARD-NOT-NUMERIC` ('Card number if supplied must be a 16 digit number') and `SEARCHED-ACCT-ZEROES` / `SEARCHED-ACCT-NOT-NUMERIC` ('Account number must be a non zero 11 digit number') literals are no longer emitted anywhere in the card feature — screen, controller or service | Keep them, on the grounds that they are declared in the source | They are declared as 88-levels in `COCRDSLC` and `COCRDUPC` and are **never SET by either program**: both `1220-EDIT-CARD` and `2220-EDIT-CARD` `MOVE` the upper-case `'CARD ID FILTER,IF SUPPLIED MUST BE A 16 DIGIT NUMBER'` directly, and the account edits `MOVE` `'ACCOUNT FILTER,IF SUPPLIED MUST BE A 11 DIGIT NUMBER'`. Emitting an unreachable 88-level is unrequested observable output. The absent-key case has its own reachable literal, `WS-PROMPT-FOR-CARD` = 'Card number not provided' | Tests that pinned the unreachable literals were updated to the reachable ones `[app/cbl/COCRDSLC.cbl:L148-L149, L685-L716; app/cbl/COCRDUPC.cbl:L193-L194, L762-L800]` |
| The length test lives in the `IS NOT NUMERIC` branch, not in a branch of its own | Give a short key its own message | `CC-ACCT-ID` is `PIC X(11)` and `CC-CARD-NUM` is `PIC X(16)`, so a short entry leaves trailing spaces in the fixed-width field and `IS NOT NUMERIC` rejects it through the same branch as a letter would take. The source has no separate length message | None |
| The invented empty-state row `No cards to display` is removed; an empty browse renders no body rows | Keep the row for screen-reader benefit | `COCRDLIC` paints seven blank row slots and reports `NO RECORDS FOUND FOR THIS SEARCH CONDITION.` on line 23, which the service already returns. The invented row was also the cause of the empty-state header reflow, because its `colSpan` collapsed the four column widths. The table is instead given an accessible name through `aria-labelledby`, which adds no visible output | An empty `<tbody>` relies on the line-23 message to tell the operator, exactly as the 3270 did `[app/cbl/COCRDLIC.cbl:L121-L122]` |

### 40.4 The card number leaves the URL (SUPERSEDES the two AAP-PRECEDENCE residuals)

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| **SUPERSEDES finding C‑08's "AAP PRECEDENCE" entry and its recorded RESIDUAL.** The card read is `POST /cards/detail` and the rewrite is `PUT /cards`, both carrying the card number in the request BODY. `GET`/`PUT /cards/{cardNumber}` no longer resolve | Keep the path segment and rely on log masking, as previously logged; mask the PAN in responses; introduce an opaque handle | The earlier entry concluded that closing this "requires an AAP amendment", citing AAP §0.6.7 and §0.7.6. On re-reading, neither freezes it. §0.6.7 enumerates the fields to MASK IN OUTPUT — a display concern, and the PAN's continued display on screen is unaffected by this change. §0.7.6 forbids changing "data formats or protocols for downstream systems" and "file-based interfaces requiring exact record layouts"; §0.2.2 names the downstream systems as payment networks, bank core systems and regulatory reporting. The SPA-to-gateway REST shape is none of those — it is an internal contract this migration created, and the AAP specifies no card endpoint URL at all (`PUT /accounts/{id}` appears only as an account example in §0.4.2). Log masking was a mitigation, not a fix: a URL is recorded verbatim by every intermediary on the path, and the residual was live — measured on the running stack, a request to the old shape still writes the full sixteen digits into the nginx access log. A request body is recorded by none of them. The read is a POST for that reason alone and remains side-effect free | The read is a POST, which is unusual for a retrieval; it is the standard shape for a sensitive lookup key. Pinned in both directions: a frontend contract test asserts no card number appears in any request URL or query string, and a controller test asserts the old path shapes return 404. The browse route keeps its `cardNumber` search PARAMETER — a filter the operator typed, which the legacy map also echoed, and which addresses no record `[app/cpy/CVACT02Y.cpy; AAP §0.2.2, §0.4.2, §0.6.7, §0.7.6]` |

### 40.5 Recorded, not changed

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| `COACTVW` and `COACTUP` publish their `INFOMSG` through the shell rather than as a body row, as `COCRDUP` does; section 47.3 then gave the shell a dedicated `INFOMSG` channel, so the text is rendered as its own NEUTRAL row above line 23 instead of as a green banner IN line 23, and both mapsets declare the field at `POS=(22,23) COLOR=NEUTRAL` | Move them to the body as `COCRDLI` and `COCRDSL` do | The QA pass examined this question across all seventeen mapsets and raised it for the card screens alone, explicitly finding the `/transactions` and `/users` body-note treatment faithful. The shared line-23 informational channel is consumed by sixteen pages, so relocating it is a shell-wide change rather than a screen fix, and the account screens' own findings were verified against the delivered green banner. Changing it here would exceed the finding set and re-open verified evidence | The two account screens' informational text renders one row lower than the map declares, and in green rather than neutral. Purely presentational; the text itself is byte-exact `[app/bms/COACTVW.bms; app/bms/COACTUP.bms]` |
| The twelve mapsets that declare no `INFOMSG` field keep their informational text on line 23 | Suppress it | With no second field to carry it, `ERRMSG` is the only message region those screens have, and the source writes its informational text there | None |

### 40.6 Both card search keys, the masking asymmetry, and paging with a changed filter

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| **SUPERSEDES the `ACCTSID`-is-optional entry in §11.4.** `/cards/view` requires BOTH keys — `2210-EDIT-ACCOUNT` then `2220-EDIT-CARD`, first message wins, then the unguarded both-blank test — while the HAND-OVER read from the card list runs no edit at all | Keep the account optional; run the edits on the hand-over too and require the list to supply an account | Two separate source facts settle this. First, both edits declare a "not supplied" branch (`CC-ACCT-ID EQUAL LOW-VALUES OR SPACES OR CC-ACCT-ID-N EQUAL ZEROS` → `SET INPUT-ERROR` + `WS-PROMPT-FOR-ACCT`), so a blank account is an error, not a widened search; the earlier entry's premise that the list carries no account is false in the delivered code, where `CardListPage` navigates with `{cardNumber, accountId}`. Second, the hand-over is exempt BY THE SOURCE: the entry branch `WHEN CDEMO-PGM-ENTER AND CDEMO-FROM-PROGRAM EQUAL LIT-CCLISTPGM` is commented "SELECTION CRITERIA ALREADY VALIDATED" and performs `SET INPUT-OK`, moves both keys out of the COMMAREA and goes straight to `9000-READ-DATA`, never reaching `2200-EDIT-MAP-INPUTS`. Only `CDEMO-PGM-REENTER` — an AID on a screen already presented — edits typed input. `/cards/update` already had this shape (§40.2); the two screens now agree because their programs do | A selection handed over with a malformed key reads instead of faulting; the service's own edit answers it (`400` with the same literal). Pinned by a test asserting a hand-over with no account still reads, and by three tests covering account-only, card-only and neither `[app/cbl/COCRDSLC.cbl:L336-L347, L615-L641]` |
| The card PAN stays UNMASKED in card list and card detail responses, and the asymmetry with SSN / government id / EFT is deliberate rather than an oversight | Mask the PAN in responses like the other three; return only the last four digits on the list | Recorded because the QA pass found the asymmetry uncovered: AAP §0.6.7 enumerates the fields to mask as SSN, government id and CVV, and the PAN is deliberately absent from that list. The mapsets settle why — `COCRDLI` paints a full sixteen-digit `CRDNUM` in every one of its seven data rows and `COCRDSL` paints `CARDSID` in full, so masking either would withhold output a frozen map displays, which §0.7.1 forbids. The three fields that ARE masked appear on `COACTVW` / `COACTUP`, where masking is what §0.6.7 requires. The asymmetry is therefore between what the AAP mandates and what it omits, not between two treatments of one class of data. What was genuinely uncovered — the PAN in the request PATH — is fixed, not accepted (§40.4) | The PAN reaches any client authorised to read the screen, as it reached any operator at the terminal. Compensating controls in force: mandatory authentication, TLS, `no-store`, no CVV in any response, and PAN masking on every log path `[AAP §0.6.7, §0.7.1; app/bms/COCRDLI.bms; app/bms/COCRDSL.bms]` |
| A paging key that carries a newly typed filter sends that filter with the page number of the page on display, so a card filter combined with page > 1 yields no rows until the operator presses ENTER | Restart the browse at page 1 whenever the filter text differs from the displayed page's; ignore un-submitted filter text on a paging key | Recorded rather than changed because the source does the same thing: `2000-RECEIVE-MAP` performs `2200-EDIT-INPUTS` on EVERY declared AID, so PF8 picks up the newly typed filter, and the PF8 branch then browses forward from `WS-CA-LAST-CARD-NUM` under `CA-NEXT-PAGE-EXISTS` with no reset — the new filter is applied to the old browse position. `9500-FILTER-RECORDS` is a per-record exclusion, so whether the legacy finds the filtered card depends on whether it sorts after the previous page's last key; under offset paging the equivalent statement is that a keyed read's single row lives on page 1. Restarting at page 1 would be the friendlier behaviour and is NOT what the program does. Measured on the running stack: the row count is otherwise a pure function of the current filters and page — a card on page 6 of the unfiltered browse is returned as the single row of page 1 when filtered, its account filter returns its one card, and an unfiltered page-1 request afterwards returns the original seven rows, so no search state is retained anywhere | The operator's next ENTER restores the result, because a fresh submit always re-drives the browse from `FIRST_PAGE`. No data is lost or mis-shown; the page indicator continues to report the page the service answered `[app/cbl/COCRDLIC.cbl:L355-L362, L486-L494, L1382-L1400]` |
| The blank-field `'*'` sentinel IS painted, in its own reserved character cell beside the field rather than inside it, and the `MOVE DFHRED` half is carried by the field's own frame | Write `*` into the input's value as the source does; or omit the marker and rely on the red frame alone | `IF FLG-ACCTFILTER-BLANK AND CDEMO-PGM-REENTER → MOVE '*' TO ACCTSIDO, MOVE DFHRED TO ACCTSIDC` marks a blank field two ways at once. Writing the `*` into the input's VALUE is the part with no modern analogue — it would overwrite what the operator is about to type — so it is painted in an adjacent one-cell slot (`.blankMarkerSlot`, `span.cardUpdate__marker`) that is always present and only lights its glyph, which cannot shift the field beside it. That reproduces what the 3270 achieved: the marker overwrote a column the map had already reserved. Both halves are now delivered on both card screens, and the two fault SHAPES stay distinct — a blank field takes marker AND colour, a rejected non-blank field takes colour ALONE, because it already holds the value that failed | The `*` sits one column left of the field instead of in its first column. No message, wire value or edit outcome differs; a lone `*` typed by the operator is still read as "not supplied", exactly as `2200-EDIT-MAP-INPUTS` does `[app/cbl/COCRDSLC.cbl:L541-L551; app/cbl/COCRDUPC.cbl:L589-L630]` |
| The cursor follows the source's `EVALUATE` while the fault marking follows its four independent `IF`s — the two rules are transcribed separately because the source states them separately | Drive both from one "first faulted field" value | The paragraph reddens fields with `IF FLG-ACCTFILTER-NOT-OK`, `IF FLG-CARDFILTER-NOT-OK`, `IF FLG-ACCTFILTER-BLANK AND CDEMO-PGM-REENTER` and `IF FLG-CARDFILTER-BLANK AND CDEMO-PGM-REENTER` — four unconditional tests, so a both-blank send marks BOTH controls — whereas the cursor is placed by a single `EVALUATE TRUE` whose first matching branch wins, so the same send puts the cursor on `ACCTSID` alone. Collapsing them into one value gets one of the two wrong: it had left the card-only fault marking `CARDSID` while the cursor stayed on `ACCTSID`, which browser measurement caught | None; three tests pin the pair (both-blank marks two controls, card-only cursors `CARDSID`, both-blank cursors `ACCTSID`) `[app/cbl/COCRDSLC.cbl:L515-L551]` |

## 41. QA Remediation — Transaction Screens (`COTRN00` / `COTRN01` / `COTRN02`)

Findings addressed: the transaction-list column behaviour, the stale detail fields, the two unreachable
cross-reference literals, the clipped confirmation caption, the silently dead ENTER, and the per-field
fault treatment on all three screens.

### 41.1 The transaction id leaves the URL path

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| The detail read is `GET /transactions/detail?tranId=<id>`; the id is a request PARAMETER, and `GET /transactions/{id}` no longer resolves | Keep the path segment and reject suspicious values in the controller; keep the path and encode the value harder on the client; make the read a POST as the card read is | `TRNIDIN` is `PIC X(16)` with `ATTRB=(FSET,IC,NORM,UNPROT)` — an operator may fill it with ANY sixteen characters, and the legacy `READ` simply misses for anything not on file. A path segment cannot carry such a value safely: an intermediary NORMALIZES the request URI before it matches a location, so `%2F` is decoded and the resulting `..` segments collapse. Measured on the running stack, `GET /api/transactions/..%2F..%2Fetc%2Fpasswd` stopped matching the `/api/` prefix, fell into the SPA history fallback and was answered `200 text/html` with the SPA document — a client waiting for JSON rendered nothing at all, which is the "completely silent dead ENTER" the QA pass reported. A query string is not path-normalized, so the value reaches the service verbatim: the same string now returns `404 application/json` carrying `Transaction ID NOT found...`, which is exactly what the legacy READ produces for a key not on file. A POST was rejected because, unlike the card PAN, a transaction id is neither a credential nor an account identifier and there is nothing to keep out of a log | The read's URL shape changed; no observable screen output did. Pinned in both directions: a frontend contract test asserts the id travels as a parameter and that a separator-bearing value never enters the path, and a controller test asserts the old path shape returns 404 `[app/bms/COTRN01.bms; app/cbl/COTRN01C.cbl; frontend/nginx.conf]` |
| `location /` additionally refuses any request whose RAW uri matches `%2f`, `%5c`, `../` or `..%2f`, with HTTP 400 | Rely on the query-parameter move alone; rewrite such requests to the API prefix; return 404 | The parameter move fixes the one endpoint; the normalisation behaviour belongs to every path the SPA fallback can absorb, so the guard is placed where the mis-routing actually lands. No CardDemo URL contains a traversal segment or an encoded separator — every identifier on the wire is digits — so refusing outright cannot reject legitimate traffic, and refusing beats silently answering with the wrong content type. The guard reads `$request_uri` (the original, un-normalized URI) because that is the only place the encoded form survives, and it sits in `location /` ONLY, so `/api/` requests never reach it and a query VALUE containing a slash still passes through untouched | A raw-URI pattern could over-match. Verified against every legitimate route and asset on the running stack — 14 URLs including all seven SPA routes, both hashed bundles, `/api/session`, `/favicon.ico` and a query-bearing route — all 200; and two `/api/` requests carrying slashes in their query VALUES were answered by the service's own edit, not the guard `[frontend/nginx.conf]` |

### 41.2 Key resolution is its own step, because the paragraph order requires it

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
