Rendering all ten overflowed the declared field and pushed the column that follows it | A ZIP+4 stored in the record is displayed as its five-digit prefix, which is what a 3270 operator saw. QA independently re-derived this as faithful and retracted it as a reported defect |
| The entry fields of a screen are NOT natively disabled while a READ is in flight — only while a SAVE is | Disable every field on every request; disable none | The distinction is what the two operations do to what is on screen. A save leaves the screen's own values in place and must not let them be edited while it commits; a read is going to REPLACE them, and disabling a field mid-read moves focus to the document body for the duration. Inhibition during a read is carried by the OIA, `aria-busy` on the body and the disabled AID keys, which is what the finding asked for | A keystroke typed during an in-flight read is overwritten by the arriving record. Recorded rather than changed: the list screens DO disable, because their entry field is the browse key the reply is positioned on. Verified across an 82-sample throttled window: `X SYSTEM`, `aria-busy="true"` and every legend key disabled in **100%** of samples |
| The `CSLKPCDY` validation tables are transcribed verbatim, INCLUDING an internal inconsistency, with the consequence that no seeded account can be saved without first correcting other fields | Reconcile the tables so they agree; relax an edit; correct the seeded fixture | `CSLKPCDY.cpy:L1013` `88 VALID-US-STATE-CODE` has 56 members and does NOT include `AA`, `AE`, `AP`, `FM`, `MH` or `PW`, yet `CSLKPCDY.cpy:L1073` `88 VALID-US-STATE-ZIP-CD2-COMBO` DOES contain combos beginning with those six (`'AP96'` at `L1087`). A customer in `AP` therefore passes the combination edit and is refused by the state-code edit alone. Reconciling either table would alter a legacy validation table, and correcting the fixture would alter seeded data — both forbidden by AAP §0.7.1 and §0.7.6 | An operator cannot save any seeded account without first correcting the FICO score, the ZIP and one or both telephone area codes. Census over all 50 seeded customers: FICO in range 29/50, state code valid 45/50, state+ZIP2 combination valid **2/50**, both area codes assigned 15/50, saveable as-is **0/50**. The transcription reproduces the inconsistency member for member — 56 states, 240 combinations, `'AP'` absent from the states and `'AP96'` present in the combinations |

### 44.6 The account screens — the field-fault contract and where each edit runs

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| A rejected edit names the request member it faulted: `FieldValidationException` carries an ordered list of members and the handler copies every one into `ErrorResponse.fieldErrors` | Return the message alone and let the client guess which field it named; encode the field in the message text | The legacy edit and the screen are the same program, so `MOVE -1 TO <field>L` and the red attribute are free. Split across a wire they are not: with only a message, the screen could not mark or focus the field the server refused, so the operator read `State: is not a valid state code` beside twenty-six unmarked fields. The list is ORDERED with the cursor field first, because the source's cursor rule is "first faulted field in screen order" and one edit (`1280-EDIT-US-STATE-ZIP-CD`) faults TWO fields at once | The member names are a contract between the DTO and the screen's field table, kept as an explicit 27-entry map rather than inferred. `ErrorResponse.fieldErrors` and the client type already existed, so no new wire member was invented. Verified at the wire for six distinct edits, including the two-field case returning `{custAddrStateCd, custAddrZip}` with state first |
| Every edit runs on the server; the client additionally reproduces the edits it can decide alone, and the server is authoritative | Validate only on the server; validate only on the client; split the edits so each runs in one place | The legacy edits run in the same task as the send, so a refused value never becomes a file access — reproducing that client-side is what keeps a rejected FICO score or a blank required field from becoming a request at all, and gives the operator the program's own message immediately. Server-side validation is not optional, because the client is not a trust boundary. The edits the client does NOT reproduce are the ones needing data it does not hold: the state and ZIP tables and the masked-identifier comparison, which requires the stored record | Two implementations of the same edit must publish the same literal; each cites the paragraph it came from and each is pinned by a test on both sides. Verified: the client FICO edit produces its message with ZERO account requests, while the server rejections arrive with `fieldErrors` and fault the same fields |
| A blank account group id is stored as NULL, not as an empty string | Store the empty string the field submits; reject a blank | `ACCT-GROUP-ID` is a `PIC X(10)` field that is genuinely optional, and a read/save round trip must not turn a stored NULL into `''`: the two are the same to the screen and different to the database, so an operator who saved an unrelated field would silently rewrite the column. Normalising at the mapper boundary makes the round trip idempotent | An empty string can no longer be stored deliberately, which no screen offers. Verified: a save with the field blank returns 200 with the member null and SQL confirms `acct_group_id IS NULL` |
| A masked identifier is accepted on save only when it is the mask OF THE STORED VALUE; any other mask-shaped input is refused | Accept anything mask-shaped and leave the stored value alone; refuse every mask-shaped value; return the unmasked value so the round trip is trivial | The screen receives SSN, government id and EFT account id already masked, so the operator submits the mask back unchanged on a save that did not touch them — the value must round-trip. But accepting anything mask-shaped let a DIFFERENT mask silently preserve the stored value, so `***-**-9999` submitted against a record ending `3888` reported success while changing nothing. Comparing against the stored value's own mask distinguishes "unchanged" from "wrong", and returning the unmasked value is not an option because the masking exists to keep it off the wire | The comparison needs the stored record, so it runs after the read as a distinct validation step rather than in the stateless validator. Verified: a mask-shaped SSN that is not this record's mask returns 400 `SSN: First 3 chars must be all numeric.` with `fieldErrors`, and an unchanged mask round-trips through a successful save |
| An optimistic-lock conflict is a DISPLAY state, not a failure state: the screen repaints the values it originally read, restores the information line to `'Update account details presented above.'`, darkens `F5` and leaves `F12` live | Treat the 409 as a failure and leave the typed values on screen; force a re-read automatically; navigate away | `2000-DECIDE-ACTION:L2611` reads `WHEN DATA-WAS-CHANGED-BEFORE-UPDATE SET ACUP-SHOW-DETAILS TO TRUE` — the source takes the screen BACK to the display state. `9700` does not re-anchor `ACUP-OLD-*`; only `9000-READ-ACCT` through `9500-STORE-FETCHED-DATA` does, so the repaint shows the snapshot this screen read, not the value the other writer stored. Nothing was written and no field is at fault, so no field is marked. `F12` is accepted whenever the filter is valid (`L2570`) even with a darkened legend, which is how the operator re-reads | The operator's typed values are discarded by the repaint, which is what the source does. Verified end to end: the conflict banner appears, the field repaints to this session's own snapshot (7777.00 — not the typed 9999.00 and not the other writer's 8888.00), no field carries `aria-invalid`, `F12` works from the keyboard despite its dark legend and pulls the newer record, and the retry then succeeds. Version ledger 0 -> 1 -> 2 -> 3 -> 4 |
| **SUPERSEDED by §47.10, which gave the shell a SECOND region for the separate `INFOMSG` field.** The shell rendered ONE line-23 region carrying both channels, with the error text taking precedence over the informational text | Render the informational line at its declared row 22 and the error line at row 23, as two regions — which is what §47.10 then did | The mapsets that paint their information line as a BODY field do get one — `COCRDLI`'s and `COCRDSL`'s `INFOMSG` are rendered in the body at their declared row and column (§40.1); `COCRDUP`, `COACTVW` and `COACTUP` publish theirs through the shell, which since §47.10 renders it as its own NEUTRAL row above line 23 rather than in line 23. For the screens whose mapsets declare no `INFOMSG` field at all the two channels are collapsed, which is the AAP's own model of the shell (`ErrorBanner` is the line-23 component) and is how the geometry contract was verified across all 17 screens. The consequence is recorded rather than hidden: `'Changes unsuccessful. Please try again'` is published on the INFORMATIONAL channel, so when the failure also carried a message from the service the message is what the operator reads, and the failure line is seen alone only when the failure carried no text of its own | `COACTUP` declares `INFOMSG` at `POS=(22,23) COLOR=NEUTRAL LENGTH=45` and `ERRMSG` at `POS=(23,1) COLOR=RED LENGTH=78`. Under this row an informational line rendered one row lower than declared and only while no error was present; §47.10 removed both consequences for the five screens that declare the field, and the collapse now applies only to the twelve that do not. No message is ever LOST: the two channels have distinct roles (`status` and `alert`) and distinct colours, and the precedence matches the source, which paints `ERRMSG` bright red over a neutral information line |

### 44.7 A key field's declared width is EXACT, and the literals that rule retired

The single most consequential source fact of this remediation, and the one that decided six separate
findings across three services: every account, card and transaction key edit in the legacy is a COBOL class
test — `IF CC-ACCT-ID IS NOT NUMERIC` — applied to an ALPHANUMERIC item. Such a test is true only when
EVERY character of the item is a digit, and BMS blank-pads the positions the operator did not type. A key
field declared `LENGTH=11` therefore accepts exactly eleven digits and refuses anything shorter, because
what arrives is digits followed by spaces. The comment above each of those tests in the source says so
outright: `*    Not numeric` / `*    Not 11 characters`.

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| Account keys require exactly 11 ASCII digits and a non-zero value; card keys exactly 16; the transaction-list filter exactly 16 | Accept one to eleven (and one to sixteen) digits and zero-pad, as the services did before | Accepting a short value let `1` and `01` resolve the SAME record as `00000000001`, and `1000683580` resolve the record stored as `0000001000683580` — an identifier with several spellings, which no browse key may have. `CC-ACCT-ID` is `PIC X(11)` with `CC-ACCT-ID-N REDEFINES ... PIC 9(11)` (`app/cpy/CVCRD01Y.cpy:L34,L36`), and the redefinition is read only AFTER the class test passes, so the eleven-digit run is a precondition of the numeric interpretation rather than a formatting nicety | An integration that previously sent an unpadded id now receives the screen's own refusal instead of a record. `\d` is deliberate rather than a `Character.isDigit` loop, because the regex class is ASCII-only: a full-width Unicode digit cannot pass it and then be normalised by `Long.valueOf`. Verified at the wire on all three keys, in both the accepting and the refusing direction |
| The card rule is enforced at the CONTROLLER's type boundary rather than in the service | Enforce it in the service beside the other card edits | `CardService` takes the filter as a `Long`, and a `Long` cannot tell `1` from `00000000001` — by the time the value reaches the service the evidence the edit needs has been destroyed. The boundary that parses the text is the only place the rule can be applied | The rule lives away from the other card edits, so the parse method carries the derivation in its own documentation. Verified: `?accountId=1` returns the `COCRDSLC` literal, `?accountId=00000000011` returns the card |
| The account edit publishes DIFFERENT text on the two verbs — `GET` the `COACTVWC` filter literal, `PUT` the `COACTUPC` number literal | Keep one shared constant for both, as before | The two screens genuinely publish different text for the same edit, so one constant could not be right for both: `COACTVWC:L672` reads `'Account Filter must  be a non-zero 11 digit number'` (with the double space after `must` and the hyphen in `non-zero` preserved) while `COACTUPC:L1806-L1809` assembles `'Account Number if supplied must be a 11 digit'` + `' Non-Zero Number'` by `STRING`. The read endpoint serves the view screen and the write endpoint the update screen, so the verb selects the screen and therefore the literal | Two constants where there was one, each cited to its program and line. Verified: `GET /accounts/1` and `PUT /accounts/1` now answer with their own screen's text |
| The text previously served — `'Account number must be a non zero 11 digit number'` and `'Card number if supplied must be a 16 digit number'` — is retired, because no program can emit either | Keep them as the friendlier wording | Both are DEAD 88-level condition names: `SEARCHED-ACCT-ZEROES` / `SEARCHED-ACCT-NOT-NUMERIC` are declared at `COACTVWC:L125/L127`, `COACTUPC:L493/L495`, `COCRDSLC:L144/L146` and `COCRDUPC:L189/L191`, and `SEARCHED-CARD-NOT-NUMERIC` at `COCRDSLC:L149` and `COCRDUPC:L194` — and a census over all four programs found each name appearing ONLY on its own declaration line. Nothing ever `SET`s them, so the text has never been on a screen. Serving it was invented observable output | Thirteen call sites carried the retired account text and were changed together, including three frontend pages, two frontend test files and two Java test files. Verified: neither literal appears in any response body or in the served bundle, and each endpoint now answers with a literal traced to a `MOVE` |
| The transaction DETAIL read applies NO width edit at all; a key of the wrong width simply misses | Apply the same 16-digit edit as the list filter | The two paths differ in the source and the difference is deliberate. `COTRN00C:L206-L219` edits the LIST filter — blank becomes `LOW-VALUES`, `IF TRNIDINI IS NUMERIC` uses it as a start key, otherwise `'Tran ID must be Numeric ...'`. `COTRN01C.PROCESS-ENTER-KEY` edits only for EMPTINESS, then moves `TRNIDINI` to `TRAN-ID` (both `PIC X(16)`) and READs. A short key on the detail screen is therefore NOT FOUND rather than refused | An operator who types a short id on the detail screen reads `'Transaction ID NOT found...'` rather than a width message, which is what the source does. Verified: an unpadded id returns 404 where it previously returned the padded record, and an id past `Long.MAX_VALUE` still returns 404 rather than a 500 |
| The `pad16` helper was DELETED rather than narrowed | Keep it for the paging cursors and the redisplay path | Five call sites shared it, and the paging cursors are the reason it had to go entirely: a cursor is a value the service itself minted and handed to the screen, so widening it on the way back in would re-introduce exactly the ambiguity the edit removes, and it would do so on a path with no operator input to blame. Every value is now used at the width it arrived in | Seven tests pinned the padding behaviour and were rewritten to pin its absence, two of them replaced outright: an over-wide filter test became a wrong-width refusal test asserting the literal AND that the repository is never touched, and a normalisation test became one asserting 404 with a lookup on the unpadded key and never on the padded one |

### 44.8 One refusal, one shape: the removed bean-validation width constraints

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| `TransactionAddRequestDto.acctId` / `.tranCardNum` and both members of `TransactionKeyDto` carry NO `@Size` constraint; the service's uniform edit answers every width | Keep the constraints and align their messages with the service's | The constraint fired only for an OVER-wide value, and it answered in a different SHAPE from every other wrong width: a `fieldErrors` entry from the bean-validation handler, where a short or non-numeric value produced a bare message from the service. The same refusal arriving in two shapes depending on which side of the width the value fell is a contract a client cannot code against. It also carried the dead 88-level text (§44.7), so the over-wide case published a literal no screen can emit | The width rule now exists in exactly one place per key, in the service, and each DTO member carries a `:note:` saying so and naming the paragraph. A test pins that the key endpoint applies no width constraint of its own, asserting that an over-wide and a short value produce the identical message with `fieldErrors: null`. Verified at the wire for both directions on both members |
| The remaining `@Size` constraints on `TransactionListRequestDto` publish the reachable `'Tran ID must be Numeric ...'` instead of the invented `'Tran ID must be at most 16 characters'` | Remove these too; keep the invented wording | These constraints guard members the SERVICE does not re-edit — the paging bounds and the selection id — so removing them would leave the width unguarded rather than guarded once. Retargeting the message keeps the guard and puts the source's own literal on it | The message no longer describes the constraint that produced it in the over-width case, which is the trade the spec-literal rule requires: the operator reads text `COTRN00C:L214` can emit |

### 44.9 No submit control in the screen body — the policy is now uniform

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| The three remaining in-body submit controls — `Sign-on` on `/signon`, `Fetch` and `Delete` on `/users/delete` — were REMOVED, bringing the application into line with the policy §16 already recorded | Amend the policy and keep the buttons; keep them but visually hide them | The policy is right and the code was wrong: a 3270 screen has exactly one ENTER affordance and it lives on line 24, which the shell renders and which also binds the physical key, so a second control duplicates the legend as unrequested observable output. The report also measured the consequence — on `/signon` the button sat at x=7.19 while its fields were at x=208.81, a 201.6px disconnect, because the button belonged to no declared column. Amending the policy was rejected because fourteen screens already follow it and the mapsets support it | Two label constants and one CSS rule became dead and were removed with them. Every affected test now drives the legend key instead, and the sign-on assertion was inverted to pin ZERO buttons inside `main`. Verified on both routes: `main` contains no `button`, no `input` of type submit/button/image, no `[role="button"]` and no `a[href]`; `form.querySelector('button, input[type=submit], input[type=image]')` is null; the physical ENTER still signs on (200) and still fetches (200); and the geometry closed up to exactly one blank terminal row rather than leaving a gap |

### 44.10 Security headers are set once, by whichever server owns the response

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| The static-response security headers are declared in the three static `location` blocks and are deliberately absent from the `server` block and from `location /api/`, where the gateway's own stricter set is passed through untouched | Keep the server-level block and let `/api/` inherit it; strip the gateway's headers at the proxy instead | Both servers were setting the same five headers, so an API response carried `X-Frame-Options: DENY, DENY`, `X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff, nosniff`, `Referrer-Policy: no-referrer, no-referrer` and TWO `Permissions-Policy` headers listing DIFFERENT directive sets. Duplication does not double the protection, it weakens it: a duplicated `X-Frame-Options` may be treated as conflicting and ignored outright, and two `Permissions-Policy` headers merge into one ambiguous list. The gateway's set is the stricter of the two (`default-src 'none'`, eight directives) and it is the server that knows what an API response needs, so it keeps ownership. nginx `add_header` does not merge across levels — a location declaring any header inherits none — which is why the set is repeated in each static location rather than declared once above them | The five headers appear in four places and must stay in step; the file header explains the model and each location says why it declares or omits them. Verified THREE independent ways — DevTools, the page's own `fetch()` through the Headers API (where a duplicate would comma-join) and the raw bytes of a hand-written HTTP/1.1 request — with a count of exactly one for each of the five on an API response, on the SPA document and on `/index.html` |

### 44.11 Rows this pass supersedes, and findings settled by census rather than by change

The report raised three findings against these documents themselves. Two are recorded above; the third and
the two stale rows are settled here.

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| **SUPERSEDES the `<hr class="neutral">` entry in §18.** The `X(70)` rule is rendered as the seventy literal hyphen characters the mapset declares, on every screen that paints one | Keep `<hr>` as the semantic thematic break; add an `hr` rule to the shared stylesheet | The earlier entry chose `<hr>` for its semantics and did not add a rule for it, and the omission was fatal: with no author rule the browser's `margin-inline: auto` applied, and inside a flex column an auto cross-axis margin SUPPRESSES stretching — so the element rendered as a 2.000 x 2.000px dot at the centre of the row, which the visual scan had been reporting as a stray `.` glyph. The literal run is also what the sibling screens already did, so the codebase disagreed with itself. Seventy characters is the mapset's declared length, and rendering them makes the rule occupy exactly seventy of the eighty terminal columns by construction rather than by a stylesheet rule that could drift | The run is decorative to assistive technology and is marked `aria-hidden`. Verified: `querySelectorAll('hr').length === 0`, `textContent.length === 70` of pure hyphens, ink 672.11px = 70 x 9.6094px inside the 768.14px frame |
| **REAFFIRMS the no-in-body-submit-control entry in §16**, which the application now follows on all 17 screens | Weaken it to permit a button where a screen has no other affordance | The policy was contradicted by three rendered controls, not by anything in the source. Removing them (§44.9) is what makes the row true, and every screen retains a submit path through the shell's line-24 ENTER binding | Verified route by route: no screen lost its only submit path |
| The `DFHRED` census that decides where a rejected field may be reddened is now complete for all seventeen screens, and `COSGN00C`, `COMEN01C` and `COADM01C` join the majority that never redden one | Apply the `.fieldError` treatment on those three for consistency with the account and card screens | All three programs contain ZERO `MOVE DFHRED` and ZERO `MOVE '*'`, so on the sign-on and the two menu screens a rejected entry is marked by the cursor and the line-23 message alone. Only four programs ever redden a data field — `COCRDUPC` (12), `COCRDSLC` (4), `COACTUPC` (3) and `COACTVWC` (2, plus one `MOVE '*'`) — and `COUSR02C`'s single `DFHRED` colours the MESSAGE line, not a field | A red frame appears on four screens and not on the other thirteen, which is what the seventeen mapsets declare. `aria-invalid` is emitted on all seventeen because it is not visible output and has no BMS analogue to contradict. Verified: `.fieldError` count zero on every rejection path of the thirteen |
| The card PAN's masking asymmetry, its removal from every request URL, and the substitution of a database sequence for the browse-last-then-increment transaction id remain recorded where they were first decided, at §40.4, §40.6 and §2 | Restate them here | Both were among the deviations the report listed as uncovered; both were in fact covered — the PAN rows were added when the card screens were remediated, and the sequence substitution has been in §1 since the migration was planned. They are cross-referenced here so a reader auditing the report's list finds them | None |

## 45. Cross-Cutting Regression Sweep — Defects The Per-Screen Passes Did Not Reach

Three defects were found by sweeping the shared-shell and shared-stylesheet changes across every route
rather than only the routes whose findings prompted them. Each is recorded here because each was a real
inconsistency between screens that no per-screen check could have surfaced.

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| The administrator menu no longer intercepts ENTER itself; the key routes through the shared shell as it does on the other sixteen screens | Keep the page-local `onKeyDown`, which called `preventDefault` and `stopPropagation` and invoked the submit directly | The local handler cut propagation at the React root, so ENTER on that ONE screen never reached the shell — and three shared rules ride on the shell's binding: the synchronous per-AID latch that discards a repeat struck inside one task, the send counter that REMOUNTS the row-23 live region so a repeated identical refusal is announced again, and the cursor re-placement. The screen was therefore the only one of seventeen where two same-task ENTERs sent twice and where a second identical refusal was silent to a screen reader. It already published an ENTER activator to the shell, so the local handler was a redundant second path whose only effect was to defeat those rules. The near-identical main menu never had one | Verified against the main menu as a control, and every compared number matched: two dispatches 0.7ms apart in one task produce exactly ONE select request on both; the second identical refusal REMOVES the alert node and ADDS a new one with the same text (zero `characterData` mutations) on both; the cursor returns to the option field on both. The rendered lock also now reaches the key — 808 samples across two throttled ~2s windows showed `X SYSTEM` and every legend key disabled in **100%**. Pinned by a test asserting one send for two same-task presses |
| The card browse table is laid out from `COCRDLI`'s own column rules — `table-layout: fixed`, a `<colgroup>` of 8/17/17 character cells and a 52-cell floor — as the other two browse screens already were | Leave the content-driven auto layout, which was passing every check | The mapset's row-10 hyphen runs (6, 15, 15, 8) ARE the column widths on a 3270, and this table was deriving them from its data instead: its Card Number column measured 17.0 cells because the value is sixteen characters, not because the mapset says 15. It passed only incidentally, through `white-space: nowrap` giving the auto algorithm a minimum content width — and at the narrowest tier it cleared its container by **0.05 of a character cell**, so any change to the data, a caption or the font metrics would have flipped a column into a squeeze with no floor to catch it. The two sibling screens had already been given the floor, so the codebase disagreed with itself about its own rule | The table now exceeds a 375px viewport where it previously just fit, and the shortfall becomes scroll range in the shared keyboard-operable container. Verified at 1440/768/375/320: `table-layout: fixed`, the floor measures 51.993–51.997 cells, the colgroup is exactly 8/17/17/none, the rendered rule cells are 8.00/17.00/17.00 cells, the page never scrolls horizontally, and at maximum scroll the `Active` header and its data are fully inside with at least 24px to spare |
| The transaction-view rule is hidden from assistive technology and carries no bottom margin | Leave both as they were | Both were omissions rather than decisions. The rule is the only one of the five rendered as a paragraph, so it alone kept the browser's `<p>` bottom margin — pushing the row beneath it 16px off the terminal grid, a pitch no other screen has. And it was the only one of the five NOT marked `aria-hidden`, so a screen reader read seventy hyphens aloud from a decorative glyph run. The margin is zeroed on the shared rule group rather than on the one screen, so the rule occupies exactly its one row everywhere | Verified: `aria-hidden="true"`, `margin-bottom` 0 with the following row's top EXACTLY on the rule's bottom (delta 0), the rule still 70 hyphens in NEUTRAL, and a verbose accessibility snapshot containing no run of even two hyphens. Pinned by a test on the rule's length, character set and `aria-hidden` |

### 45.1 Test expectations the exact-width key rule superseded

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| Six integration-test classes now address a record by its FULL key rather than by its bare number, and the account update path asserts its OWN screen's literal | Relax the width rule so the existing tests keep passing; assert whichever literal the shared constant happened to hold | The tests encoded the behaviour the exact-width rule replaced (§44.7): they fetched `/accounts/1` and filtered `?accountId=1`, which is precisely the unpadded spelling that let one record answer to several identifiers. A test that pins a superseded value is not evidence, so the tests were aligned to the rule rather than the rule to the tests. Each class gained a documented `acctKey` helper stating the class-test derivation. The account update case additionally became parameterized over the over-wide, non-numeric, all-zeros and unpadded spellings, all four now answering with the `COACTUPC` literal | These are the module suites that guard the endpoints, so a future relaxation of the width rule now fails them by design. The card-list invalid-filter case was likewise widened from one over-wide value to over-wide, unpadded and non-numeric. Verified: the full reactor builds and every module suite passes, unit and integration together |

## 46. QA-Remediation Deviations (final runtime QA checkpoint)

Decisions taken while resolving the 28 findings of the final runtime QA report. The report was written against an ancestor commit, so several findings were already closed by intervening work; only the decisions that changed observable behaviour, invented a literal, or departed from a finding's suggested fix are recorded here.

### 46.1 Credential contract and user administration

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| The user-id upper-casing rule is centralised in ONE shared `UserIdNormalizer` that six call sites now delegate to, rather than fixing only the write path the finding named | Add `toUpperCase` to `UserService` alone, as the finding suggested | The rule was reimplemented SIX times across four modules and the copies had already drifted: `AuthenticationService` and `LoginAttemptService` trimmed and upper-cased, `UserDetailsServiceImpl` and `SignonMapper` upper-cased WITHOUT trimming, `SessionPrincipalIndex` did both, and `UserService` trimmed but never upper-cased at all — which is the finding. Patching one copy would have left five, and the drift itself is the defect: a lookup key that disagrees with the key a row was written under. `Locale.ROOT` is load-bearing, not decoration — under a Turkish default locale `"admin001".toUpperCase()` yields `"ADMİN001"` and no row would ever match | `MenuController.resolveAid()` was deliberately left alone: it normalises an action key, not a user id, so folding it in would have coupled two unrelated vocabularies |
| The password is OPTIONAL on update, which departs from `COUSR02C`'s five-field emptiness edit | Keep the mandatory-password edit for byte-fidelity with `COUSR02C` | The legacy screen can demand the password because the 3270 map redisplays whatever the operator typed; a REST update route cannot, because it never returns the stored credential, so the field is unavoidably blank when a caller re-submits. Keeping the edit made a profile-only change IMPOSSIBLE — the finding's actual symptom. An absent or blank password now leaves `sec_usr_pwd` untouched and a supplied one is re-encoded, so the legacy capability is preserved and a new one is not added | The guard `passwordSupplied &&` is essential and easy to get wrong: `matches("", hash)` returns false, so comparing without it would have re-encoded the BLANK as the new credential and locked the user out. Both branches are pinned by tests, and the request body is asserted to OMIT the key rather than send `""` |
| The Add, Update and Delete screens build their confirmation banner from the SERVER response instead of the text the operator typed | Leave the client-side echo; upper-case in the browser too | The service upper-cases the id, so echoing the entry reported an id that was not the one stored — a lowercase add showed `User qac00011 has been added ...` while the row was `QAC00011`. That made a CORRECT normalisation indistinguishable from a broken one at the only place an operator looks. Upper-casing in the browser as well would duplicate the rule in a second language and invite exactly the drift 46.1 row 1 removes; `UserWriteResponseDto` already carries the verbatim legacy banner for this purpose and was simply being discarded | The delete route returns `204` with no body, so its banner names the id from the record fetched for confirmation; that fallback is exercised by deleting with a lowercase-typed id and observing the upper-cased banner |

### 46.2 Validation fidelity, precision and message reachability

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| The account money edit accepts TEN integer digits, not nine | Leave the nine-digit cap | `CVACT01Y` declares all five amount fields `PIC S9(10)V99` and the column is `NUMERIC(12,2)`, so a nine-digit cap refused values the record layout and the schema both admit. AAP 0.6.1 forbids altering COBOL precision, and under-accepting is as much an alteration as over-accepting | The bound is now a named constant derived from the picture clause rather than a literal, so the two cannot drift apart |
| The record read stays AFTER the field edits, and was hoisted only into the no-change branch | Read the record first for the whole update, as the finding's wording suggests | `COACTUPC` performs `1200-EDIT-MAP-INPUTS` at L1028 and `9000-READ-ACCT` only at L2575: the edits genuinely precede the read, and an existing test pinned that order. The finding's real symptom is narrower — the no-change comparison ran against a record that had never been read, so a missing account could not report `404`. Hoisting the read wholesale broke the ordering test, and checking the source proved the TEST was right and the broader fix wrong | A body that fails an edit still reports the edit rather than `404`, which is the legacy order; the `404` is reachable only with a body that passes every edit, and is verified that way |
| Date of Birth rejects today as well as a future date | Reject only dates strictly after today | `CSUTLDPY`'s `EDIT-DATE-OF-BIRTH` compares with a strict `>`, so the legacy screen refuses today too. The literal is reproduced byte-for-byte including its leading colon and trailing space: `Date of Birth:cannot be in the future ` | None; both the future date and today are asserted |
| A wrong-typed amount reports the legacy field message through a new `TypeMismatchMessageResolver` seam rather than the generic unreadable-body text | Parse the type mismatch inside the shared handler; accept "Malformed request body" | Jackson fails to BIND `"ABC"` into a `BigDecimal` before any validator runs, so the legacy message was unreachable by construction. Teaching the shared handler about account field names would put screen vocabulary in a cross-cutting component; an injected resolver keeps each frozen literal defined exactly once, in the validator that owns it, and derives the `old*` counterparts from the same table | The resolver is wired by SETTER injection with `required = false`, deliberately: `new GlobalExceptionHandler()` is constructed directly in many unit tests, and constructor injection would have broken all of them |
| `MSG_EXPIRY_DATE_INVALID` — "Card expiry date is not a valid calendar date" — is an INVENTED literal with no legacy counterpart | Reuse a legacy month/year message; accept the impossible date | `COCRDUPC` edits the month and year separately and never composes them, so a day-of-month impossibility such as `2026-02-30` has no legacy message to reproduce — the calendar check itself is new, added for consistency with the account date validator that already rejects it. The edit is ordered LAST so every legacy month/year literal keeps precedence and no existing message becomes unreachable | This is the one caller-visible string in this checkpoint with no legacy origin, recorded here explicitly under the spec-literal rule. A byte-fidelity audit against the mainframe will find it and should find this row |
| An unchanged card update now reports the legacy no-change message and answers `400`, where it previously answered `200` with no message | Leave the silent `200` | `COCRDUPC:188` carries `'No change detected with respect to values fetched.'` — the identical literal `COACTUPC:492` uses — and the screen was reaching that state silently, so the operator got no feedback at all. The account screen already answered this way, so the change also removes an inconsistency between two screens sharing one literal | This CHANGES a status code from `200` to `400` for the no-change case. Three tests encoded the previous contract and were updated to the legacy one; a client that treated the silent `200` as success will now see a `400`, which is the intended report |
| An unknown `action` on the transaction list is rejected with the `CSMSG01Y` invalid-key text instead of being treated as ENTER | Continue ignoring unrecognised values | `COTRN00C` L129-132 ends its AID dispatch with `WHEN OTHER → MOVE CCDA-MSG-INVALID-KEY`, so silently listing the first page invented a behaviour the legacy screen does not have. Absent and blank still mean ENTER, which is the legacy default | The selection branch was ALSO returning an empty page because it called an empty `echoState`; it now calls the same `redisplayCurrentPage` helper the paging boundaries use, so a selected row keeps the page contents |
| Unknown `tranTypeCd` / `tranCatCd` values remain ACCEPTED on add — no change made | Add a reference-data lookup and reject unknown codes | Four independent pieces of evidence say the legacy program does not validate them: `COTRN02C` L452-453 merely `MOVE`s the fields with no lookup, the only foreign key on `transactions` is `fk_transactions_card`, the runtime accepts `99`/`9999` and stores them verbatim, and those codes genuinely do not exist in `tran_type` (which holds exactly `01`–`07`). Adding a check would be a business-rule change, which AAP 0.7.1 and 0.7.6 forbid | Referential integrity for transaction classification is therefore not enforced at the database. That is the legacy behaviour, recorded so it is a known position rather than an oversight |

### 46.3 Error-response contract

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| A filter-chain denial (`401`, `403`) and a throttled request (`429`) now carry the shared `ErrorResponse` envelope, where they previously carried NO body | Keep the empty bodies, which an earlier review had accepted as "by design" | The API answered one failure in six different shapes, which is the finding. The empty body was defensible in isolation but it forced every client to special-case the security statuses, and the envelope discloses strictly less than the alternatives: the message is ONLY the status reason phrase, never an exception detail or resource name. A leak scan over eleven sensitive tokens returns nothing, and the `/actuator/env` denial names no property source, profile, datasource or credential | This SUPERSEDES the earlier "empty by design" position, so it is a reversal and recorded as one. Two `ObservabilityEndpointsIT` suites asserted an empty body as a PROXY for non-disclosure; both now assert the envelope AND assert non-disclosure directly, which is a stronger check than the one they replaced. `Retry-After` is set BEFORE the `429` body is written, because writing commits the response and a later header would never reach the client |
| A session-store outage is answered by a dedicated `DatastoreOutageErrorFilter` writing `503` + `Retry-After`, not by the shared `@RestControllerAdvice` | Handle the exception in the advice; let the `500` stand | The timeout is raised in the FILTER layer — `SessionContextAuthenticationFilter` → `getSession` → `RedisSessionRepository.findById` — before any handler is selected, so `@RestControllerAdvice` can never see it; the container ERROR dispatch then failed a SECOND time on the same lazy session load and degraded the response to a bare `500` with no correlation id. The filter writes the envelope directly rather than calling `sendError`, precisely to avoid re-entering the filters that are failing | Order is load-bearing: `HIGHEST_PRECEDENCE + 20` sits INSIDE `RequestLoggingFilter` so the outage is still logged, and OUTSIDE `SessionRepositoryFilter` (`Integer.MIN_VALUE + 50`) so the lazy load is within reach. It catches exactly `QueryTimeoutException` and `DataAccessResourceFailureException`, so an application fault is not laundered into a `503` | **Consolidated:** three filters had been written for this one condition — a narrow `InfrastructureOutageFilter`, a `SessionStoreFailureFilter` and `DatastoreOutageErrorFilter` — two of them registered at the SAME order, which leaves their relative position undefined and made the response headers depend on it. They are now one filter: it unwraps the cause chain a servlet filter may have wrapped the failure in, intercepts `DataAccessResourceFailureException`, `QueryTimeoutException` and `TransactionException` (a statement the datastore REJECTED is deliberately NOT absorbed, so it is still a `500`), writes `503` with `Retry-After: 10` — the same interval the advice advertises for the identical condition — and resets only the response BUFFER so the headers the outer filters already wrote survive.
| A batch hand-off transport failure is a `503` (`UpstreamUnavailableException`), distinguished from a `400` validation refusal | Keep the single `400` | A caller cannot tell "your request was wrong" from "the batch service is unreachable" when both answer `400`, and only the second is worth retrying — which is why the `503` carries `Retry-After` | None; the validation path is unchanged |
| `correlationId` on the container ERROR dispatch required THREE separate fixes, not the one the finding implied | Rely on `shouldNotFilterErrorDispatch()` alone | Each was independently sufficient to keep the id null. (1) `FilterRegistrationBean` defaults to `REQUEST`-only dispatcher types, so on an ERROR dispatch the correlation filter was never INVOKED at all — `shouldNotFilterErrorDispatch()` governs what a filter does once invoked, never whether the container invokes it. (2) `CardDemoErrorController` set `traceId` but never `correlationId`. (3) `SilentErrorReportValve` passed a literal `null`. Fixing any one alone leaves the id null | The valve can still legitimately resolve `null`: Tomcat refuses a malformed URI before a context is selected, so no filter has run and there is no id to report. That path is documented in place as reachable-and-correct rather than treated as a defect |

### 46.4 Data protection at rest

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| The seeded-PII migration detects the `gcm1:` envelope through `CryptoConverter.isProtected` instead of attempting a decrypt | Keep the try-decrypt probe; compare the prefix inline | The probe asked "did `convertToEntityAttribute` throw?", but that converter deliberately PASSES LEGACY PLAINTEXT THROUGH rather than failing on it — so it never threw, every value looked like ciphertext, the sweep skipped every row, and Flyway recorded SUCCESS while reporting `rewritten=0` against a database full of plaintext. `isProtected` already existed for exactly this question and its docstring says so; it was simply never called | No data was ever exposed, because a separate runtime initialiser encrypts the columns at service startup — the defect was that the migration's REPORT was untrue and its idempotence accidental rather than designed. Both properties are now verified from a cold volume: `rewritten=50` on first run, nothing rewritten on the second, and zero doubly-enveloped rows |

### 46.5 Session rotation under a re-authenticating caller

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| A `SessionRepository` decorator absorbs the single `IllegalStateException("Session was invalidated")` raised when a rotated session's key is already gone | Disable session-fixation protection; catch the exception in the controller; widen the advice to map it to a `200` | Signing on again while holding a live session rotated the id, and the save that followed addressed a key the rotation had already removed — a `500` on a legitimate action. Disabling rotation would trade a cosmetic failure for session fixation, and catching it in the controller would leave every other caller of the repository exposed. The decorator absorbs EXACTLY that message and re-raises anything else, so rotation and its security property are untouched | Registered by a static `BeanPostProcessor`, which is the only ordering that reliably wraps the auto-configured repository. Verified at the level the finding was reported: six consecutive re-sign-ons return `200`, the id rotates every time, and `"Session was invalidated"` appears zero times in any service log |

### 46.6 Deployment topology — SPA upstream resolution

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| The SPA's nginx re-resolves the gateway hostname PER REQUEST (a `resolver` plus a variable in `proxy_pass`), replacing the documented "recreate the frontend after the gateway" contract | Keep the literal upstream and the operating instruction; pin container IPs; add a healthcheck that restarts nginx | Open-source nginx resolves a literal hostname once, at configuration load. Recreating the gateway gives it a new container IP and hands its old address to whichever container starts next, so `/api/**` kept reaching a stale address — in the observed case the batch-service, which has no `/auth` route and issues no CSRF cookie, so CORRECT credentials returned `401` and the SPA was entirely unusable. The failure is indistinguishable from a rejected password, which makes it expensive to diagnose and dangerous to leave to a written instruction. An operating instruction cannot prevent a class of failure that a configuration change eliminates | The resolver addresses come from the container's own `/etc/resolv.conf` via the base image's `NGINX_LOCAL_RESOLVERS`, so the same image is correct for Docker's embedded DNS and for a Kubernetes pod without per-environment configuration. The generated file lives under `/tmp` because this container runs with a READ-ONLY root filesystem and `/tmp` is the only writable path granted by BOTH `docker-compose.yml` (tmpfs) and `k8s/deployment-frontend.yaml` (emptyDir) — writing to `/etc/nginx/conf.d` puts nginx into a restart loop, which was observed and corrected. `rewrite ... break` replaces the trailing slash on `proxy_pass` because nginx does not apply the URI replacement when the target contains a variable |

### 46.7 Tests aligned to the legacy contract rather than to the shipped behaviour

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| Where a correct fix contradicted an existing test, the TEST was changed — never the fix | Preserve the shipped behaviour to keep the suite green | Six suites encoded the defect rather than the contract: two `ObservabilityEndpointsIT`s asserted an empty denial body, `RateLimitFilterTest` asserted an empty `429`, three card suites asserted the silent no-change `200`, and `UserUpdatePage.test.tsx` asserted that a blank password is REJECTED — the precise behaviour the finding reports as broken. Each was rewritten to assert the legacy or documented contract, keeping whatever it genuinely verified | One test was NOT changed for the same reason: `AccountServiceTest.updateAccount_runsEditSequenceBeforeAnyFileRead` failed against a first attempt at the no-change fix, and the source proved the test right and the fix wrong (see 46.2). The rule cuts both ways, which is what makes it a rule rather than a licence |


## 47. QA-Remediation Deviations — Frontend UX and Data-Domain Checkpoint

Non-literal choices made while resolving the runtime QA report raised against the SPA boundary
(`frontend/src/main.tsx`) — ninety findings across the seventeen screens, the shared components and
hooks, the axios client, the nginx SPA host and the services behind them. Recorded here per the
Explainability rule, including the findings that were DECLINED on AAP grounds, the ones whose
suggested fix was replaced by a different one, and the defects found while remediating that the
report did not raise. Every row was verified against the running stack, not only against the tests.

### 47.1 Shared attention-identifier dispatch and duplicate-submit protection

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| A shared `useScreenAction` hook publishes every function-key handler through a ref that is refreshed during RENDER, and `PFKeyBar` dispatches through that ref on BOTH the document `keydown` path and the legend-button `click` path | Add each page's live state to its `pfKeys` dependency array; debounce the dispatch; give `PFKeyBar` a `key` that changes with state | A page rebuilt its `pfKeys` array from a `useCallback` whose dependencies excluded the field it read, so the handler `PFKeyBar` held could be one render behind the input the operator had just typed into — a keystroke was dispatched against a stale snapshot and the value the operator could see on the screen was not the value submitted. Adding dependencies was rejected because it makes the array identity change on every keystroke, which re-registers the document listener continuously and would have to be repeated correctly on all seventeen pages. A ref refreshed during render is always current by construction, and it keeps `pfKeys` identity STABLE so no keystroke can be superseded mid-flight. React flushes a discrete event's render, commit and layout effects before the event dispatch returns but defers passive effects, so the refresh is a layout-phase concern: `useLayoutEffect` closes the stale window and `useEffect` does not | `eslint react-hooks/refs` forbids assigning `ref.current` during render, so the assignment is encapsulated in the hook rather than repeated in pages, and the hook is the only place the rule is reasoned about. A page that publishes a handler WITHOUT the hook reintroduces the staleness silently; `useScreenAction.test.tsx` pins the freshness property |
| Duplicate-submit protection uses a synchronous `useRef` latch, not a `busy` state flag | Disable the control while the request is in flight; guard on the existing `busy` state; debounce | A state flag is not readable by the handler that sets it — three activations dispatched in the same tick all observe the pre-update value and all three fire, which is exactly what produced duplicate user adds, duplicate menu navigations and duplicate browse requests. A ref is written and read synchronously, so the second activation in the same tick sees the latch already closed. Disabling the control was rejected as unrequested observable output: a 3270 screen does not grey out its own function keys, and AAP 0.7.2 forbids output the source does not produce | The latch must be released on every exit path including the error path, or the screen becomes permanently unresponsive. Each page's suite drives a same-tick triple activation and asserts exactly one request, which is the property that fails if a release is missed |

### 47.2 Session, CSRF and error transport at the client

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| `frontend/src/api/client.ts` returns to sign-on on `401` ALONE; a `403` keeps the screen, re-primes the double-submit token and lets the operator retry in place | Keep the previous `if (status === 401 or status === 403)` and treat both as an ended session; treat both as an in-place refusal | The two statuses now mean different things by construction (see the entry in section 37 that classifies them), and acting identically on them discards that work: a `403` is an AUTHENTICATED caller who lacked the authority or whose token was stale, so clearing the screen and demanding a new sign-on destroys typed input the operator can legitimately resubmit. `401` is the only status that means "there is no session", and it is the only one that returns to sign-on. This is also the correction of the E1 explainability gap: the earlier log entry justified the server-side classification on the premise that the SPA already behaved this way, which was not true of the client the QA report exercised | The split means a `403` no longer produces a visible state change other than the message line, so a genuinely revoked session that answers `403` would leave the operator retrying. That path cannot occur: revocation deletes the session, so the next request is unauthenticated and answers `401` |
| A `401` or `403` with a zero-length body is given the client's own fixed message, and typed input is preserved | Show nothing; show the raw axios error; clear the screen | The refusal now always carries a body (section 47.9), but the client must not depend on that — a proxy or a future component can still answer empty. Without a message the screen simply blanked, which on a 3270 is indistinguishable from a crash. The client's fallback text for `401` is `Your session has ended. Please sign on again.`, character-for-character the same sentence the server-side `RefusalEnvelopeWriter` emits, so the operator sees one sentence whichever side produced it | Two copies of one literal can drift. They are pinned on both sides: the server constant in `RefusalEnvelopeWriter` is asserted by `RefusalEnvelopeWriterTest` and by `SecurityConfigTest`, and the client constant by the client suite |
| The double-submit token is primed by a safe request when the cookie is ABSENT, and re-primed after a `403` | Fetch the token eagerly at application start; retry the write automatically after re-priming | A first write after a cookie was dropped (a restored tab, a cleared cookie jar, an expired token) failed with no way back except a manual reload. Priming on demand keeps the eager path unchanged for the common case, and re-priming after a `403` means the operator's own retry succeeds. Automatic retry was rejected: replaying a write the caller did not re-authorise is exactly the duplicate-submit behaviour section 47.1 exists to prevent | Priming issues one extra safe request on the affected path. It is idempotent and answers from the session, so it cannot itself create a session |
| A transport failure reports the legacy-consistent sentence rather than axios's `Network Error` | Surface the axios message; show nothing and log | `Network Error` is a library string, not an operator sentence, and it appeared on the message line where every legacy literal appears. The replacement states that the request could not be completed and asks the operator to retry, which is the shape every other line-23 message has | The specific cause is no longer visible on the screen; it remains in the console and in the correlation-tagged telemetry record |
| The SPA document is served `Cache-Control: no-store`, and `App.tsx` revalidates the session on `pageshow` and `visibilitychange`, clearing rendered protected data on a back-forward-cache restore | Rely on `no-cache`; clear on `beforeunload`; leave the restore alone | `no-cache` still permits STORAGE, and a document restored from the back-forward cache is not re-fetched at all — so a screen drawn under a session that has since been revoked could be reinstated with account and card details still painted on it. The document carries that data once the SPA has drawn it, so the directive it needs is `no-store`, and the restore path needs an explicit revalidation because no network request happens on it | The revalidation costs one safe request per restore or tab re-focus. It answers from the session and is the same request the client already makes on load |
| Sign-on by an ALREADY-authenticated principal is idempotent for the SAME user and refused for a DIFFERENT one; the live session is not destroyed | Always rotate and re-create; always refuse; always destroy and re-create | The previous behaviour destroyed the live session and created a new one on every sign-on request, so a duplicate submit or a re-entered sign-on screen invalidated the session the operator was already using and the following request answered `401`. Rotation cannot be the answer here because TWO Spring Session participants observe the id — the gateway and the service that authenticated — and rotating mid-flight leaves one of them holding a key that no longer exists. Returning the live context for the same principal is the behaviour `COSGN00C` has by construction: it re-reads `USRSEC` and transfers to the menu, it does not tear down the terminal's state `[app/cbl/COSGN00C.cbl:L211-237]` | A different principal signing on over a live session is REFUSED rather than switched, so an operator changing identity must exit first. That is the safe direction and matches the 3270, where `F3` returns to sign-on before a new user can enter |
| Session-fixation defence is preserved by REUSING the pre-authentication session and stripping every attribute from it, instead of rotating the id at sign-on ("Part B"); the gateway-side companion change ("Part A") was written and then REVERTED | Rotate the id as before and accept the broken session; add a distributed rotation protocol between the gateway and the services | The fixation risk is that an attacker-known id becomes an authenticated id while still carrying attributes the attacker planted. Stripping every attribute removes the planted state, which is the property that matters, without changing the id that two participants have already observed. Part A had the gateway participate in a rotation handshake; it was reverted because it made the gateway a second writer of session identity, and the failure it was compensating for disappeared once sign-on stopped destroying the session | The id itself is no longer rotated, so the defence rests on the strip rather than on a new identifier. The strip is asserted directly, and the surviving attribute set after sign-on is pinned |
| A role-`U` operator who deep-links to an administrator-only route is told why they were returned to the menu, rather than being bounced silently | Bounce silently as before; render a dedicated forbidden screen; leave the route reachable and let the API refuse it | A silent bounce is indistinguishable from a broken link, and the operator cannot tell whether the screen exists. `COMEN01C` refuses an option outside the role-filtered table with a message on line 23 and keeps the operator on the menu, so the message — not a new screen — is the legacy answer. Letting the route render and relying on the API refusal was rejected: the screen would paint its captions before being refused, which shows an operator a screen they may not use | The message is composed rather than a frozen literal, because no legacy program answers a URL. The route guard and the API authority are independent, so both must hold; the gateway asserts the route set and the guard is asserted per route |
| A live session revoked in place (role change, self-demotion, self-delete) exits through a composed message that names what happened, rather than a bare return to sign-on | Let the next request fail with the generic `401`; poll for revocation | An administrator who demotes or deletes their own account was returned to sign-on with no explanation, which reads as a fault. The `useSelfRevocationExit` hook composes the exit message from the action that caused it, so the operator sees the consequence of their own edit. On the 3270 the equivalent feedback was immediate because the next transaction re-read `USRSEC`; there is no polling analogue and none was added | A revoked record can legitimately linger in a list drawn before the revocation, because the list is a snapshot the legacy screen also took. The exit happens on the next request, not on a push |

### 47.3 Field-error marking, the message line, and the in-flight state

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| `CardDemoException` gained a `field` member and an `onField(name)` factory, and `handleCardDemoException` populates `errorCode` and `fieldErrors` on the envelope, so a domain refusal identifies the failing field | Have each page infer the field by matching the message text; return only the message and mark nothing | The legacy programs do not merely emit a sentence — they also `MOVE -1` to the failing field's length, which places the 3270 cursor on it. Reproducing only the sentence loses half the behaviour, and the operator has to find the offending field by reading. Matching on message text was rejected outright: the literals are frozen contracts, so a text match makes every page depend on the exact wording of a message it does not own, and a single wording change would silently stop marking anything | The field name is now part of the API contract for those refusals. It is derived from the DTO property name, so a DTO rename must be carried into the exception's `onField` call; the service suites assert the pair |
| The faulted control is styled through the `aria-invalid='true'` ATTRIBUTE (`input[aria-invalid='true']`), not through a class added at each call site | Add a `.fieldError` class alongside the attribute in all 46 places a control is rendered | The attribute is the accessible signal and is already set by the marking logic, so styling it makes the visual and the assistive signal the same fact rather than two facts that can disagree. Adding a class in 46 places is 46 opportunities to set one and forget the other | Any control that must look faulted without being announced as invalid cannot be expressed this way. No such case exists: every marking in the application corresponds to a refusal the operator must correct |
| The message line renders the error line AND the informational line together for the five mapsets that declare both fields | Show only the error; concatenate them into one line; alternate between them | Those five mapsets declare two separate display fields at two separate screen positions, and the programs write to them independently — the error explains the refusal while the informational line states what to do next. Collapsing them loses the instruction exactly when the operator needs it | Two lines occupy two rows of the 24-row frame on those screens. **Corrected in section 47.10 on three counts:** the example literal quoted here originally (`Press PF5 key to save your updates ...`) is `COUSR02C`'s GREEN `ERRMSG` text and COUSR02 declares no `INFOMSG` field at all, so it was never an example of this case; the row said nothing about ORDER, and the two rows shipped INVERTED; and it said nothing about COLOUR, and the informational row shipped GREEN where all five maps declare `COLOR=NEUTRAL` |
| The in-flight state is announced through a visually hidden live region, not through visible screen output | Render a spinner or a "please wait" literal; disable the whole form | A 3270 signals a busy terminal by locking the keyboard, not by drawing anything, and AAP 0.7.2 forbids observable output the source does not produce — so a spinner is out. A hidden live region is not screen output; it makes the state perceivable to assistive technology without changing what the terminal displays | The state is invisible to a sighted operator except through the keyboard lock described in section 47.5. That is the legacy behaviour being reproduced, not an omission |
| The end-of-list notices (`You have reached the bottom of the page...`) keep the message line's ordinary colour — red — while carrying `role='status'` rather than `role='alert'` | Recolour them green or neutral to match their informational role | The 3270 message line has ONE colour; the programs move these literals into the SAME field as every refusal, so recolouring them would be a visual distinction the source does not make (AAP 0.7.2). The role, unlike the colour, is invisible to a sighted operator and can therefore carry the politeness distinction without altering what the screen shows | An observer measuring colour alone will read these as errors — a QA pass did exactly that, twice. The role attribute is the discriminator, and it is asserted per screen |
| Input `:hover`, `:active` and `:disabled` treatments were added, and `:focus-within` on a table row was separated from the `.selected` treatment | Leave the states unstyled as the mapsets imply; reuse the `.selected` colour for `:focus-within` | The mapsets describe a character terminal and say nothing about pointer states, so there is no source literal to reproduce — but a control that looks identical whether it is enterable or protected misinforms the operator, which the 3270 never did (it used field attributes for exactly this). The states therefore reuse the existing field tokens and add no new colour. `:focus-within` had been sharing the `.selected` background, so a row the operator had merely tabbed into looked SELECTED — a false statement about what the next action will act on (section 47.7) | Pointer states are presentation with no legacy analogue, so they are justified by the field-attribute intent rather than by a literal. They use tokens only, so a theme change carries |
| A protected (display-only) value is visually distinguished from an editable input AND is reachable, but a value rendered as protected is rendered as a `<dd>` element rather than a disabled input | Keep every field an input and set `readonly`; render protected values as disabled inputs | A disabled input is unreachable, so an operator cannot read a long protected value that overflows its box; a readonly input still looks editable. A `<dd>` reads as a value, is selectable and is reachable by ordinary caret browsing. The trade-off is that the element TYPE changes when a field becomes protected, so the `id` disappears and only `data-testid` survives — which is documented here because it is a real constraint on every test and every runtime probe of those screens | Any probe that selects a protected field by `id` finds nothing. The two screens that protect fields (`/cards/update`, `/cards/view`) are the ones affected, and their suites select by `data-testid` |

### 47.4 Card screens

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| **DECLINED ON AAP GROUNDS**: the card list does NOT retain the page the operator was on when returning from a detail screen | Remember the page in the router state or in the session and restore it | `COCRDLIC` resets the browse to page 1 on every re-entry: it re-establishes the browse from the filter fields rather than from a saved position, so returning from the detail screen shows the FIRST page. AAP 0.7.1 requires workflow sequencing to be reproduced exactly, and this is sequencing the operator can observe. Retaining the page would be an improvement, and improvements to business behaviour are what AAP 0.7.6 forbids | Accepted: an operator returning from a card on page 4 pages forward again. This is the legacy behaviour and is recorded rather than repaired |
| The card-detail screen uses `COCRDSLC`'s SUPPLIED-and-not-numeric literals even though the three declared `88` levels carrying them are never `SET` anywhere in the program | Use the declared `88` wording as if it were reachable; invent a message | This is the third occurrence in this project of a declared `88` level whose wording looks authoritative but which no statement ever activates. The reachable path composes its message elsewhere, so the declared literal is dead text. Verifying activation with `grep -n "SET <88-NAME>"` before trusting a declared literal is now the standing rule, and it is recorded here because two earlier rows in this log were written before it was | A future reader who finds the declared literal and not this note may "correct" the screen back to dead wording. The three literals are pinned by the card-detail suite with the citation in the test name |
| The card-detail screen requires the FULL composite key (account id AND card number), and the hand-over from the list carries the card number in a router-state member named `from` rather than in the URL | Put the card number in the path (`/cards/:cardNumber`); pass it as a query parameter | A primary account number must never appear in a URL: the URL is written to the nginx access log, to the browser history and to the referrer of any subsequent request. `COCRDSLC` reads by the composite key, so the key is required rather than inferred, and the `from` member also carries the originating screen — the role `CDEMO-FROM-PROGRAM` plays in the COMMAREA — so `F3` returns where the legacy program would have returned | A deep link to a card detail cannot carry the card number, so arriving without the hand-over shows the empty entry state and asks for the key. That is the same state the legacy screen shows when entered directly. A documented consequence remains: after a MANUAL search on `/cards/view` the router state still holds the ORIGINAL hand-over, so `F3` returns to the screen the operator arrived from rather than the one they searched from |
| One finding arrived with NO description anywhere in the report; it was root-caused at runtime instead of being closed as unactionable | Close it as unactionable for lack of a description; guess at the intent from its title | The screen it named was exercised directly and the defect was real and MINE: a change made earlier in this same remediation had left the card-detail screen losing focus to `<body>` after every read, so the cursor was nowhere. Four smaller fidelity defects on the same screen surfaced with it. An undescribed finding is a reason to go and look, not a reason to close | The resolution addresses what was observed rather than what the report intended, which may differ. What was observed, fixed and re-verified is recorded in the resolution report so a reader can judge the match |
| Card Update renders the account and card key fields as INPUTS while the record has not yet been fetched, and works as a standalone entry point | Protect them always and require arrival from the list; redirect a direct visit to the list | `COCRDUP.bms` declares those fields `UNPROT` and the program protects them only once `CCUP-DETAILS-NOT-FETCHED` is false — so on entry they are enterable, which is what makes the screen reachable from the menu as option 5. Redirecting a direct visit would remove a menu path the legacy application has | The fields change element type when the fetch succeeds (see section 47.3), which is the behaviour the mapset describes |
| Card Update refuses an unchanged submission instead of reporting a commit | Write the record anyway; report success without writing | Reporting a commit that did not happen is a false statement to the operator, and writing an identical record burns a version increment that would then make a concurrent editor's legitimate retry fail. The no-change guard compares the typed state against the fetched snapshot | The comparison must use the same normalisation the save path uses, or a cosmetically different but semantically identical entry is reported as unchanged. The account-screen equivalent (section 47.5) shares that reasoning and its `toRecordState` helper is the precedent |
| `F12=Cancel` on Card Update re-reads the record and repaints the screen IN PLACE; it does not navigate to the view screen | Navigate to `/cards/view`; clear the fields; go back in history | `F12` on this screen is the program's discard-my-edits action, not a navigation: it restores what is on file and leaves the operator on the same screen able to start again. Navigating away is a different action with a different legend. Confirmed still dispatchable after a `409`, which is when an operator most needs it | The re-read costs one request. It is the same request the initial fetch made |
| The first refusal reported by Card Update follows `COCRDUPC`'s own edit ORDER — card name, then status, then expiry month | Report all refusals at once; order them by screen position | The program edits in a fixed sequence and stops at the first failure, placing the cursor there. Reporting a different field first sends the operator to the wrong box, and reporting all of them at once is output the source does not produce | The order is a frozen behaviour with no other expression than the code that implements it; the suite asserts the sequence with a case per position |

### 47.5 Account screens

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| A new endpoint `POST /accounts/{id}/validate` runs the program's own `1200-EDIT-MAP-INPUTS` pass server-side, and ENTER on the update screen calls it | Port the semantic edits into the client; validate only on save | The edits are not format checks: the FICO range, the North-American area-code lookup, the state code and the state-and-zip cross-edit are business rules that live with the data, and `COACTUPC` runs them as a distinct pass on the ENTER key before any write. Porting them to TypeScript would create a second implementation of the same rules that can disagree with the first, and the AAP forbids changing the rules at all — so the only faithful reading is one implementation, invoked from where the program invokes it. Validating only on save would remove the ENTER behaviour the operator relies on | A new route in the API surface with no legacy transaction id of its own. It is a validation pass with no side effects: it returns `204` when the input is acceptable and the standard field-marked `400` when it is not, and it writes nothing |
| The account-status refusal reads `Account Status must be Y or N.` — composed by `1220-EDIT-YESNO` — and NOT the declared `ACCT-STATUS-MUST-BE-YES-NO` wording `Account Active Status must be Y or N` | Use the declared `88` literal, which reads like the authoritative message | `ACCT-STATUS-MUST-BE-YES-NO` is declared at `[app/cbl/COACTUPC.cbl:L503-504]` and never `SET` anywhere in the program. The reachable path is `1220-EDIT-YESNO`, which STRINGs the trimmed variable name with the literal `' must be Y or N.'`, so the sentence the operator actually sees is composed, not declared — including its trailing full stop, which the declared literal lacks. `requireYesNo(value, variableName)` reproduces the composition rather than hard-coding one sentence, so the same routine serves every yes-or-no field the program edits | A composed message depends on the variable NAME passed in; a wrong name produces a plausible-looking wrong sentence. The names are taken from the program's own `MOVE` into `WS-EDIT-VARIABLE-NAME` and asserted per field |
| The customer zip is TRUNCATED to `ACSZIPC LENGTH=5` for display, and a stored ZIP+4 is therefore an ANNOUNCED change on save rather than a silent truncation | Widen the input to the record's `PIC X(10)`; store the truncated value silently | The mapset declares five characters, so five is what the screen shows and five is what the operator can type — widening the box would be a screen the source does not have. But the RECORD holds ten, and comparing the five-character display against the ten-character record would make every save of an untouched ZIP+4 look like an edit that silently discarded four digits. `toRecordState()` keeps the record's own value in the snapshot so the comparison is record-against-record, and a genuine truncation is reported to the operator as the change it is, which is what `1205` does when it announces a changed field `[app/cbl/COACTUPC.cbl:L1744-1747]` | An operator cannot enter a ZIP+4 through this screen, because the legacy screen could not either. The five-character box was runtime-verified as fully visible (`scrollWidth` 65 equals `clientWidth` 65, maximum `scrollLeft` 0), which is the finding that started this row |
| The save path re-runs the edit pass against the values being written, so a validated screen that is then EDITED is refused at save | Trust the earlier `validate` call; validate only on ENTER | A successful validate is a statement about the values that were sent to it, not a licence for whatever is sent next. Runtime-proven: a FICO of 800 validated (`204`), was changed to 999, and the save answered `400` with the field marked, the cursor moved and nothing stored. This is the same reasoning as the add-transaction row in section 47.6 — the transport, unlike CICS, cannot guarantee the two payloads match | The edit pass runs twice on a saved screen. It is CPU-only and inside the response budget |
| After a successful save, a browser BACK then FORWARD lands on the screen's legacy ENTRY state — key field enabled, empty and focused — rather than on the stale saved form | Restore the submitted values; block the history entry; navigate away on success | The record on file may have changed since, so repainting the submitted values would show data that is no longer true and offer a save against a superseded version. The entry state is what the legacy screen shows after a completed update, and it is the only state that cannot mislead. Runtime-verified: 43 inputs present, key field enabled, empty and focused, and a full re-key through ENTER and `F5` committing again | An operator using FORWARD to "get back to" their edit finds an empty screen and must re-key. That is the legacy behaviour after a completed update |
| While a write is in flight the screen sets a `locked` flag that disables the function-key legends, and a key pressed during that window is DISCARDED rather than queued | Queue the keystroke and replay it; leave the keys live; block with an overlay | This is the 3270 keyboard lock: between the AID being sent and the reply arriving the terminal accepts nothing, and nothing is buffered. Queuing would deliver an action against a screen that has since been repainted by the reply — the very class of defect section 47.1 addresses.
