The lock covers WRITES only, because a read does not lock the legacy keyboard | Runtime-verified on a throttled connection: all four legends disabled for about fifteen seconds, an `F3` pressed during the window discarded rather than queued, and no navigation request ever issued. An operator who presses a key during a slow write must press it again |
| On a `409` the screen RE-READS the record and shows the values now on file (`3202-SHOW-ORIGINAL-VALUES`), replacing an earlier fix that refreshed only the version snapshot and kept the typed values | Keep the typed values and refresh the version so the retry succeeds; abandon the edit | The earlier approach was found to be a LOST-UPDATE path: refreshing the version while keeping the operator's stale field values means the retry overwrites the concurrent editor's changes with values typed against the superseded record — the retry succeeds and the other edit vanishes. `COACTUPC` answers a concurrent change by displaying what is on file with the literal `Record changed by some one else. Please review`, which is a request to look before deciding, not an offer to retry blindly. `reviewCurrentRecord` reproduces that | The operator must re-key their changes after a conflict. That is the legacy behaviour and the only reading that cannot lose an update. Runtime-proven on both `/accounts/update` and `/cards/update`: `409`, then re-read, then a retry that commits |
| The `F12=Cancel` legend is published only while `ACUP-CHANGES-MADE AND NOT OKAYED-AND-DONE` | Publish `F12` always; publish it whenever the screen is in an update state | The legend set is part of what the screen displays, and the program builds it from those two flags at `[app/cbl/COACTUPC.cbl:L660-665]` — an operator who has made no change has nothing to cancel and the legacy screen does not offer the key | The condition is state-derived, so a state the flags do not cover would hide the key. The two flags are the program's own complete enumeration of the screen's update states |
| An empty definition cell is given a line box with `content: '\200B'` on `dd:empty::before` — a zero-width space in CSS GENERATED CONTENT — scoped to the seven row containers that lay out value cells rather than to every `dd` in the document | Set a `min-height` on the cell; render a non-breaking space in the DOM; leave the cell collapsed | A collapsed cell has no line box, so the whole screen re-flowed as values arrived and the function-key bar and message line moved on first load. A zero-width space in generated content gives the cell exactly one line box with no ink and no text node. Note the deliberate distinction from section 47.9, which STRIPS that same character from DATA: a zero-width space is inert as presentation and dangerous as content, because content is what an operator reads and believes | A probe that scans rendered text for zero-width characters will find these and may mistake them for un-normalised data — a QA pass did. They are CSS pseudo-element content, not DOM text, and are never submitted anywhere. Runtime-verified layout deltas of 0.00 px on both the function-key bar and the message line |
| The blank `Account Group:` value is NOT a defect and was left as it is | Populate it from another field; hide the label when empty | `ACCT-GROUP-ID` occupies positions 113-122 of the account record and is blank for all fifty seeded accounts in `app/data/ASCII/acctdata.txt`. The screen is displaying what is on file. Hiding the label would remove a caption the mapset declares | None. Recorded so the empty caption is not "fixed" by inventing a value |

### 47.6 Transaction screens, bill payment and reports

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| Screen filter edits examine the RAW field value; trimming before the class test was the shared root cause of three findings | Trim first and then test, as the previous code did; trim only for the not-supplied test | A COBOL class test examines the `PIC X(n)` field exactly as it stands. `IS NUMERIC` on a field containing a tab or `' 15 '` is FALSE, so the program refuses it — but code that trims first sees an empty string (not supplied) or a clean `15` (valid) and dispatches a request the legacy screen would never have sent. One root cause produced the tab case, the embedded-space case AND the menu-option case, which is why the edits are now a shared module (`screenFilters.ts`, `menuOptionEdit.ts`) rather than three local helpers | Trim-before-edit reads like an obvious tidy-up and will be reintroduced by anyone who does not know why it is wrong. Each module carries the class-test citation, and the suites include the decisive cases (a genuine tab, `' 15 '`, and `-1` which must NOT become `1`) |
| Key arithmetic on transaction ids stays in the STRING domain (`leftPadZeros`, `decrementDigits`); `Long.parseLong` was removed | Parse to `long` and arithmetic there, guarding the range; use `BigInteger` | A sixteen-digit key is a fixed-width zero-padded digit string, so its lexicographic order IS its numeric order and no conversion is needed to page or compare. Parsing imposed a range the CALLER controls: a filter longer than nineteen digits threw `NumberFormatException` and surfaced as a `500`. Clamping the field to its declared width closes the client side, but the server must not depend on the client having done so | String arithmetic must handle the all-zeros and all-nines edges explicitly, which it does. Runtime-verified: twenty-five typed characters clamped to sixteen, a React-aware setter with nineteen characters clamped to sixteen, and a sixteen-digit filter answering `200` with no `NumberFormatException` anywhere |
| A new endpoint `GET /transactions/keys` restores `COTRN02C`'s key-then-data ordering, and supplying either key cross-fills its counterpart | Fetch the whole transaction and discard what is not needed; validate the keys client-side | `COTRN02C` edits and resolves the KEYS first and only then reads data — an account id alone is enough to resolve the card number through the cross-reference, which is what the operator relies on when they know one and not the other. Fetching a whole transaction to obtain a key would read data the program has not yet decided to read, and would answer differently when no transaction exists | A second route serving one screen's key resolution. It returns only the resolved key pair, and the five refusal literals are asserted exactly. Runtime-verified: account `00000000011` alone cross-filled card `7427684863423209` |
| The add-transaction service re-runs the whole edit pass on EVERY write, although `1200-EDIT-MAP-INPUTS` skips it on the confirming `F5` pass | Trust the confirmed pass because the first pass already validated; re-run only the key edits | The program can skip the second pass safely because the terminal state cannot change between the two passes — CICS holds the screen. An HTTP client can send the confirming request with a different body, so skipping the pass would let a caller bypass every edit by confirming a payload the first pass never saw. The skip is an artifact of the transport, not a business rule, so reproducing it would be reproducing a vulnerability | The confirmed request costs the edit pass a second time. It is CPU-only and measured inside the response budget |
| The launched report identifier is carried in a `data-message-reference` attribute rather than being rendered on the screen | Render the job-execution id in the confirmation message; omit it entirely | The operator-facing confirmation is a frozen literal that does not name a job id, and adding one would be unrequested observable output. But the id is what makes a submission traceable, so it travels as an attribute — machine-readable, invisible on the screen, and available to a support probe | An operator cannot read the reference off the screen. Support reads it from the attribute or from the correlation-tagged log record. Runtime-verified: seven submissions produced seven distinct references |
| Monetary values the screen displays as a signed COBOL picture are rendered by `toSignedAmountPicture` into the exact fourteen-character form (`+0000000194.00`) | Render the plain decimal and let the screen format it; format server-side into the locale's currency form | The mapset field is a signed numeric picture of a fixed width, so the leading sign, the zero fill and the two decimal places are part of what the operator sees — a plain `194.00` is a different screen. Formatting is done where the picture is known, and the width is asserted rather than assumed: `+0000000194.00`, `+0000000002.00` and `+0000000000.00` all measure fourteen characters. The wire still carries `BigDecimal` for the value itself, per AAP 0.3.6 | The picture is presentation, so a caller needing the number reads the numeric member, not the picture. Both travel |
| A bill payment with nothing to pay answers `200` and shows the refusal literal together with the balance, rather than `400` | Answer `400` with the refusal; answer `200` with the refusal alone | `COBIL00C` redisplays the screen with the balance visible and the refusal on line 23 — the operator needs to SEE the zero balance that caused the refusal. A `400` would be a client-error classification for a screen the program repainted successfully, and the client's error path clears the data area, which is exactly the balance the operator must read | A caller that treats `200` as "payment taken" would be wrong. The response carries the refusal message, and the payment path is identified by the balance change, not by the status |
| The transaction list accepts a PARTIAL transaction-id filter while the card list requires a full account id — an asymmetry that is preserved | Make both partial; make both exact | The two programs differ: `COTRN00C` browses from the supplied key, so a partial key is a legitimate starting position, while `COCRDLIC` uses the account id as an equality filter. Making them uniform would change one screen's behaviour to match the other's | The asymmetry looks like an inconsistency and will be reported as one. It is the source behaviour, cited in both screens' suites |
| The confirmation separator is a literal row of seventy hyphen-minus characters, replacing an `<hr>` element | Keep the `<hr>` and style it to the right width; use a CSS border | The mapsets draw these rules as seventy `-` characters in a text field, so the rule is TEXT that occupies exactly seventy cells of the eighty-column grid. An `<hr>` is a box whose width comes from its container, and it rendered about two pixels wide with roughly 383 px margins. Runtime-verified after the change: zero `<hr>` elements in the document, seventy U+002D characters, margins 0 px, rendered ink 672.11 px | The literal must stay exactly seventy characters or the rule no longer aligns with the frame; each screen's suite counts them |

### 47.7 User administration

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| On the update-user screen the password is OPTIONAL: leaving it blank means "unchanged", and the credential write is skipped when it is absent | Require the password on every update, as the shipped code did; pre-fill the field with the stored credential the way `COUSR02C` does | `COUSR02C` pre-fills the password field with `SEC-USR-PWD` at `[app/cbl/COUSR02C.cbl:L169]` and the mapset renders it `ATTRB=(DRK,FSET,UNPROT)` — dark, so the operator cannot read it, but present, so an unchanged submission rewrites the same value. That is IMPOSSIBLE once the credential is a one-way hash (AAP 0.6.7): there is nothing to pre-fill with, and pre-filling the hash would both display it and store a hash of a hash. Absence-means-unchanged is the only faithful reading of a screen where an untouched field must not alter the credential. The refusal `Password can NOT be empty...` remains reachable where the source actually raises it — on ADD, at `[app/cbl/COUSR01C.cbl:L138]` — so no literal is lost | An operator cannot tell from the screen whether a password will change, because the field is blank either way. That is closer to the legacy screen (which showed nothing readable) than a mandatory field, which forced a credential reset on every name correction — the defect this replaces |
| **DECLINED ON AAP GROUNDS**, both halves | Add a confirm-password field; add a change-password affordance separate from the update screen | `COUSR02.bms` declares one password field and `COUSR02C` edits one, so a second field is a screen the source does not have and a separate affordance is a transaction the application does not have. AAP 0.7.2 freezes the field set of each mapset | Accepted: a mistyped new password is stored as typed. The legacy screen had the same property |
| The user type is accepted case-INSENSITIVELY and canonicalised to upper case | Accept only upper case and refuse `a`/`u`; canonicalise silently without refusing anything else | `COUSR00C` branches on BOTH cases in its `WHEN` clauses, so the legacy application already treated `a` and `A` as the same type; refusing lower case would be stricter than the source. Canonicalising on the way in keeps one stored representation, which is what the role mapping reads | A caller sending a lower-case type sees an upper-case value returned. That is the stored value, and the round-trip is asserted |
| The destructive Delete action is removed from the click path and from the tab order; deletion happens only through the `F5=Delete` function-key legend | Keep the body button and add a confirmation dialog; keep it and style it as destructive | The mapsets declare no body buttons at all — the function-key bar is the entire action surface — so a body button was undeclared observable output, and an undeclared TAB STOP on the most destructive action in the application. A confirmation dialog is likewise a screen the source does not have. The legend button is reachable, is labelled with the key that performs the action, and sits where every other action sits | An operator who expects a button finds none. The legend is on line 24 of every screen and reads `F5=Delete` |
| A zero-row user list renders an explicit empty-state row rather than an empty table | Leave the table body empty; hide the table | An empty `<tbody>` renders as a caption and a header over nothing, which reads as a failed load rather than as an empty result. The two sibling list screens already rendered an empty-state row, so this makes the three consistent rather than introducing a pattern | The empty-state row is not a legacy literal; it is the absence of rows made visible. It carries `role='status'` and is selected by `data-testid` |
| The row highlight marks the TOPMOST selection only | Highlight every selected row; highlight none | The program acts on the first selected row and ignores the rest, so highlighting all of them would show the operator a set of rows that is not what the action will use | An operator who ticks three rows sees one highlighted. That is the row the action will take, which is the information the highlight exists to convey |
| `resolveFaultedField` returned without a null guard and could throw when a refusal named no field | Catch the throw at the call site; make every refusal name a field | This was a NEW abend found while remediating and not raised by the report: a refusal with no field member reached the resolver and the screen blanked with `UNEXPECTED ABEND OCCURRED.`. Not every refusal has a field — a firewall or transport refusal has none — so requiring one is wrong; the resolver must tolerate absence. Related and worth recording: `fieldErrors` is ALWAYS a present key in the envelope, serialised as an explicit `null` when unset (section 47.9), so a truthiness check on the key is not a null check on the value | A null-tolerant resolver silently marks nothing when a field name is missing, which is correct but indistinguishable from a resolver that is broken. The refusals that DO name a field are asserted per screen |

### 47.8 Responsive adaptation at 375 px

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| ONE root-cause change: below 48 rem the terminal frame keeps its eighty-column width via `min-width: calc(var(--term-cols) * 1ch + 2 * var(--term-pad-x))`, and the DOCUMENT pans | Reflow the screens at narrow widths; stack the fields; wrap the text; shrink the type further | Nine reported symptoms — clipped captions, truncated values, overlapping confirm prompts, broken rule lines, a wrapped header, two panned screens, an unreachable function-key bar and a clipped sign-on note — were not nine defects. They were consequences of one: the frame narrowed to the viewport (`width: 100%` with `max-width: 100%`), giving a 360.6 px body while every mapset declares eighty columns. A 3270 is not a reflowable document; it is a fixed grid the operator pans. `min-width` overrides `max-width` in the used-width calculation, so the single declaration restores the grid and every symptom resolves at once. Runtime-verified at 375 px on every screen: frame 590 px, body 576 px, body `scrollWidth` equal to `clientWidth`, maximum `scrollLeft` 0, document 590 px wider than the 375 px viewport, and zero overflowing elements on exhaustive whole-subtree sweeps | The screens must be panned horizontally on a phone-width viewport. That is the correct behaviour for a fixed-grid terminal and is what the operator does on real hardware. Thin scrollbars are styled so the pan is discoverable |
| `useFocusOnChange` gained an additive `ready` parameter (defaulting to true), so a cursor-placement token is not consumed while the target is still `disabled`, and a REPEAT of the same refusal re-places the cursor | Place the cursor unconditionally; compare the message text and skip a repeat | A token spent against a disabled input is spent for nothing, and the field the operator must correct keeps the cursor only until the next render. Skipping a repeated refusal is worse than it sounds: the programs `MOVE -1` to the failing field on EVERY failing pass, so a second identical refusal re-places the cursor too, and an operator who presses ENTER twice on the same bad value must see the cursor move both times. Runtime-verified over seven consecutive refusing passes: `focusout #option`, `focusin BUTTON`, `focusout BUTTON`, `focusin #option` on every pass including the identical repeat, with ZERO HTTP requests for all seven | The parameter defaults to true, so existing call sites are unaffected — which also means a new call site that needs it must pass it. The two menu screens pass `!busy` |
| Seven per-symptom patches were written, tested against the root-cause fix, and DELIBERATELY NOT APPLIED | Apply them as belt and braces alongside the root-cause fix | Each was measured as unnecessary once the frame kept its width, and each would have introduced a cost: a focusable scroll container adds an undeclared TAB STOP and breaks the byte-identical tab order; `pre-wrap` on the confirm prompts would wrap text the mapset does not wrap (measured clearance afterwards: 180.03 px and 194.44 px); `min-width: max-content` on the rule lines was moot once the rules measured 504.094 px of ink, exactly 70.01 cells; `minmax(0, 1fr)` on the card-update grid and `display: contents` on the header row were both moot (the Date and Time cells measured 16 px, one line box); a sticky function-key bar was unnecessary once the bar sat 209.5 px above the fold. Eight brittle rules to express one fact is the outcome this avoided | The patches are not in the tree, so a future regression of the root cause reproduces all nine symptoms at once rather than degrading gracefully. That is preferable: nine loud symptoms point at one cause, and the per-breakpoint measurements are pinned |
| The incomplete `@media (max-width: 32rem)` stacking block was DELETED rather than extended, and the frame's own twenty-four-row `min-height` was restored at that breakpoint | Extend the stacking list to cover the remaining screens; leave the partial block in place | The block stacked three selectors out of the many that needed it, so it produced a half-stacked layout that was worse than either alternative — and stacking is the reflow the previous row rejects. Restoring the row-count `min-height` was needed because with a real horizontal scrollbar present the frame's `100vh` put line 24 exactly under the scrollbar strip. Runtime-verified frame heights 375/603/391/391 px with computed `min-height` 374.4 px, and clearance from the function-key bar to the fold of 436.8/209.5/421.4/421.2 px, where it had been 0 | The `min-height` is expressed in rows rather than in viewport units, so a future change to `--term-line` changes the frame height. That is the intended coupling: the frame is twenty-four rows tall by definition |
| Program-driven cursor placement goes through a shared `placeCursor(el)` that calls `focus({ preventScroll: true })` | Use `el.focus()`; scroll the element into view deliberately | `focus()` scrolls the element into view, which has no 3270 analogue: the terminal shows all 24x80 at once, so placing the cursor never moves the operator's view. On a panned narrow viewport the default behaviour opened `/cards/update` already scrolled to about x=157, hiding the left-hand captions. The placement itself is correct — `COCRDUPC` does place the cursor on the card-name field after a successful fetch `[app/cbl/COCRDUPC.cbl:L1210-1235]` — only the scrolling was wrong. All nine program-driven placements route through the one primitive | A page that calls `el.focus()` directly reintroduces the pan. Verified with three anti-vacuity controls: the layout genuinely requires a 156.98 px pan, `HTMLElement.prototype.focus` was confirmed NATIVE and a raw `el.focus()` still panned 71 px, and a refusal fired while panned to x=215 left the view at exactly 215 |
| The sign-on screen's visible in-body `Sign-on` button was removed, leaving a hidden implicit-submit shim | Keep the visible button; remove it and bind submission only to the key handler | `COSGN00.bms` publishes `ENTER=Sign-on  F3=Exit` and no body control, so the button was undeclared output — the same class as the delete button in section 47.7. The hidden shim is necessary rather than decorative: a two-field form has no implicit submission in HTML without a submit control, so removing it outright would have broken the Enter key. The shim is `hidden`, `aria-hidden` and `tabIndex={-1}`, so it is not rendered, not announced and not a tab stop | A raw `querySelectorAll('button')` finds three on that screen and two non-hidden; zero are in the body. Recorded because a probe counting buttons will otherwise read this as a leftover |
| The report's claim that `#tranDesc` is a fifty-character field is a FACTUAL ERROR in the report and was not acted on | Clamp the field to fifty characters as the finding implies | `COTRN02.bms` declares `LENGTH=60` at `[app/bms/COTRN02.bms:L161-164]`, so sixty is the field width and `maxLength=60` is correct. Clamping to fifty would truncate a description the legacy screen accepts | None to the application. Recorded so the finding is not re-raised from the report rather than from the mapset |

### 47.9 The error-response contract and adversarial input

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| Every failure — handled, refused, firewalled or container-level — answers with the SAME nine members of `ErrorResponse`, and an unavailable member is written as an explicit `null` rather than omitted; `@JsonInclude(NON_NULL)` was considered and deliberately NOT added | Add `NON_NULL` so absent members disappear; let each producer emit what it has | Four components can answer a failure and they were emitting four shapes: the exception handler's full envelope, an entry point and access-denied handler that emitted NO BODY AT ALL (`content-length: 0`), a hand-written refusal envelope that omitted `fieldErrors`, and a container valve that omitted `correlationId`. A reader cannot treat that as a contract. Suppressing nulls would make the shape vary with what happened to be available, which is precisely the property that made four producers look like four contracts — so absence is stated rather than hidden. Verified across seven producers on the running stack: domain `404`, framework `404`, firewall `400`, container `400`, `401`, `403` and a field-marked validation `400` all carry the identical member set | Jackson orders POJO members alphabetically while the hand-written producers use declaration order. JSON objects are unordered, so no reader is affected; the two hand-written producers pin their order by test so a member cannot be dropped unnoticed |
| `RateLimitFilter` answers `429` with the shared envelope alongside `Retry-After`, replacing a zero-byte body; both it and the firewall handler go through a `writeOutsideSecurityChain` entry point that also supplies `Cache-Control: no-store` and `X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff`, and `Retry-After` is set BEFORE the body because writing the body commits the response | Leave the `429` empty and rely on `Retry-After` alone; narrow the one-shape claim in the row above to exclude the `429` | Found while running the full integration sweep, not raised by the report: a test asserting an empty `429` body revealed an EIGHTH producer of a failure response, which would have made the one-shape claim above false as written. A throttled operator with no body sees the same blank screen a zero-byte `401` produced, and `Retry-After` is a header no 3270 screen can display — so the sentence is what tells them waiting is the remedy. The message names nothing about the budget, the window or the source address | The envelope adds a body to a path that exists to shed load; it is a fixed ~260-byte document with no database or session access. The refusal still discloses nothing: `RateLimitFilterTest` asserts the client address does not appear in it. Both outside-the-chain paths were observed carrying ONLY the correlation id before this change — no cache directive and no sniffing guard, because `HeaderWriterFilter` never runs on either — which is why supplying them there cannot weaken anything |
| A firewall rejection is answered by a `RequestRejectedHandler` installed through a `WebSecurityCustomizer`, NOT by a servlet filter wrapping the security chain | Wrap `FilterChainProxy` in a `OncePerRequestFilter` and catch `RequestRejectedException`; publish a `RequestRejectedHandler` bean and expect it to be wired; leave the container's error dispatch to answer | The filter was written first and PROVEN DEAD at runtime: `GET //accounts` returned the reduced body while the filter logged nothing and the access-log filter OUTSIDE it logged the `400`. `FilterChainProxy` catches the rejection itself and delegates to the installed handler; the default one calls `sendError`, which defers the body to Tomcat's ERROR dispatch — run after the chain has unwound, i.e. after the correlation-id filter cleared the MDC, which is exactly why the refusal carried no message and no correlation id. Completing the response in the handler keeps it inside the request scope where those ids still exist. A bean alone is not enough: no framework code autowires a `RequestRejectedHandler` (only `WebSecurity`, `FilterChainProxy` and the two composite proxies reference it), so the customizer is required plumbing rather than ceremony | The customizer must exist for the handler to have any effect at all — a handler bean with no customizer is silently inert. `RequestRejectedEnvelopeHandlerTest` asserts both beans and that a service-supplied handler replaces the shared one and is still the one installed |
| `RefusalEnvelopeWriter` authors NO `Cache-Control` directive; the firewall handler sets `no-store` itself | Set `no-store` in the writer; set it only when absent; set nothing anywhere | `CacheControlHeadersWriter` returns IMMEDIATELY if any of `Cache-Control`, `Expires` or `Pragma` is already present, and Spring Security writes its headers LAZILY at commit time — so a bare `no-store` set before the flush SUPPRESSED the entire hardened triple (`no-cache, no-store, max-age=0, must-revalidate` plus `Pragma: no-cache` plus `Expires: 0`). A containment check on the writer does not help either, because at that moment nothing has been written yet. Inside the chain the security writer is the right author; the firewall path is the one place no writer runs, so the handler supplies the directive there, where it cannot suppress a stronger one | The asymmetry (one caller sets it, two do not) is only correct while the security chain owns the other two paths. Both properties are pinned: the writer must author none and overwrite none, the handler must set `no-store`, and `SecurityConfigTest` asserts the full triple survives on a real `401` |
| nginx declares the response security headers in each location that serves a response FROM THIS CONTAINER, and NOWHERE at the server level; `location /api/` declares none | Declare them once at the server level and let locations inherit; re-declare them in `/api/` as before | nginx's `add_header` is inherited by a location ONLY when that location declares none of its own, so a server-level set is not a default that locations extend — it is a set any location silently opts out of by adding one header. The original file declared them in `/api/` AND at the server level, so the gateway's own headers arrived DOUBLED, with two disagreeing `Permissions-Policy` values. Deleting them from `/api/` made it worse: the location then inherited the server set, including the SPA's permissive `default-src 'self'` CSP appended after the gateway's `default-src 'none'`. Emptying the server level and declaring the full set per content-serving location is the only arrangement where `/api/` both declares nothing and inherits nothing. Verified on the wire: exactly one of each header on a `401`, CSP `default-src 'none'`, while the SPA document, a real root file and the hashed assets all keep the full set | The set is now written in three places and can drift. A future location that serves content must declare it too or be unprotected — the failure is visible on the wire rather than silent. Noted and left alone: `/assets/` emits two `Cache-Control` lines (`max-age=31536000` from `expires 1y` and `public, immutable` from `add_header`), which HTTP combines and which agree in intent |
| `api-gateway` and `batch-service` now `@Import` `CardDemoErrorController` and `ContainerErrorReportConfig`, which the other eight services already did | Leave the edge on Boot's `BasicErrorController`; move both into the auto-configuration import file | The gateway is the ONLY module a browser talks to, and it was the one module with no shared error controller — so a container-level dispatch at the edge answered Boot's abbreviated `{timestamp,status,error,path}` document while the same failure behind the gateway answered the full envelope. Moving them into auto-configuration was rejected as a wider change than the finding needs: the eight siblings already import them explicitly, and one consistent mechanism is worth more than a shorter list | The import list is the mechanism, so a NEW service must remember both. A container-level `400` still carries a null `correlationId` because Tomcat's connector rejects such requests during request-line parsing, upstream of every filter — genuinely unavailable rather than omitted |
| A framework `404` reports the fixed sentence `The requested resource was not found`; the framework's own detail is suppressed for `404` ONLY | Suppress the detail for every status; keep the detail as it was | The detail read `No static resource accounts/...`, which fingerprints the framework and tells a prober how the request was interpreted. Suppressing it for every status was rejected because other statuses' details are USEFUL and harmless: a `405` carries the `Allow` set and a `415` names the acceptable media types. Domain `404`s are untouched — `RecordNotFoundException` keeps its verbatim legacy literals, so `Did not find this account in account card xref file` still reaches the operator | The fixed sentence is not a legacy literal, because no legacy program answers an unmapped URL. It is the minimum an HTTP client needs |
| Inbound and outbound JSON strings are NFC-composed and stripped of every Unicode FORMAT (Cf) character; CONTROL (Cc) characters are deliberately LEFT IN PLACE | Strip all non-printable characters including controls; reject a request containing them; normalise on the way in only | A bidi override genuinely reverses rendered text — `test\u202eevil` displayed as `testlive` and passed validation — and zero-width spaces are invisible and were accepted, so a stored value could read as something other than what it is. Stripping FORMAT characters removes that class entirely. Stripping CONTROL characters would have DEFEATED a validation rule: a tab in the transaction-id filter must still fail `IS NUMERIC` and produce `Tran ID must be Numeric ...` (section 47.6), and a stripped tab looks like an empty field and browses from the start. Rejecting instead of stripping would produce a refusal literal no legacy program has. Normalising OUTBOUND as well covers values that entered through Flyway seeds or a fixed-width batch feed, which the inbound path never sees | The normaliser is a boundary-wide behaviour, so a future field that legitimately needs a Cf character cannot have one. None exists: every field is a `PIC X(n)` display field. Runtime-verified with an anti-vacuity control — the payload was proven live (it rendered `testlive` on the add form and the captured request body contained the raw escapes) and read back clean on three surfaces, with the control cases still refused |
| The focus indicator is unified to `1px solid var(--term-green)` at `outline-offset: 1px` on `:focus` — not `:focus-visible` — and the yellow two-pixel `button:focus-visible` rule was deleted | Keep the button rule and unify the inputs to match it; keep `:focus-visible` for buttons and `:focus` for inputs | A 3270 has ONE hardware cursor with one appearance, so two indicators for two control kinds is a distinction the terminal does not make. `:focus-visible` is wrong here for a specific reason: the cursor is placed by the PROGRAM as often as by the operator, and after a pointer interaction `:focus-visible` does not match — so a program-placed cursor would be invisible. Verified on the real path: after a MOUSE CLICK on `F4=Clear` the program moved the cursor to the first field and the ring was still shown. Runtime-verified across 32 focusable controls on three screens, all reporting one tuple, with zero yellow pixels in any rendered ring and zero `focus-visible` rules in the shipped bundle | `:focus` also shows the ring when a control is focused by a pointer, which some style guides discourage. On this application that is the desired behaviour, because the ring IS the terminal cursor |
| Heading levels: the shell renders `h1` and `h2`, every page body renders `h3`, and no page carries an `h2` of its own | Give each page an `h2` so its body heading is `h3` by inheritance; promote page headings to `h2` | The shell's `h1` and `h2` are the mapset's own first two title lines, present on every screen, so a page-level `h2` would be a third title the source does not have. With the shell always contributing both levels, a body `h3` never skips. Verified on five screens: every one runs `h1` then `h2` then `h3` (then `h4` where the account screens declare a sub-heading), maximum level jump 1, and no page has an `h3` without an `h2` — so the reported "`h3` with no `h2`" no longer holds | The outline depends on the shell always rendering both levels; a screen that suppressed the chrome would skip. No screen does |
| Zero transition durations are retained everywhere | Add short transitions to focus, hover and message changes | A 3270 repaints instantaneously; an animated transition is observable output the source does not produce (AAP 0.7.2). Recorded because a measurement pass will keep reporting `transition-duration: 0s` as a finding | None. The absence is the specification |


### 47.10 Correction — the two BMS message fields

A regression pass on the account screens found the two message rows rendering in the wrong ORDER and the informational row in the wrong COLOUR. Both traced to one cause: `ErrorBanner` used a single prop for two different BMS fields. The rows below record the split, the two corrections it enabled, a second defect on the card-update screen found while verifying the first, and two earlier notes in this log that this work proved wrong. The stale rows in section 4 and section 47.3 have been annotated in place rather than rewritten, so the original reasoning and its correction are both readable.

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|---|---|---|---|
| `ErrorBanner` takes TWO informational props, one per BMS field: `infoMessage` for text a program writes into the line-23 `ERRMSG` field itself, and `infoFieldMessage` for the separate `INFOMSG` field five mapsets declare | Keep one `infoMessage` prop and a boolean discriminator; keep one prop and infer the field from the route; keep one prop and render every informational message the same way | They are two different display fields with different positions, widths and colours, and the single prop was conflating them — which is what produced both the inverted order and the wrong colour. Eight programs green the `ERRMSG` field itself (`COADM01C` L148, `COMEN01C` L158, `COBIL00C` L526, `CORPT00C` L448, `COTRN02C` L727, `COUSR01C` L254, `COUSR02C` L371, `COUSR03C` L317) and NONE of those eight declares an `INFOMSG` field; the five that declare one never move `DFHGREEN` anywhere. The split is therefore exact, not a heuristic: the five pages needing `infoFieldMessage` are precisely the five whose mapsets declare the field. A boolean discriminator was rejected because a page that forgot to set it would silently get the other field's colour and position, and roughly half the screens would need it either way | Two props whose names differ by one word. Mitigated by naming the BMS field in both docstrings and by the page-level tests now asserting the FIELD (`.errorBanner--infoField`) rather than the role |
| The `INFOMSG` row renders ABOVE the line-23 `ERRMSG` row | Keep the shipped order (error above informational); place the informational row at its absolute mapset row with blank rows between | Every one of the five maps places `INFOMSG` at a LOWER row number than `ERRMSG` at `POS=(23,1)`: `COACTUP.bms` and `COACTVW.bms` at `POS=(22,23)`, `COCRDLI.bms` at `POS=(20,19)`, `COCRDSL.bms` and `COCRDUP.bms` at `POS=(20,25)`. The programs confirm it in their write order — `COACTUPC` L2979-2981 moves `WS-INFO-MSG` to `INFOMSGO` and THEN `WS-RETURN-MSG` to `ERRMSGO`. The shipped order contradicted the mapsets outright | The three card maps place `INFOMSG` at row 20, two rows clear of `ERRMSG`, and the frame renders the region in flow rather than at absolute rows, so the informational line sits immediately above the error rather than two rows above it. Reproducing the absolute offset would require rendering the intervening blank rows, which no mapset field occupies. Recorded because a row-exact comparison will see the difference |
| The `INFOMSG` row is NEUTRAL (`--term-neutral`, `#e6e6e6`), not GREEN | Leave it green as shipped; colour it from a page-supplied value | All five maps declare the field `COLOR=NEUTRAL`, and the programs confirm that colour at RUNTIME rather than merely inheriting it: `COCRDLIC` L929, `COACTVWC` L570 and `COCRDSLC` L556 each `MOVE DFHNEUTR TO INFOMSGC` when the line is shown and `DFHBMDAR` when it is not, while `COACTUPC` and `COCRDUPC` manipulate only the attribute byte (`INFOMSGA`), leaving the declared `NEUTRAL` in force. None of the five moves `DFHGREEN` anywhere in its source. Green was inherited from the `.errorBanner[role="status"]` rule written for the OTHER twelve screens | None. Verified at runtime as the literal computed `rgb(230, 230, 230)` in every state on all five screens, corroborated by exact-pixel counts and by zero pixels of `rgb(51, 255, 102)` inside either message row |
| The `INFOMSG` row is RESERVED even when the field is blank: it renders as an empty row with no `role`, and the row is reserved by the page supplying the prop at all — an empty string still renders it | Render the row only when it has text; omit the prop on screens whose field is always dark | A darkened `INFOMSG` field still occupies its row on a 24-row device, and the programs darken it (`DFHBMDAR`) rather than removing it. Reserving the row also removes a layout shift that has always existed on these screens — the message region grew from one row to two as the second message appeared — which is the same class of defect as the PF-bar shift already fixed for finding F81. The `role` is omitted while blank because an empty live region is a reader-only artefact of reserving space | It costs exactly one text line on the five screens: 15.594px at 375px, 18.188px at 768px, 20.797px at 1280px and above. Re-measured across all twenty (viewport x screen) combinations — the function-key bar stays at or above the fold everywhere, worst clearance +94.375px, and at 768px the frame did not grow at all because `main.screen__body` (`flex: 1 1 auto`, `min-height: 345.8px`) absorbed the row, leaving ~449px of further shrink headroom. `AccountViewPage` supplies the prop as a constant empty string because `COACTVW` declares the field at `POS=(22,23)` even though `COACTVWC` never `SET`s any of its `WS-INFO-MSG` 88 levels, so the field is permanently dark |
| The three announced message-line colours are made mutually exclusive by SELECTOR, so each wins on specificity: the green rule is guarded `:not(.errorBanner--notice):not(.errorBanner--infoField)`, and the notice and info-field rules each name two classes to out-specify the base red | Leave all three at equal specificity and rely on CSS source order, as shipped | All three variants matched at (0,2,0) and the colours were decided by rule order alone. That is a property no one would notice was load-bearing until a change to emission order silently reverted the green defect this section exists to fix — and the fix was won on exactly that order. Guarding the selectors makes each variant's colour hold wherever its rule sits | None. A six-variant model confirms every variant now resolves to a unique winner by specificity alone, and the runtime probe reports the green rule NO LONGER MATCHES either the notice row or the info-field row |
| `CardUpdatePage`'s informational line is NEVER blank: each of its seven previously-blank states now publishes the literal `3250-SETUP-INFOMSG` selects for that state | Leave the line blank whenever an error is raised, as shipped; blank it only for some errors | `COCRDUPC` 3250-SETUP-INFOMSG has no blank branch — all eight `WHEN`s select a literal and the final `WHEN WS-NO-INFO-MESSAGE` guarantees `PROMPT-FOR-SEARCH-KEYS` as a fallback — so the field always carries text once the screen has been sent. Three of the seven mappings are not what they look like and were each read off the source: the no-change comparison `GO TO`es `1200-EDIT-MAP-INPUTS-EXIT` at L691 BEFORE L696 `SET CCUP-CHANGES-NOT-OK`, so the state stays `CCUP-SHOW-DETAILS` and the line reads `Details of selected card shown above`, not `Update card details presented above.`; L997-998 answers `DATA-WAS-CHANGED-BEFORE-UPDATE` with `SET CCUP-SHOW-DETAILS` rather than a failure state, so a 409 reads the same; and `INFORM-FAILURE` is declared under `WS-INFO-MSG` (L170-171), so it belongs on the informational line and not on `ERRMSG` where it had been placed | The empty entry screen now states `Please enter Account and Card Number` where it previously said nothing. That is the `CDEMO-PGM-ENTER` branch and it matches the sibling `/cards/view` entry screen, which already showed that literal |
| Six `CardUpdatePage` tests that asserted the informational line was ABSENT were rewritten to assert the literal the COBOL publishes | Keep the assertions and leave the line blank; delete the assertions | Each had encoded the defect as the expectation — `expect(infoBanner()).toBeNull()` in precisely the states `3250-SETUP-INFOMSG` populates. Tests align to the source, never the reverse. The two `infoBanner()` helpers were also tightened from `.errorBanner[role="status"]` to `.errorBanner--infoField[role="status"]`, because the loose selector matched the GREEN `ERRMSG` variant too and so could not have caught the field being confused in the first place | This is the fifth time in this checkpoint that a declared-looking expectation encoded a defect rather than the source. The guard against it is a citation per assertion, which these six now carry |
| `/reports` pre-selects NO report type — recorded as a correction to an earlier note in this log, not as a change | Pre-select `MONTHLY` to match the note | `CORPT00.bms` declares `MONTHLY`, `YEARLY` and `CUSTOM` each with `INITIAL=' '`, and `CORPT00C` L636-638 `INITIALIZE`s all three, so none is selected on entry. `MONTHLY` carries the `IC` attribute, which places the CURSOR there — that is why a measurement pass reports it focused and reads that as selected. The shipped behaviour is faithful and pressing ENTER with nothing chosen correctly yields `Select a report type to print report...` | None. Recorded because the mistaken reading has now been made twice from the focus ring alone |


## 48. QA Remediation — Deployment Resilience, Outage Contract, Workstation Topology and Operational Readiness

Runtime QA of the complete deployment / configuration / observability surface raised eleven
findings (2 critical, 2 major, 6 minor, 1 informational). The two critical ones — `batch-service`
aborting its context on duplicate `@Bean` method names, and `transaction-service` /
`reporting-service` crash-looping on a duplicate `jobRepository` definition — had already been
resolved by the acceptance-gate pass that removed the three per-service `BatchInfrastructureConfig`
classes and the duplicated listener block; this pass proved that at runtime (a clean
`docker compose up -d` now exits 0 with all nine services healthy and no bean-override flag
anywhere) rather than changing code again. The decisions below are the ones the remaining nine
findings forced.

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| nginx resolves the `api-gateway` upstream PER REQUEST, through a `resolver` generated at container start from the container's own `/etc/resolv.conf` by `/docker-entrypoint.d/15-carddemo-resolver.sh`, with `proxy_pass` naming a VARIABLE and `rewrite ^/api/(.*)$ /$1 break` preserving the prefix strip | (a) Hard-code `resolver 127.0.0.11` (Docker's embedded DNS) in the committed nginx.conf; (b) leave the literal `proxy_pass` and keep documenting "recreate the frontend after recreating the gateway"; (c) `depends_on: service_healthy` alone | nginx resolves a literal name in `proxy_pass` ONCE, when the configuration loads, so a gateway recreated with a different address was black-holed until the frontend container was recreated too — measured as an A/B on one network with the gateway forced from 172.23.0.8 to 172.23.0.18: the fixed container answered 200/200/200 while a control built from the previous configuration answered 502/502, with the gateway itself answering 200 directly. Option (a) was rejected because the SAME nginx.conf runs in Kubernetes, where 127.0.0.11 is dead and every `/api` call would fail; deriving the resolver from resolv.conf is correct in both topologies. Option (b) leaves a documented outage in the browser tier for every gateway rollout | The include is a WILDCARD (`/tmp/carddemo-nginx/*.conf`), which nginx treats as empty rather than as an error, so the container still starts if the hook is ever bypassed — verified with `nginx -t` both with and without the generated file. The generated file lives on the tmpfs both topologies already mount, because the root filesystem is read-only |
| The batch working tmpfs keeps its EXPLICIT `mode=1777` in the Compose short form; no application or image change | (a) Long-form `volumes: [{type: tmpfs, tmpfs: {mode: …}}]`; (b) create `/var/carddemo/batch{,/output}` in each Dockerfile owned by the runtime uid; (c) default `CARDDEMO_BATCH_OUTPUT_DIR` under `/tmp` | Measured on this daemon with three probes of the same image under `--user 1000:1000 --read-only`: a BARE `--tmpfs /var/carddemo/batch` is 1777 on first start and **0755 root:root after `docker restart`**, so `mkdir` fails and the context aborts — the reported failure; the same mount WITH `mode=1777`, and the long form, stay 1777 across restarts. Docker re-applies an explicit mode on every mount creation and only defaults it on creation, so the committed short form is already correct. Option (b) cannot help by itself: the tmpfs mount hides whatever the image created underneath it | The dependency is on the mode being STATED. This row exists so the option is not "simplified" back to the bare form; two restart rounds of transaction, reporting and batch services, plus a posting job that wrote its 16,378-byte `dalyrejs.txt` afterwards, are the regression evidence |
| Runtime `VITE_*` environment entries are REMOVED from the frontend service in `docker-compose.yml` and from `k8s/deployment-frontend.yaml`; the build args remain the only mechanism | Keep them as documentation of the contract; convert them into a genuine runtime mechanism (an entrypoint writing `/usr/share/nginx/html/config.js`) | Vite bakes `import.meta.env.VITE_*` into the static bundle at build time, so nothing in the nginx runtime reads those variables: an operator could change them, see no effect and get no error. Proven both ways — the running container now exposes no `VITE_` variable at all, while a container started with `VITE_API_BASE_URL=http://evil.example.com` still serves the baked ``return`/api` `` (0 occurrences of the override in the bundle). A runtime mechanism was rejected as a behaviour change nobody asked for; the honest fix is to stop offering a knob that does nothing | To change the API base or the AppID you must rebuild the image with the build arg and roll out the new tag. Both manifests now say so in place |
| A datastore outage that escapes the Spring dispatcher is answered by a filter — `DatastoreOutageErrorFilter`, registered by the auto-configured `WebObservabilityConfig` at `HIGHEST_PRECEDENCE + 20` — with `503` and the documented `ErrorResponse` envelope | (a) An `ErrorPage` / `HandlerExceptionResolver` for the failure; (b) set `spring.session.servlet.filter-dispatcher-types=request` so the ERROR dispatch skips the session filter; (c) accept the container's status page | Spring Session's `SessionRepositoryFilter` runs at `Integer.MIN_VALUE + 50`, ahead of the dispatcher AND of Spring Security, so with Redis down the `QueryTimeoutException` never reached `@RestControllerAdvice`; the container's ERROR dispatch then re-entered the same failing filter, which is why neither the advice nor `CardDemoErrorController` could render and the caller received a 455-byte Tomcat HTML page. Only a filter OUTSIDE the failing one can answer, and ordering it inside `CorrelationIdFilter` is what keeps the MDC populated — the outage log line now carries the correlationId and traceId that were `null`. Options (a) and (b) both still route through the ERROR dispatch. `resetBuffer()` rather than `reset()` is used so the correlation and security headers already written survive | Only `DataAccessException` is converted, so an application defect can never be reported as an outage; when the response is already committed the failure is re-thrown rather than the body truncated. A statement the datastore REJECTED still answers 500 — only an unreachable datastore is a 503 |
| Each service's DEFAULT-profile `server.port` becomes its workstation port (auth 8081 … batch 8088), and the container port is pinned where that topology is defined: `SERVER_PORT=8080` in the Compose shared environment and a new `SERVER_PORT` key in `k8s/configmap.yaml` | (a) Change the gateway's default route URIs to the Compose DNS names, so only container topologies work; (b) leave the mismatch documented; (c) publish per-service host ports | Every service pinned `server.port: 8080` in its default document while the gateway's default route table addressed `localhost:8081`-`8088`, so a plain `mvn spring-boot:run` estate collided on one port and the gateway's own defaults reached nothing — the file was internally inconsistent. Aligning the file defaults with the route table makes the workstation topology work with no environment variable at all, verified end to end: auth-service bound 8081, account-service 8083, and a gateway started with no profile and no `*_SERVICE_URI` signed on and read an account through its DEFAULT routes. Option (a) would have deleted a supported topology instead of repairing it | The k8s ConfigMap key is now load-bearing: without it pods would bind 8081-8088 while every containerPort, probe, targetPort and scrape target says 8080. It is stated in place, and the Compose render was re-checked to be byte-identical at 8080 for all nine components |
| The seven `await-schema` init containers are bounded at requests `10m`/`32Mi`, limits `100m`/`64Mi` | Leave them unbounded and rely on a namespace LimitRange; give them the app containers' figures | An unbounded container in a namespace without a LimitRange is admitted with no QoS guarantee and can be scheduled anywhere, and these seven were the only containers in the repository without a `resources` block. The figures were not guessed: the exact image and command from the rendered manifest were run under precisely those limits against the live schema owner (exit 0, "schema owner ready") and against an unreachable one for 40 s (`oomkilled=false`, 2.016 MiB of 64 MiB, 0.25% CPU) | A future probe that does more than one TCP readiness check per five seconds would need the figures revisited; the audit that proved 0 findings over 14 app and 7 init containers is re-runnable |
| Backup and recovery is delivered as a documented procedure plus a Kubernetes CronJob, not as application code | Application-level export endpoints; a sidecar; nothing at all (AAP 0.4.6 places it out of application-code scope) | The legacy continuity obligation (`app/jcl/DEFGDGB.jcl` defined the generation-data group, `TRANBKP.jcl` wrote the daily generation) has to land SOMEWHERE, and AAP 0.4.6 maps it to database backup, so it belongs in the deployment layer. The procedure is proven rather than described: an application write (bill payment, balance to 0.00, transaction `0000001996722788`), `pg_dump -Fc`, a simulated loss visible through the API (balance −99999.99, transaction 404), `pg_restore --clean --if-exists`, and the API view restored to 0.00 with the transaction 200 — zero loss to the backup point. The CronJob's own rendered script was then executed against the live database under its declared image and limits | Point-in-time recovery is NOT provided: the server runs `archive_mode=off`, so the recovery point is the last dump. That is stated in README-target.md together with what enabling PITR would require, rather than being left for an operator to discover during an incident |
| Prometheus alerting rules are added even though AAP 0.7.5 does not mandate them, and no Alertmanager is configured | Add rules and an Alertmanager; add nothing, since the mandate is satisfied by the dashboard | The mandate lists structured logging, tracing, a metrics endpoint, probes and a dashboard template — all delivered — but a dashboard only reports to someone who is looking at it. Eight rules now cover the conditions the dashboard's own panels plot, and every threshold is a number the project already commits to (the 200 ms p95 target and the 14400 s batch window of AAP 0.7.1), never an invented one. Wiring a notification route would be a deployment decision this reference stack does not make | Rules are evaluated and exposed on `/api/v1/rules` and `/api/v1/alerts` but dispatched nowhere until an Alertmanager is attached. They are proven neither always- nor never-firing: stopping a service drove `CardDemoServiceDown` to pending in 15 s and firing at 90 s, and starting it cleared the alert within 30 s |


## 49. QA Runtime-Testing Remediation — Batch Deliverable Durability, Edge Resilience, Log Hygiene and Single Schema Ownership

This section records the non-trivial choices made while resolving the 21 findings of the runtime
QA pass. Several findings were already closed by earlier commits and were re-verified rather than
re-decided; the entries below cover only where a decision was actually taken.

### 49.1 Batch deliverable durability and restart semantics

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| The `DALYREJS` reject file is written as a PER-RUN GENERATION, `dalyrejs.G0042V00.txt`, with the generation number taken from the Spring Batch job execution id | Keep one fixed name and append rather than truncate; timestamp the name; keep the fixed name and archive the previous copy in the cleanup listener | `POSTTRAN.jcl` allocates the deliverable as `DSN=AWS.M2.CARDDEMO.DALYREJS(+1)` with `DISP=(NEW,CATLG,DELETE)` — a NEW generation-data-group generation per run. A single fixed name is not a naming detail but a data-loss defect against that contract: the writer opens with delete-if-exists, so every run destroyed the previous run's rejects, and a failing run's cleanup deleted a file it had not written. Appending was rejected because it merges two business days into one deliverable, which no downstream reader of a 430-byte reject file expects. The execution id was chosen over a timestamp because it is unique for all time, orders runs monotonically, and is directly resolvable back to a `BATCH_JOB_EXECUTION` row, so an operator holding a generation can find its run | The name changes shape, so anything matching the exact literal `dalyrejs.txt` must match the stem instead; the repository's existing `.gitignore` pattern `dalyrejs*.txt` already covers it. A five-digit execution id widens the field rather than wrapping onto an earlier generation |
| A FAILING run deletes only the generation IT created, and never any other | Keep the deliverable on failure; delete the whole output directory | This is exactly `DISP=(NEW,CATLG,DELETE)`: the generation a run creates is discarded when that run abends, so a half-written reject file is never mistaken for a complete one, while every previously cataloged generation survives untouched. Making both the writer and the cleanup listener `@StepScope` and deriving both paths from the same `stepExecution.jobExecutionId` is what guarantees they address the same file — a shared singleton listener could only ever hold one name | A run that fails after writing rejects leaves no reject artifact to inspect; the rejected records remain in the durable feed table and the reject reasons are in the step's log, so the diagnosis is not lost |
| The reject writer opens with `shouldDeleteIfExists(true)` and `saveState(false)` | Keep Spring Batch's saved write offset so a restart appends from where it stopped | A restart of a failed instance had been impossible: the writer demanded the saved offset of a file its own cleanup had just deleted, so the restart failed on the file rather than on whatever had actually gone wrong. A restart is a NEW execution and therefore a new generation, so there is no prior offset to honour — the correct behaviour is to open the new generation fresh. Verified end to end: an execution failed with the destination unavailable, and re-submitting the same identifying `postingDate` produced a second execution on the SAME job instance that completed | Chunk-level restart of the FILE is therefore not resumed — the restarted execution rewrites its own generation in full. The database side is unaffected and remains exactly-once through `transactions_pkey` |
| An empty `DALYTRAN` feed COMPLETES with return code 0 and the legacy zero tallies | Keep the return-code-12 failure; complete with return code 4; make it configurable | `CBTRN02C` (L202-L234) simply ends its read loop on the first read, DISPLAYs both tallies, and raises `RETURN-CODE` to 4 only when `WS-REJECT-COUNT > 0`; return code 12 belongs to the `OPEN`/`READ` failure paths that ABEND the program. Failing the step on an empty feed therefore paged an operator for a no-transaction business day — a functional-equivalence break, and the more damaging kind, because it trains operators to ignore the alert. The condition is still reported, at WARN, so an absent feed stays visible without being an incident | An operator who relied on the job failing to detect a missing feed delivery must now watch for the WARN or for the zero tally. Verified: an empty feed completed with exit code `COMPLETED`, step counters all zero, and the verbatim `TRANSACTIONS PROCESSED :0` / `TRANSACTIONS REJECTED  :0` displays |
| A clean run's generation is CATALOGED even when it is empty (a zero-byte reject file) | Delete a zero-byte reject file after a clean run | `DISP=(NEW,CATLG,DELETE)` catalogs the generation on normal termination regardless of how many records were written, so an empty generation is the faithful outcome and its presence is itself the evidence that the run reached the end. Deleting it would make "no rejects" indistinguishable from "the job never got that far" | A reader that treats file existence as "there were rejects" must check the size instead |

### 49.2 Launch-parameter validation — refuse before an instance exists

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| `postingDate` is validated against the shared `DateUtil` with `MASK_ISO` and STRICT resolution, and a bad value is refused BEFORE the job operator is touched | Accept whatever is submitted; validate inside the job; coerce an unparseable value to today | `postingDate` is the IDENTIFYING job parameter: it is the instance key of a posting cycle AND it is persisted verbatim in `BATCH_JOB_EXECUTION_PARAMS` as the audit record of the run. Unvalidated, any caller could mint unlimited instances that re-post the same feed under names like `NOT-A-DATE` or an injection string, and the batch audit trail carried them permanently. `CSUTLDTC` is the legacy date validator (AAP 0.6.6) and STRICT resolution is what makes `2026-02-30` a refusal rather than a silent roll into March. Coercion was rejected because it would post a cycle under a date the operator did not ask for. The refusal quotes neither the submitted value nor any internal detail, so a hostile string is never reflected back | A caller previously able to submit a free-form label can no longer do so. Verified: seven hostile values each returned `400` and the audit table contained none of them. NOTE for future readers: `DateUtil.isValid(String)` — the single-argument overload — validates `YYYYMMDD`, so the ISO mask MUST be named explicitly; the first attempt at this fix used the overload and would have refused every legitimate hyphenated date, making the flagship financial job unlaunchable |
| `stmtFile` and `htmlFile` are resolved against the batch output root SYNCHRONOUSLY in the launcher, and an unusable name is refused with the resolver's own message | Leave the containment check where it was, inside the step-scoped writer factory; sanitize the name silently; substitute the default | The containment rule was already enforced, but only later, inside the writer factory — so a traversal attempt received `202 ACCEPTED`, a `BATCH_JOB_EXECUTION` row was created for a run that could never produce a statement, and the run then failed with a `BeanCreationException` whose stack trace was persisted into `exit_message` as the operator-visible outcome. Validating at the call site refuses it the way every other launch refusal is refused and writes nothing to the batch metadata. Silent substitution was rejected because an operator who names an output file must be told the name was not used | The refusal surfaces the resolver's message, which names the rejected VALUE — deliberate, so an operator can see which parameter was wrong, and safe because the message contains nothing internal. Verified: four traversal values each returned `400` and the statement execution count was unchanged |
| Every step-scoped file-name job parameter carries its property-backed default ON THE BINDING (`#{jobParameters['x'] ?: '${carddemo.batch.…:default}'}`) rather than only in the HTTP launcher | Keep the default in the launcher and require every caller to pass the parameter; give each job a `JobParametersIncrementer` | The default had lived only in the HTTP launch path, so any OTHER launch path failed. Measured: launching by `--spring.batch.job.name=…`, which is exactly how `k8s/cronjob-*.yaml` runs a job, failed 8 of 9 jobs with `BeanCreationException … Batch file path must not be blank` before the writer opened. A default on the binding holds for every launch path by construction, which is the property the scheduled route needs | Because a shared listener bean can carry only ONE default, the single `outputFileCleanupListener` had to become six per-destination `@StepScope` listeners; that is more beans, but each one names the destination it cleans |

### 49.3 Authorization of the financial batch surface

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| `/transactions/batch/**` requires `ROLE_ADMIN` at the gateway AND again in transaction-service's own filter chain | Gate at the gateway only; gate in the service only; leave it with the online transaction surface | `CBTRN02C` had no CICS transaction at all — the job stream was submitted to JES by an operator, and the run POSTS FINANCIAL MOVEMENTS: it rewrites account balances and inserts transaction rows. A signed-on `ROLE_USER` reaching it is a privilege boundary crossed, not a UX detail. The prefix sits under `/transactions/**`, which the online inquiry and add screens (`COTRN00C`/`COTRN01C`/`COTRN02C`) legitimately open to `ROLE_USER`, so the carve-out has to be an ordered rule ahead of it. Enforcing in BOTH places means a caller that reaches the service directly on the cluster network is refused too — the edge is not the only control | Two places now express one rule and could drift; both are pinned by tests (`BatchLaunchRouteAuthorizationTest`, `PostingJobLaunchAuthorizationTest`). Verified at runtime including a direct container-network POST to `transaction-service:8080`, which a `ROLE_USER` session was refused |

### 49.4 Edge resilience and single-layer header emission

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| Upstream connect and read timeouts are set in the Boot 4.1 `spring.http.clients.*` namespace | Set a Spring Cloud Gateway timeout property; wrap routes in a circuit breaker; leave the JDK default | Spring Cloud Gateway Server WebMVC 5.0.2 exposes NO timeout property of its own — confirmed by reading the `spring-configuration-metadata.json` inside the shipped jar. Its proxy exchange is built from the Boot-managed `RestClient.Builder`, and its own `GatewayHttpClientEnvironmentPostProcessor` drives that same Boot namespace, so these keys are what actually bound a routed call. Without them the JDK client waits indefinitely: a FROZEN upstream (a paused container, a stop-the-world pause, a host black-holing packets) returned nothing while a gateway thread stayed pinned, and enough of those take the edge down. A refused connection was always answered promptly because the OS reports it immediately; only the frozen case hung | `read-timeout` is the ceiling for a whole routed response, so it must stay well above the slowest legitimate call while remaining a small multiple of the 200 ms p95 target. Note the singular `spring.http.client.*` is deprecated since Boot 4.0 and binds without effect. Verified: a paused upstream returned `503` in 10.03 s instead of a 90 s client abort |
| The browser security-header set is emitted at exactly ONE layer, and for proxied `/api/` traffic that layer is the gateway | Keep both emitters and accept duplicates; strip the gateway's headers and emit only at nginx; use `proxy_hide_header` | Two emitters produced two values for the same header on one response, and where the two disagreed — the `Permissions-Policy` did — the effective policy became whichever the browser resolved first, which is not a policy anyone chose. The gateway is the correct owner because it also serves API responses that never pass through nginx. The static document responses keep their own set, since nginx serves those alone | The mechanism is subtle and worth stating: nginx INHERITS a level's `add_header` set into any location that declares none of its own, so removing the `location /api/` block alone did NOT stop the duplication — the server-level set had to move down into the document locations. All three nginx `Permissions-Policy` values were also aligned to `SecurityHardening.PERMISSIONS_POLICY`. Verified: each of the five headers appears exactly once on `/api/csrf`, `/` and `/index.html` |

### 49.5 Log hygiene and one error contract for a dependency outage

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| Path masking masks a card-number-shaped segment ONLY where the preceding path segment is `cards`; free text keeps the broad PAN mask | Keep masking every 12-19 digit run in a URI; mask nothing in a path; mask by controller annotation | The broad rule was applied to whole URIs, so the 16-digit TRANSACTION id in `/transactions/0000000000683580` was masked too. That is not a harmless excess: an operator reading a log or an error body could no longer identify which transaction failed, which is the whole purpose of echoing the path. Enumerating every controller mapping showed `/cards/{cardNumber}` is the only path that carries a PAN, so segment position is a precise discriminator where digit-shape is not. Free text (exception messages, audit values) keeps the broad mask because position tells you nothing there | The `cards` segment is matched as a WHOLE segment, so a path such as `/discards/…` is untouched. If a future endpoint places a PAN elsewhere in a path it must be added deliberately; the alternative — masking everything — was already shown to destroy diagnosis. Verified: zero raw PANs across all ten containers, while `/transactions/{id}` and `/accounts/{id}` report verbatim |
| One shared message describes every dependency/infrastructure failure, in every surface that can report one | Keep the three different messages; expose the failing dependency in the message | The same Redis or PostgreSQL outage produced three different bodies depending on which surface answered — a data-access handler, an unexpected-error fallback, and the container's bare reason phrase — so a caller could not tell one incident from three unrelated ones. Naming the failing dependency was rejected: it tells an attacker the internal topology and tells a legitimate caller nothing actionable | The classification must be complete or the old message reappears. It very nearly was not: `TransactionException` (`CannotCreateTransactionException`, raised when `@Transactional` cannot open a connection) is NOT a `DataAccessException`, so every transactional read fell through to the generic fallback until a handler for it was added. Verified: all four DB-outage surfaces return the identical message |
| A session-store failure is answered INSIDE a dedicated servlet filter, which writes the JSON envelope itself | Give the error controller content negotiation; register an error page; catch it in the controller advice | Spring Session registers its `SessionRepositoryFilter` for the REQUEST, ERROR and ASYNC dispatcher types. So when Redis is down the request fails inside that filter, the container error-dispatches to `/error`, the dispatch RE-ENTERS the same filter, Redis fails a second time and Tomcat renders its stock HTML page — before any application handler is consulted. No amount of content negotiation on the error controller could fix that, because the second failure happens before it. Answering in a filter placed OUTSIDE Spring Session's filter but INSIDE the correlation filter avoids the error dispatch entirely, which is also what lets the response carry the real request path and the in-scope correlation id | Filter order is now load-bearing and is stated at the registration: after the correlation and request-logging filters, before Spring Session's. Verified: a Redis outage returns `503` with `Content-Type: application/json`, `Retry-After: 5`, the real path (`/menu`, `/accounts/{id}`, `/auth/signon`) rather than `/error`, and the inbound correlation id echoed |
| An unmapped sub-path returns a domain 404 by OVERRIDING the framework's `NoResourceFoundException` hook | Add a second `@ExceptionHandler` for it; let the framework message stand; add a catch-all controller | `NoResourceFoundException` is a framework `ErrorResponse`, so the unhandled-exception path preserved its 404 but copied its detail text — a caller asking for `/billpay/{id}` was told "No static resource billpay/…", which describes the framework's resource resolver rather than the API. Overriding the inherited hook is the supported extension point; a second `@ExceptionHandler` for the same type is an AMBIGUOUS mapping against `ResponseEntityExceptionHandler` and fails context startup, which is how the first attempt failed | The message is deliberately generic so it does not enumerate what does exist. Verified: `GET /billpay/{id}` returns the shared envelope with "The requested resource was not found", the real path, and both a correlation id and a trace id |

### 49.6 Cross-service trace continuity and a single schema owner

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| reporting-service takes an explicit dependency on `org.springframework.boot:spring-boot-restclient`, builds its outbound client from the MANAGED `RestClient.Builder`, and adds an interceptor for `X-Correlation-Id` | Keep `RestClient.builder()` and set an observation registry by hand; propagate the trace headers manually; accept the broken trace | `spring-boot-starter-web` ships the RestClient TYPE but not its auto-configuration, which in Spring Boot 4 lives in this separate module — and that module contributes both the managed builder AND `RestClientObservationAutoConfiguration`, the client instrumentation that writes `traceparent`. Built from a bare builder, the ONE hop in the estate that crosses a service boundary carried no trace context, so batch-service opened an unrelated trace for every launched run and AAP 0.7.5's cross-boundary tracing requirement was unmet precisely where it matters. `X-Correlation-Id` needs the interceptor because it is a BUSINESS identifier, not a tracing header, so no instrumentation carries it | Simply injecting the builder without the dependency fails the whole module at startup (`No qualifying bean of type RestClient$Builder`), which is how the gap was found — api-gateway had the module transitively via Spring Cloud Gateway and reporting-service did not. Verified twice over: both services log the identical `traceId` AND `correlationId` for one submission, and Jaeger shows a single 35-span trace spanning api-gateway → reporting-service → batch-service |
| The correlation header is SET, not set-if-absent, and is OMITTED entirely when no id is in scope | Always send a value, generating one if needed; use set-if-absent | Setting means a stale value can never survive onto a later request from a pooled thread. Omitting rather than sending an empty header matters because the receiving service's own filter adopts whatever arrives — an empty header would give the run a blank business identifier instead of a real one | Both branches are pinned by tests that capture the outbound header set |
| Exactly ONE component runs Flyway per environment — batch-service — enforced by `SPRING_FLYWAY_ENABLED`, with the application default left at `true` | Let every service migrate and rely on Flyway's history-table lock; run migrations as a separate init job; disable Flyway everywhere and migrate by hand | The deployment topology ALREADY assumed a single owner: `docker-compose.yml` gates every other backend on batch-service reporting healthy, Kubernetes uses an await-schema init container, and batch-service's own configuration called itself "the single owner" — but all eight services shipped `flyway.enabled: true`, so the schema was in fact provisioned by whichever container won the race, and concurrent migrators contend on the shared history table and on cross-table foreign keys. Aligning the configuration with the documented topology was therefore the smaller change; the per-service comments, which had documented the OPPOSITE ("whichever service starts first provisions the schema") and still cited "V1..V4" when there are eight migrations, were corrected with it. The application default stays `true` so a standalone `mvn verify`, whose Testcontainers PostgreSQL is private to that module, still provisions its own schema | Every non-owner now genuinely cannot start before the schema exists, which is what makes the startup gate meaningful rather than decorative. Because the migration set is a SINGLE shared set shipped in `carddemo-common`, no service loses a migration by not running it. Verified on a cold start against an empty database: all 8 migrations applied once with `installed_by = carddemo_batch` on every row, and batch-service the only service with any Flyway log activity |
| The transaction-posting CronJob gains the same `await-schema` init container the online Deployments carry | Leave it ungated; let a failed schedule retry on the next tick; keep Flyway enabled for the CronJob alone | Disabling Flyway for every non-owner left this pod depending on a schema it could no longer create, with `ddl-auto=validate` — a failure mode introduced by the ownership change itself, so closing it belongs with that change. Keeping Flyway on for the CronJob alone would reintroduce a second migrator, and a batch pod is the last place that should own a schema | A schedule that fires while batch-service is unavailable now WAITS rather than failing the run, which is the behaviour the online pods already have |

### 49.7 Observability panels that could not show data

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| The batch chunk-write and item-read panels plot CUMULATIVE counters instead of `rate()` | Keep `rate()` and widen the window; use `increase()`; drop the panels | A one-shot batch job reaches its final counter value inside a single 15 s scrape, so the counter never increases ACROSS scrapes and `rate()` is identically zero — the panels rendered flat-zero series and the reject-writer step the title promised never appeared at all. This is a property of one-shot jobs, not a window-tuning problem, so no interval would have fixed it. Plotting the counters shows the work each step actually did, which is the question the panel exists to answer | The panels no longer show a rate, so the titles, units and axis labels were changed with them (`Batch Item Read / Process Rate` became `Batch Items Read / Processed`, unit `ops` became `short`) rather than leaving a title that promises something the panel does not plot.
