Verified live: 708 items read, 700 processed, and seven chunk-write series all carrying non-zero values |


## 50. QA-Remediation Deviations (runtime and non-functional checkpoint)

Decisions taken while resolving the runtime QA findings of this checkpoint. Every one
was measured before and after on a running nine-service stack; the figures quoted are
the measurements, not estimates, and where a figure comes from an EARLIER checkpoint's
published run rather than from this one it is named as such. Section 40.1 records the
ones the report named as missing; 40.2 to 40.9 record the deviations the remediation
itself introduced.

### 50.1 Batch commit granularity and the card-paging window

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| The transaction-posting step commits **one record per chunk** (`chunk(1)`), and this is a fidelity decision rather than a tuning oversight | Chunk 100-1000 records per commit; make the chunk size configurable | `CBTRN02C` processes the daily feed record by record: it reads one `DALYTRAN` record, validates it, posts it, updates the account, and only then advances. A larger chunk would batch several feed records into one database transaction, so a failure mid-chunk would roll back records the legacy program had already committed — the reject tally, the posted count and the reject file would all diverge from the COBOL. Per-record commit is what makes exactly-once posting observable at the record level, and it is what allows a restart to resume at the exact failed record | It is the largest single cost driver in the batch window: one commit fsync per feed record. Inside the window by two orders of magnitude even so — 558 records/second at the timed 50,000-record run published in README-target.md from an earlier checkpoint, so the 4-hour window admits ~8.0 million postings. If a future volume outgrows that, the chunk size is the first thing to revisit, and this entry is the reason it was not already raised |
| The card list is windowed **on the server** by an offset/limit `Pageable` (`ScreenWindow`) computed as offset `(page-1) x 7` with limit `7+1`, and a page whose first row lies beyond the addressable row space is treated as an EMPTY window rather than queried | Ask for `page-1` at size 7 and use Spring Data's own paging; fetch a larger set and slice; reject out-of-range pages with a new message | Spring Data's `PageRequest(page, size)` computes its own offset, and the delivered screen had used it in a way that advanced by 8 rows per screen while displaying 7 — one row in every eight was unreachable through the UI. The explicit window makes the two numbers independent: the screen steps by exactly `MAX_SCREEN_LINES` (7, from `COCRDLIC`) and the 8th row is the look-ahead that decides whether `PF8` is offered, which is precisely the legacy browse. Section 15 records the SPA half of this decision (the screen renders what the service answered); this row records the server half | An `int` offset overflows above `Integer.MAX_VALUE`, which Spring Data rejects and which surfaced as `500` for `page=2147483647`. The guard sends that request down the SAME path a beyond-last page already takes — zero rows and the existing frozen `NO MORE RECORDS TO SHOW` banner — so no new observable string was invented. Verified by walking 150 consecutive pages over 1,050 seeded cards: 1,050 rows, byte-identical to the database key order, zero skips and zero duplicates |

### 50.2 JVM heap and GC policy, and the memory footprint baseline

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| One identical `JAVA_OPTS` policy in all nine service images — `-XX:+UseContainerSupport -XX:InitialRAMPercentage=25.0 -XX:MaxRAMPercentage=60.0 -XX:+UseG1GC -XX:MaxGCPauseMillis=100` — paired with a declared memory limit in BOTH topologies | Set flags per service as before; set absolute `-Xmx` values; rely on Java's container awareness with no flags at all; use `JAVA_TOOL_OPTIONS` instead of an entrypoint variable | The images had drifted into five different states: four declared `JAVA_OPTS=""`, one declared 75% with container support, one hard-coded 75% into its entrypoint with no override hook, and two (`card-service`, `user-service`) plus `batch-service` had no hook at all. Measuring the SAME delivered image three ways showed this was not cosmetic: with no memory limit it sized a 2 GiB initial and 29.97 GiB maximum heap on G1 (the JVM caps its view of physical memory at `MaxRAM` = 128 GiB and takes 25% of it), while inside the 1 CPU / 1Gi limit its own ergonomics chose a 248 MiB heap on the single-threaded **Serial** collector. One artifact, two footprints, two collectors, decided by where it ran. Percentages need a declared limit to resolve against, so the limit was added in the same change: `deploy.resources` in compose, `resources.limits` already present in Kubernetes. Pinning the collector removes the Serial downgrade regardless of how many CPUs a runtime grants | 60% of 1 GiB is 616 MiB of heap against ~190 MiB of non-heap and ~100 MiB of stacks, direct buffers and GC structures — 88% of the limit in the worst case where G1 commits the whole ceiling, which the measured live set (60-188 MiB) makes unlikely. Verified in a real container: `heap_max` 616 MiB, G1 pools, longest pause 68 ms, 400 MiB resident (39% of the limit). PID 1 is still the JVM after the entrypoint change, and `docker stop -t 30` was confirmed to run a full graceful Spring Boot shutdown |
| Absolute per-service memory figures and the measurement method are PUBLISHED as the baseline, and the AAP's 10% delta is left explicitly unverified | Assert a percentage against an assumed mainframe figure; report nothing | No z/OS region is available, so a percentage would be fabricated. A baseline plus a method is the strongest honest substitute: it makes the 10% gate computable against this release by anyone who re-runs the method. Measured across the nine-service stack: 462-618 MiB resident per service after a 150-user run, 3,923 MiB idle and 4,706 MiB peak for the tier, against 5,427 MiB idle before the policy | The comparison is to THIS release, not to the mainframe, and the entry says so. Closing the AAP gate as stated still requires a measurement from the source platform |
| Every container in both topologies carries a memory limit, including the ones with no Kubernetes manifest (Prometheus, Grafana) | Limit only the JVMs; leave the local stack unbounded | A bound that the monitoring stack can breach is not a bound on the host: an unbounded TSDB competes for memory with the services it is measuring. The observability containers were given local-stack figures and the rest mirror their manifests exactly | The monitoring figures are local-stack sizing rather than production sizing, and are labelled as such in `docker-compose.yml` |
| Redis is bounded with `--maxmemory 192mb --maxmemory-policy volatile-ttl` inside its 256 MiB limit, in compose and in `k8s/deployment-redis.yaml` | Leave `maxmemory` at 0; use `allkeys-lru`; use `noeviction` | Unbounded, Redis grows until the container limit is reached and is then OOM-killed, discarding EVERY live session at once — and the session IS the externalized COMMAREA (AAP 0.6.3). `volatile-ttl` evicts only keys carrying a TTL, which is every key Spring Session writes, nearest-to-expire first, so a full store sheds the operators whose sessions were about to lapse anyway. `allkeys-lru` was rejected because it would silently evict a non-expiring key; under `volatile-ttl` such a write fails loudly instead. The budget sits below the container limit because the AOF buffer, client buffers and allocator fragmentation are not counted by `maxmemory` | A session evicted before its timeout returns the operator to the sign-on screen. Verified in a real container for both the compose shell form and the Kubernetes argv form: `maxmemory` 201326592, policy `volatile-ttl`, `PING` still answered — against `maxmemory 0` on the delivered configuration |

### 50.3 CPU budget, and what the p95 target costs

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| Every service's CPU budget is raised from 1 core to 2 (`requests` 250m to 500m), uniformly, in both topologies | Size each service individually from its own measured demand; leave the budgets and report the exceedance; add replicas instead | Sizing was derived rather than guessed: CPU per request was measured by differencing `process_cpu_time_ns_total` against the served request count over a 150-user run — 2.0 ms for the gateway (paid by EVERY request), 3.5 to 5.5 ms for the read services, 18.3 ms for user-service, 94.8 ms for a sign-on. Per client request the tier costs 6.3 ms, and `264.5 req/s x 6.3 ms = 1.67` cores predicted exactly the 1.67 cores the run consumed, so the model is validated against itself. At the 619 req/s a heavier probe drives, the tier needs 3.9 cores and the GATEWAY ALONE needs 1.24 — which a 1-core limit cannot serve at any utilisation. Two cores keeps every service at or below half utilisation at the measured rate, and it keeps the JVM's ergonomics multi-core in every runtime (at one available processor it had been selecting SerialGC, section 50.2) | A uniform figure over-provisions the two services whose measured demand is negligible (`billpay-service`, `reporting-service`). That was preferred to nine different numbers that would drift out of date independently; the sizing rule is published in README-target.md so any of them can be recomputed |
| Sign-on's cost is documented and PROVISIONED FOR rather than reduced, and the residual p95 exceedance on the three password-hashing endpoints is published | Lower the BCrypt work factor; cache verification results; exclude the endpoints from the target | Measured directly, a strength-10 verification costs **72.8 ms of CPU** (60 verifications, stable to ±0.4 ms), so one core sustains 13.7 verifications/second and ~10.5 sign-ons/second end to end. The work factor is the property that makes the stored credential resistant to offline attack and the whole reason `COSGN00C`'s plaintext comparison was replaced (AAP 0.6.7); trading it for a latency figure would undo that. Caching a verification result would keep a credential decision in memory and is worse. So the endpoint is provisioned for its ARRIVAL BURST instead: 150 operators signing on inside a 15-second window is 10/second, needing ~2 cores at half utilisation | At 1 core that burst runs at ~95% utilisation, and the measured server-side p95 was 715.8 ms against a service time of 81-106 ms in the same run. `POST /users` and `PUT /users/{id}` hash on the same path and are published with the same caveat. Every endpoint that does NOT hash a password measured inside the 200 ms budget in both cadences |
| Absolute latency figures are published together with the machine that produced them | Publish the numbers alone, as before | The same harness and the same configuration measured 72.94 ms overall client p95 on this host warm and 646 ms cold-ish on the same host minutes earlier, and 8-10x worse per request than a quiet host at trivial concurrency. A latency number without its host is not reproducible, and the calibration probe makes the difference quantifiable: BCrypt cost 72.8 ms of CPU but 177-234 ms of wall time here, a 2.4-3.2x CPU-steal factor | Readers must now check the host line before comparing two runs. The alternative was numbers that silently disagree |

### 50.4 Connection-pool exhaustion degrades instead of failing

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| A connection-acquisition timeout answers `503` with `Retry-After` instead of `500`, mapped by handling `CannotCreateTransactionException` and by probing the cause chain for `java.sql.SQLTransientConnectionException` | Handle `DataAccessException` alone; widen the generic `500` handler; raise the pool until the case disappears | The generic handler never saw these: HikariCP's timeout signal is a `SQLTransientConnectionException`, and inside a transaction Spring wraps it in `CannotCreateTransactionException`, which is a spring-tx `TransactionException` and NOT a `DataAccessException` — so it fell through to the unmapped `500`. A pool timeout is backpressure, not a server fault; `503` plus `Retry-After` is the honest answer and it is what lets a caller retry rather than treat the request as lost. The cause-chain probe is depth-bounded and covers the other wrapping (`DataAccessResourceFailureException`) that occurs outside a transaction | The advice lives in shared code that `api-gateway` also imports, which has no spring-orm on its classpath; both exception types ship in the same artifact, so it still compiles there. Measured with the reporting steps that produced the finding — the table locked and 20 concurrent readers — 16 x `200` and 4 x `503` carrying `Retry-After`, and **zero** `500`s |
| `maximum-pool-size` raised from 8 to 16 on the seven online services | Keep 8 and rely on the new `503`; raise `connection-timeout`; raise it further still | Graceful degradation is not a substitute for capacity. 16 keeps the deployment ceiling at 7x16+12 = 124 of `max_connections` 200, leaving room for replicas and operator sessions, and HikariCP's own guidance argues against large pools served by one node | Verified sufficient rather than assumed: **zero** acquisition timeouts across all nine services in two 150-user runs, at 54.1 and 264.5 requests/second, with PostgreSQL at 6.9% CPU |

### 50.5 Rate limiting sized for the mandated user population

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| The limiter counts a SIGNED-ON caller per session (600/minute) and an anonymous caller per source address (1200/minute), with `/auth/**` at 300/minute per address; the identities are exactly complementary and the session key is an opaque SHA-256 digest that is never logged | Keep one per-address budget; remove the limiter; key everything on session | One per-address budget at 600/minute is a single budget for every operator behind one NAT address, so the AAP's 150 concurrent users could not sign on: measured, only 60 of 150 succeeded and a 700-request burst produced 106 `429`s. Splitting the identity keeps the control where it is actually needed — per-address counting still throttles credential stuffing BEFORE a session exists, which is the attack the limiter exists for — while a signed-on operator is measured against their own budget. Hashing the session id means the counter key can never carry a live credential | The identity had to be included in `getAlreadyFilteredAttributeName()`; without it the two `/`-prefixed budgets collided on the same request. Measured on shipped configuration: 150 of 150 sign-ons from ONE source address, a 150-session gateway workload with **zero** `429`, and the abuse budget still tripping above its limit |

### 50.6 Report submission, statement isolation and batch re-drive

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| Statement runs are SERIALISED to a single worker with a 4-deep queue, and admission is decided by a `Semaphore` taken BEFORE the job instance is created | Derive a per-execution output file name; run several statement jobs concurrently against distinct files; rely on the executor's own `RejectedExecutionHandler` | Two concurrent statement runs shared one output path, so a failing run's cleanup listener deleted a concurrent success. The alternative — per-execution file names — would have broken a frozen contract: `CREASTMT.JCL` names `STMTFILE` and `HTMLFILE` as fixed DD names, and the reject/output shape is a downstream deliverable. Serialising the runs preserves the byte-faithful contract instead of renaming around it. Admission is decided before instance creation because `TaskExecutorJobLauncher` CATCHES `TaskRejectedException` and marks the execution `FAILED` without rethrowing, so relying on the pool's rejection would have recorded a phantom failed execution for a request that was never accepted | Statement generation no longer runs in parallel with itself, so a large statement batch queues behind the current one. Measured: 12 concurrent submissions produced 5 x `202` in 0.44 s and 7 x `400` in 0.38 s carrying the frozen `Unable to Write TDQ (JOBS)...`, all five accepted runs reached `COMPLETED`, no shared-file collision occurred, and the X(80) line contract held (statement text exactly 80 characters per line) |
| The posting writer uses `saveAndFlush` so a duplicate-key violation surfaces INSIDE the chunk, making a failed posting date restartable | Make the posting date non-identifying so each launch is a new instance; add a run key; catch and re-write the status in a listener | A failed date could not be re-driven at all: the violation was raised at COMMIT time, outside the chunk, so the step's own status write was rolled back with it and the execution was left `UNKNOWN` — a status Spring Batch will not restart. Flushing inside the chunk turns the same error into an ordinary rollback, so the execution ends `FAILED`, which IS restartable, and the SAME instance resumes at the failed record. Making the date non-identifying was rejected because the identifying date is what refuses a second run of a completed cycle, which is the double-posting guard | Abend-equivalence is preserved deliberately: an already-posted feed record still aborts the run rather than becoming a business reject, exactly as `CBTRN02C` abends. Verified end to end: attempt 1 `FAILED` with 5 of 9 posted, attempt 2 accepted and resumed at item 6 with no double posting, attempt 3 after correcting the feed `COMPLETED`; the 430-byte `DALYREJS` contract intact. Section 39.8 records the residual case — a genuinely unrollable interruption still reports `UNKNOWN` |
| The reject writer is restart-tolerant (`saveState(false)`, `shouldDeleteIfExists(true)`) | Keep the writer's saved state; delete the file in a listener | A restart re-opened a reject file whose saved offset no longer matched the ephemeral working directory, failing the step for a reason unrelated to the data. Not saving the state and truncating on open makes a restart deterministic: the reject file is rewritten from the records the restarted run rejects | The reject file of an abandoned attempt does not survive the restart. That is correct here — the file is a per-cycle deliverable, and a partial one must not be mistaken for a complete one |
| The redundant per-step `.listener(...)` registrations in `DataManagementJobConfig` were removed (six steps registered the same listener twice) | Leave them; make the listener idempotent | `FailedOutputCleanupListener` implements only `StepExecutionListener`, so `.listener((StepExecutionListener) x)` and `.listener(x)` bind the SAME overload and the listener joined the composite twice. The effect was benign because `Files.deleteIfExists` is idempotent, but section 10.7 declares listener ORDER significant, and a doubled registration makes that declaration unverifiable | None observable. Verified after the change: all nine data-management jobs reach a terminal state and the output of a failed step is deleted exactly once, and not at all on success |

### 50.7 Screen-input validation kept on the legacy message path

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| An over-long transaction-id filter is rejected by the service's own key test (`isTranIdKey`: numeric AND at most 16 characters), NOT by adding `@Valid` to the list `@ModelAttribute` | Annotate the DTO binding with `@Valid` and let Bean Validation answer; widen the parse to `BigInteger` | The DTO already carried `@Size(max=16)`, but the controller binds without `@Valid`, so the constraint was dead and a 21-digit filter reached `Long.valueOf` and threw `NumberFormatException` — a `500`. Adding `@Valid` would have answered with a Bean Validation field error instead of the screen's own frozen message, changing what the operator sees. Testing the key where the key is used keeps `Tran ID must be Numeric ...` as the response, which is what `COTRN00C` says | The DTO constraint remains decorative on this path; it is exercised where the DTO is validated elsewhere. The same root cause was fixed on the VIEW path in the same change, where a 21-digit id now answers `404` (`Transaction ID NOT found...`) rather than `500`. Verified: 21-digit and 1000-digit filters both `400` with the frozen message, and a valid 16-digit filter still pages |

### 50.8 Trace attributes are redacted like every other channel

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| A shared `ObservationFilter` masks card numbers in every HIGH-cardinality observation value; LOW-cardinality values are deliberately left untouched | Override the server request convention per service; filter at the span exporter; record only the templated route | The trace pipeline was the one observability channel storing a PAN: the servlet convention's single high-cardinality key, `http.url`, is the raw request URI, and three spans carried it — the service's server span, the gateway's server span and the gateway's CLIENT span to the upstream. High-cardinality values are span-only, which is why the metric label set was already clean. A filter runs after the convention contributes its values and before any handler records the span, so one bean in the shared library covers all nine processes and both span kinds. Low-cardinality values are the Prometheus label set and the request timer's `uri` is already the templated route, so masking them could only change metric labels and break dashboards | Masking is by VALUE SHAPE (a 12-19 digit run reduced to its last four) rather than by key name, so a 16-digit transaction id in a path is redacted in spans too. That is consistent, not incidental: the log stream, the audit log and the error envelope already redact it identically through the same helper. Verified: zero raw-PAN occurrences in the traces of all nine services, `http.url` reading `/cards/************5740`, the templated `uri` tag and span operation names unchanged, and the business response still carrying the full card number |

### 50.9 What this checkpoint measured rather than assumed

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| Every claim in this section is backed by a before-and-after measurement on a running stack, and the repository's OWN committed harness (`perf/carddemo-online-load.js`) was used for the load figures | Re-verify by unit test alone; write a bespoke load script for this checkpoint | Three of the findings this checkpoint resolved were defects that a passing test suite had not caught, and two of the claims it corrected (sections 9.2 and 39.8's predecessors) were rationales asserted without measurement that measurement then refuted. Using the committed harness means the figures are reproducible by anyone with the repository, and it exercises the deployed path — gateway route, Redis session, downstream service, PostgreSQL — rather than a service in isolation | The harness needs a running stack, so nothing runs it automatically; that limitation is already recorded in section 39.8 |

## 51. QA-Remediation Deviations — FINAL Frontend Performance Checkpoint (production Vite/nginx SPA)

Non-literal choices made while resolving the FINAL frontend-performance QA report against the
production bundle served by nginx. Recorded here per the Explainability rule, including the two
tradeoffs that report found unlogged, the places a fix departs from the report's own suggestion,
and the findings deliberately accepted rather than changed.

### 51.1 Data-fetch orchestration — real cancellation and the 3270 keyboard lock

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| The caller's `AbortSignal` reaches axios through an ambient request scope (`runWithRequestSignal` in `api/client.ts`) read by the request interceptor, which is registered `{ synchronous: true }` — NOT by adding an Axios-config parameter to each of the thirty domain api functions, as the report suggested | (a) Add a trailing `options?: AxiosRequestConfig` to every api function and have `useApi` append it — the report's suggestion; (b) type the api contract as `(...args: [...A, RequestOptions])` so the extra argument is checked; (c) monkey-patch `apiClient.get`/`.post`/…; (d) leave cancellation unimplemented and log the deviation | `useApi.run` receives an opaque `(...args: A)` tuple, so it cannot append an argument SAFELY: several api functions carry optional middle parameters (`getCard(cardNumber, accountId?)`, `updateCard(cardNumber, request, accountId?)`), and an appended options object silently lands in the omitted position. Option (b) is not expressible — TypeScript forbids an optional element after a rest element — and would force every direct caller and test double to pass the parameter. The ambient scope is exact rather than best-effort BECAUSE of `synchronous: true`: axios then runs the whole request-interceptor chain INSIDE the `apiClient.get(...)` call rather than in a later microtask, so six calls started in one tick each stamp their own signal instead of racing for the last one written. An explicitly supplied `config.signal` always wins, so a caller that threads its own signal is untouched | The binding covers the request a call issues SYNCHRONOUSLY — every api function issues exactly one request before it awaits — so a second request issued after an `await` inside an api function would carry no signal. Adding a second, asynchronous request interceptor would silently return the chain to the microtask path and break the exactness; `useApi.test.ts` pins the contract by asserting through the real interceptor that the adapter receives the call's signal, that a superseded request's signal is `aborted`, and that a request issued outside a call inherits nothing |
| A rapid attention identifier (ENTER / PF7 / PF8) arriving while a browse is outstanding is INHIBITED, through the synchronous `isInFlight` lock the three list screens consult; the report's alternative of deriving the paging target from a pending-intent ref was NOT taken | (a) Derive PF7/PF8 targets from a pending-intent ref and issue every press — the report's suggestion; (b) queue the presses and replay them; (c) disable the PF-key buttons while a request is in flight; (d) leave every press to issue its own request and rely on the generation counter | The 3270 keyboard is LOCKED from the moment an AID is transmitted until the next map arrives (`CTRL=(FREEKB)` frees it on the SEND, not before), so keystrokes in that window are physically discarded by the terminal — inhibiting them is the faithful behaviour, not a shortcut. It is also the only correct option for `COTRN00C`: that browse is keyed by the boundary transaction ids of the page CURRENTLY displayed (`tranIdFirst`/`tranIdLast`), which are unknowable until the response lands, so a pending intent cannot be formed at all. With the lock in place the target derived from the last response is exact again, which is why `CardListPage` keeps deriving it that way. Option (c) was rejected because the mapset's row-24 legend is static text that a program never withdraws, and because a disabled key cannot report the boundary message the service answers with | An operator holding PF8 down advances one page per completed round trip instead of appearing to queue six. That IS the legacy behaviour, and it replaces the measured alternative of six concurrent identical requests, five discarded actions and a 12.7 s wait for one page advance. The lock releases on success, on failure and on `reset`, and a superseded call settling does NOT release it while its successor is still outstanding (asserted in `useApi.test.ts`) |
| A FAILED call keeps the payload already on screen; only a target change or an identity change clears it | Keep `setData(null)` on failure (the shipped behaviour); clear the payload but preserve the page cursor separately in each list screen; retry automatically | A CICS program that failed a read re-sent the map it had already built with its text moved into `ERRMSG` — the operator's screen did not go blank and the browse position did not move. The shipped behaviour discarded the rows AND regressed the page indicator from "Page 2" to "Page 1", so a transient failure silently moved the operator's place in the file; retrying then resumed at the wrong page. Automatic retry was rejected outright because it would mask the 409 optimistic-lock conflict (AAP 0.6.2) | Stale rows remain visible beside the error message until the next successful turn — which is exactly what the legacy screen showed. `useApi.test.ts`'s 409 case was realigned from asserting `data === null` to asserting the payload is kept: the assertion had encoded the defect |
| A cancelled request is reported as NOTHING — no error, no message line — and is distinguished from a failure by `ApiError.cancelled` rather than by inspecting axios internals at each call site | Let the cancellation surface as a `status` `0` `ApiError` like any transport failure; re-throw axios's `CanceledError` unnormalized; suppress it only inside `useApi` | A cancellation is the caller having moved on, not an outcome the operator caused, so it must never reach a screen's row-23 region — and it must not clear the session either, which a `status` `0` failure path would eventually be tempted to do. Normalising it to an `ApiError` (rather than re-throwing `CanceledError`) preserves the client's documented contract that EVERY rejection from the shared instance is an `ApiError`, so no caller has to learn a second failure shape | `ApiError` gains a sixth, defaulted constructor parameter; existing five-argument construction is unaffected. When a cancellation belongs to the current generation (an abort through the published `signal` with no successor) only the wait is cleared, so a screen cannot be left permanently busy |
| The empty-row copy is REMOVED from all three browse screens — card, transaction and user — so the row region carries no invented literal in any state | Withhold the copy only while a browse is outstanding and keep it for an answered empty result; keep asserting it from the first frame; replace it with a spinner or skeleton copy | A screen must not state a result it does not have: the copy was displayed for ~2 s on Slow 3G BEFORE the seven rows arrived, a false statement of the browse outcome. Withholding it while the browse is outstanding removes the false claim but leaves the invented literal, which sections 15, 16 and 40.3 of this log reject outright: `COCRDLIC` paints seven blank row slots and reports `NO RECORDS FOUND FOR THIS SEARCH CONDITION.` on line 23, `COTRN00C` leaves its ten row fields at LOW-VALUES, and `COUSR00C` answers a browse that finds nothing with `'You are at the top of the page...'` — each on line 23, which the services already return. Removing the row therefore closes the false claim in EVERY state and closes the inconsistency with it. Section 40.3 had already removed it from the card browse; the user browse, where it had been added for symmetry, is aligned here | An empty `<tbody>` relies on the line-23 message to tell the operator, exactly as the 3270 did. The tables carry an accessible name through `aria-labelledby`/`aria-label`, so a zero-row table is still announced as a table; the affected assertions on all three screens were realigned to require the ABSENCE of any placeholder row |

### 51.2 Session lifecycle — a visible expiry message and a client-side return to sign-on

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| An EXPIRED session publishes `SESSION_ENDED_MESSAGE` (`'Your session has ended. Please sign on again.'`) on the sign-on screen's row-23 region — a literal that exists in NO legacy source, declared ONCE in `api/messages.ts` and read by every producer of the condition (the response interceptor for a bodyless `401`, the route guards, the self-revocation exit and the session store's notice) | (a) Keep the silent redirect (the shipped behaviour); (b) reuse an existing legacy literal, for example `CCDA-MSG-THANK-YOU` or `Unable to verify the User ...`; (c) render the notice as a modal or toast; (d) have the gateway supply the text in the `401` body | The condition itself has no legacy analogue: a 3270 operator reached the sign-on map only by pressing PF3, so `RETURN-TO-SIGNON-SCREEN` had nothing to explain `[app/cbl/COMEN01C.cbl:L170-177]`. A cookie-backed session expiring underneath a rendered screen is a target-architecture failure mode, and the shipped behaviour answered it by presenting a blank sign-on screen — measured across 2,814 DOM samples with ZERO `role="alert"` regions — which is indistinguishable from the operator never having signed on and silently discards the task they were mid-way through. Reusing a legacy literal was rejected as worse than a new one: `Unable to verify the User ...` is `COSGN00C`'s answer to a failed credential read and would misreport the cause. Row 23 is where every other screen-level condition is reported, and the precedent for target-only text there already exists — axios's `Network Error` reaches the same region | A literal with no legacy origin now appears on a screen, which AAP 0.7.5 (spec-literal fidelity) constrains. It is confined to the one condition that has no legacy counterpart, is never shown for any condition a COBOL program answers, and is exported as a single module constant that `useSession.test.ts`, `SignonPage.test.tsx`, `AppComposition.test.tsx`, `App.test.tsx` and `client.test.ts` all assert against, so it cannot drift into a second wording — which it briefly had, four producers of one condition having each declared their own copy |
| The api client performs NO navigation of its own; `window.location.assign('/signon')` is removed and the hop is left to `App`'s existing `RequireAuth` / `RequireAdmin` guards | (a) Keep `window.location.assign('/signon')`; (b) call `history.pushState` from the client; (c) inject a `navigate` function into the client at bootstrap; (d) hold a router singleton the client can reach | `location.assign` re-downloads and re-parses the whole bundle to report an expiry — 116,958 B and a measured 4,433 ms transfer on Slow 3G — destroys anything the operator had typed, and, decisively, destroys the notice the store had just published, because the store is module state a document navigation discards. So a message and a full reload are mutually exclusive; the reload had to go. No new mechanism was needed: the guards already wait for `isSessionResolved` and already render `<Navigate to="/signon" replace/>` for a resolved signed-out session, so clearing the authority IS the navigation trigger. Options (c) and (d) were rejected for coupling the transport layer to the router for a transition the router already owns | The hop now depends on the rejected request having come from a route inside a guard. Every non-public route in `App.tsx` is, and `AppComposition.test.tsx` pins the whole chain end-to-end with nothing under `src` mocked — a `401` from a business read reaches the sign-on screen, shows the notice, and leaves `window.location.href` untouched, which is the assertion that the document did not navigate |
| A `403` REFUSAL publishes no client message AND does not drop the local authority: the session-expiry notification is raised for `401` alone, and the `403` path keeps the screen, re-primes the double-submit token and lets the operator retry in place (section 47.2) | Publish `SESSION_ENDED_MESSAGE` for `403` as well; invent a distinct client-side "not authorized" text; treat `403` as a session end and clear the authority, as it was before section 47.2 | `COMEN01C` already owns the refusal text — `'No access - Admin Only option... '` `[app/cbl/COMEN01C.cbl:L139]` — and `MenuController` sends it verbatim, so a second client-invented refusal message would put two different texts on one condition and break spec-literal fidelity. Clearing the authority as well was rejected because the two statuses do not mean the same thing: a `403` answers a live session — a missing or stale double-submit token, or a call the principal has no authority for — and both of those are recoverable on the screen the operator is already on, while a `401` means the session itself is gone. Returning a signed-on operator to sign-on for a recoverable refusal discards their typed entry for nothing | The reason handed to the session store therefore only ever arrives as `'expired'` from this client; `'refused'` remains part of the published handler contract, and the store's own behaviour for it (drop the authority, publish nothing) is retained and covered by `useSession.test.ts` for a caller that must report a refusal that way |
| The notice is withdrawn on submit, on a completed sign-on, and by an explicit `clearSessionNotice()`; the identity probe that FOLLOWS an expiry does not erase it | Clear it on any signed-out republish; clear it on a timer; leave it standing until the next expiry | The expiry itself triggers a fresh `GET /session`, which answers "no session" — the naive republish would therefore erase the notice within milliseconds of raising it, reproducing the silent screen the fix exists to remove. `signedOutState()` carries the notice across that confirmation and only an identity (a real sign-on) clears it, so the message lives exactly as long as the condition it reports | The shared module store outlives an individual test, so a suite that raises an expiry must withdraw it; `testing/sessionHarness.ts` exposes `withdrawSessionNotice()` for that, driven through the same production `clearSessionNotice` call the screen makes, and both affected suites call it in their per-test setup or teardown |
| The anonymous `GET /api/session` `401` on the public sign-on route is ACCEPTED as-is; the browser console entry it produces is not suppressed (report Info‑3) | (a) Answer `200` with a null identity instead of `401`; (b) skip the probe while on `/signon`; (c) intercept the request in a service worker; (d) issue the probe with `fetch` and swallow the rejection | The console entry is written by Chrome's own network stack for ANY `4xx` XHR — `Failed to load resource: the server responded with a status of 401` — before a line of application code runs, so no client-side change can remove it, which rules out (c) and (d). (a) would change the `GET /session` wire contract that sibling checkpoints have already verified, and would make "no session" indistinguishable from a successful read at the HTTP layer. (b) breaks `signIn`, which asks the server whether a session is already live and revokes it before sending credentials, so the sign-on screen genuinely needs the probe's answer | One browser-generated console entry on the first anonymous load of the public sign-on screen. It is the ONLY console entry the application produces on any of the 17 routes, it appears only for an anonymous caller, and it reports the correct HTTP outcome rather than a defect |

### 51.3 Account-view layout stability and the bounded 24-row frame

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| An unpopulated read-only row is held at one character row by the 80-column character grid the screen is laid out on (`.accountView__details` places every field on its declared `POS=(row,col)` with `grid-auto-rows: var(--term-line)`, and `.accountView__details .field { min-height: 0 }` keeps a value box from raising its row), NOT by fixing a `height` / `min-height` of 24px on the row as the report suggested, and not by centering a flex row | (a) `height: 24px` or `min-height: 24px` on the row — the report's suggestion; (b) `align-items: center` on the flex row, which answers the alignment but not the two declared columns; (c) render skeleton rows sized to the populated height; (d) give the empty value box a zero-width text strut through `::before` generated content; (e) drop the border from an empty value box | The row was never the wrong SIZE — it was the wrong ALIGNMENT. `align-items: baseline` asks each item for a baseline, and an EMPTY bordered box has no text to derive one from, so the flex algorithm synthesises it at the box's bottom border edge — 6.8px lower than a text baseline — and the row grew to 30.8px unpopulated while measuring 24px populated. A fixed `height` would have papered over that by clipping, and would then fight the one row whose populated value legitimately wraps at 375px (`Government Issued Id Ref`, measured 46.78px there). Centering removes the dependency on where a baseline falls and reproduces the populated geometry exactly: the 24px value box sets the row and the 20.8px caption sits 1.6px inside it, which is what was measured before the change. Skeleton rows were rejected as invented output (AAP 0.7.6), and generated content was rejected because Chrome folds `::before` text into the accessible name of the field it would sit in | The rule is scoped to the one row class that renders protected values as bordered boxes — `COACTVW` is the only mapset that does — so no other screen's vertical alignment moves. Verified after the change: all 28 rows measure 24px on both the empty and the populated screen, and the entire geometry payload (row count, both `dl` rects, header, body) is byte-identical between the two states |
| Every one of the seventeen screens publishes its chrome in a `useLayoutEffect`, so the header, the line-23 message region and the line-24 key legend are final at the FIRST paint; the header's height is NOT separately reserved as the report suggested | (a) Reserve the header's two-row height with a `min-height` — the report's suggestion; (b) fix each header row's height and clip a wrapped cell; (c) give the centre title column a fixed width so the arrival of the titles cannot re-flow the right-hand cell; (d) stop the frame rendering its body until the chrome is published | A CICS program moved every field into the symbolic map and then issued ONE `SEND`; nothing half-built ever reached the terminal. A passive effect is the opposite: the frame paints once with an empty header, message region and key legend, and then moves. So the honest fix is to stop painting the intermediate frame rather than to pad around it. (a) cannot work at narrow widths, where the populated header is legitimately THREE rows because the 80-column row wraps — a two-row reservation would still grow. (b) clips `Date : 08/08/26` at 375px, losing a field the mapset paints. (c) sizes the centre column to the 40-column `TITLE01` field and leaves the right-hand cell 28px at 375px, wrapping it far worse than today. (d) delays first paint behind the session probe, trading a shift for a blank document | Seventeen files change one hook each, and a layout effect runs synchronously in the commit, so a slow effect body would delay paint — these bodies only assemble a small object. Measured after the change: `header.appHeader` is a constant 41.59375px across all fourteen swept routes, desktop CLS on the account view fell from 0.096 to 0.000156 (Lighthouse 0.000119, score 1), and the two act() warnings the suites used to emit are gone because the publication is no longer an unwrapped post-commit update |
| `div.screen` is bounded by the display (`max-height: 100vh`, then `100dvh`), so a screen taller than its region scrolls INSIDE the body region — the report's own first suggestion — rather than compressing the account layout into two columns | (a) Compress `COACTVW` to the two-column arrangement `COACTUP` uses — the report's second suggestion; (b) shrink the type or the row pitch on this screen; (c) paginate the 28 protected fields; (d) pin the message region and key legend with `position: sticky` | A 3270 was bounded by its glass: it had 24 rows, and rows 23 and 24 were always on them. The frame declared `min-height: 24 rows` and the body already declared `overflow: auto` for exactly this case — the bound was simply missing, so the frame grew and pushed the message region and the key legend below the fold, measured 216px below on the empty search screen with its only key at y 991.50 where a click hit nothing. The bound also fixes this for EVERY screen rather than for one, which a per-screen re-layout would not. Option (a) was considered on its merits and declined for scope, not for fidelity: `COACTVW.bms` does place two or three caption/value pairs on each of its rows 5-20, whereas `AccountViewPage` renders its twenty-eight protected fields one per row — that arrangement predates this checkpoint, the page is treated as frozen by §25 of this log (its rendered captions were asserted rather than edited), its suite pins the rendered output, and the finding's stated acceptance criterion ("the 24-row grid fits the viewport with the message row and function-key bar visible") is met without touching it. `sticky` was rejected because it overlays the body's last rows instead of giving the body its own region | The bound applies to every screen, so any screen whose content exceeds the viewport now scrolls in-frame instead of extending the document. Verified across all fourteen routes plus both account screens at 1280x800 and 375x667: `document.scrollHeight === clientHeight` everywhere (the empty search screen went from 1016 vs 800 to 800 vs 800, mobile from 866 vs 667 to 667 vs 667), nothing outside the body region is below the fold, `elementFromPoint` at the centre of `F3=Exit` returns that button at both viewports, and clicking it navigates. RESIDUAL, recorded rather than glossed: because the account view stacks one field per row where the mapset pairs them, its body needs 738px against the 706px the bounded frame gives it at 1280x800, so its last row (`Primary Card Holder Y/N`) is reached by scrolling the body region — 32px, one row. Every other screen fits its region with room to spare. Closing that gap means adopting the mapset's row pairing on this screen, which is a screen-layout change outside this checkpoint's findings and is noted here so it is not mistaken for fidelity already delivered |
| The residual 0.049 layout shift measured on the account view at 375x667 is ACCEPTED rather than removed | (a) Seed the two shared `COTTL01Y` title literals into the frame's published-nothing state so the header cannot re-flow when a screen mounts; (b) reserve a third header row through a narrow media query; (c) make the header row scroll horizontally instead of wrapping, as the body already does below 48rem; (d) hold the first paint until the session probe answers | Below roughly 410px an 80-column header genuinely needs three rows, and the frame that precedes any screen — the one the session probe is still resolving behind — has no transaction id, program name or titles to render, so it is two. The shift is the difference between those two frames, and it is 0.0494 by PerformanceObserver and 0.0414 by Lighthouse (score 0.99) against the 0.1 "good" budget the finding names, down from 0.1921. (a) would paint `AWS Mainframe Modernization` on a frame where no map has been sent, which is observable output with no legacy counterpart (AAP 0.7.6) and would also contradict the frame's published-nothing contract. (b) needs a breakpoint derived from the rendered advance width of a font, so it would leave a band of widths where a blank header row appears at rest. (c) turns the header into a scroll container whose scrollbar can itself change the header's height, trading a measured shift for an unmeasured one. (d) replaces the shift with a blank document for the duration of the probe | The account view remains the app's largest CLS contributor at mobile widths, inside budget rather than at zero. The two mechanisms the finding named are both gone — the row collapse and the chrome-arrival header growth — and what remains is the pre-authentication placeholder frame, which is a property of the externalized session (AAP 0.6.3) rather than of this screen |
| `MainMenuPage.test.tsx`'s F3-legend case now awaits the exit transition instead of asserting it synchronously | Leave the synchronous assertion; make `handleExit` navigate before revoking the session | The exit revokes the SERVER session before it navigates, so the transition spans at least one round trip and a synchronous assertion was racing it — it failed once under a loaded host while passing in isolation. Its sibling case, the physical-`F3` one, already awaited the same transition; the two now agree. Navigating first was rejected because it would present a signed-out screen over a session cookie that is still valid | None to behaviour: the assertion is unchanged, only awaited. Ordering the revocation before the navigation is the contract `useSession.signOut` documents and `useSession.test.ts` pins |

### 51.4 Account-update marker geometry, narrow-width folding, and uniform field-level fault marking

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| The re-entry `*` marker occupies a RESERVED one-character column that is always rendered (`.accountUpdate__marker`, `flex: 0 0 1ch` with `margin-inline-end: -1ch` and `min-width: 0`), rather than the report's `visibility: hidden` or absolute positioning | (a) Always render the span and hide it with `visibility: hidden` — the report's first suggestion; (b) take it out of the flex flow with `position: absolute` — the report's second; (c) keep the conditional insert and accept the shift; (d) put the `*` into the entry field's own value, as the copybook literally does | The condition is the marker APPEARING, so the fix is to stop its appearance from being a layout event — which a reserved column does directly. `visibility: hidden` reserves the same box, so it is the same fix expressed as two states instead of one; the reserved column needs no second state and matches `.cardUpdate__marker`, the pattern the card-update screen already ships. Absolute positioning paints the glyph outside its group's box, which for the second group on a row overlaps the preceding entry field. Option (d) is what `CSSETATY` does (`MOVE '*' TO (SCRNVAR2)O`) but it would overwrite the operator's own entry, which a browser input cannot do without destroying data. The `-1ch` end margin absorbs the row's one-column gap so the caption sits exactly one cell right of an unmarked row rather than two | `min-width: 0` is load-bearing and was found by measurement, not by inspection: `1ch` is used at 9.59375px while the `*` glyph's min-content advance is 9.609375px, and a flex item's AUTOMATIC minimum size raised the used width to the larger of the two while the negative margin still cancelled only the smaller — moving the caption, the entry field and the neighbouring group by 1/64px. With the floor released the marked and unmarked states are byte-identical: raw `left` and `width` equal to the last bit on all seven measured targets, and a pixel diff of the two states shows 0 differing pixels in the captions, the neighbouring group and the whole rest of the form, against 54 and 119 disturbed pixels before |
| The account-update field group FOLDS at narrow widths (`flex-wrap: wrap`, plus `min-width: 0` on the group and its controls) rather than the form being shortened below 32rem | (a) Reduce the populated form height at `≤32rem` — the report's suggestion; (b) shrink the type or the row pitch on this screen; (c) leave the body region's horizontal scroller to carry the overflow; (d) drop the `size` attribute so the controls have no intrinsic width | The vertical half of this finding was already answered by bounding the frame (§51.3), so what remained was purely horizontal: the three `PIC X(50)` address controls are 373.6px of intrinsic width in a 361px row. Folding is the treatment the row above them already declared and the one the report screen already uses, so it needs no new vocabulary. Option (c) is the shipped behaviour and is what removed the right border from all three controls — a scroller the operator has no reason to expect. Option (d) would lose the mapset's declared field width, which is what sizes the control | Again `min-width: 0` is what makes it work: the controls' automatic minimum size is their `size="50"` intrinsic width, so without releasing it they still overhung the row by 13px and lost their right border. `maxLength` is untouched, so the field still holds its declared 50 characters. Verified: `main.screen__body` horizontal overflow 13px → 0, each control flush with the frame edge (`pxPastBodyRight` 0), the right border drawn as a solid full-height `rgb(31,156,64)` run, and at 1280px the same controls still take their full declared 516.78px — the release is a no-op where there is room, confirmed against an identically-styled control probe and against the untouched `size="50"` control on the card-update screen |
| The faulted control is derived from the CURSOR TARGET (`MOVE -1 TO <field>L`) rather than by matching the row-23 message text, and it is marked by the accessible flag and the cursor ALONE — not by colour | (a) Extend each screen's message→field table with the missing entries; (b) have the server name the faulted field in its error body; (c) mark nothing and rely on the message text; (d) mark the whole form; (e) also render the faulted control RED through a shared `faultedFieldClass` helper, as this checkpoint first did | A table of message text can only enumerate the messages the CLIENT produces. Two of the three gaps the report found were exactly the cases it cannot reach: the bill-pay account-not-found message comes from the SERVER, and the report screen's confirmation prompt is a screen state rather than a rejected value. The cursor target has neither limitation — every legacy path ends by naming the field the next send places the cursor on, so it IS the field the message is about, and it is already recorded because the screens use it to place focus. Option (b) would change a verified error envelope for a presentation concern. Option (e) was NOT taken, and the shared class helper was withdrawn with it: `COSGN00C`, `COTRN02C`, `COBIL00C` and `CORPT00C` contain no `MOVE DFHRED` anywhere, so a red frame on one of their entry fields is output the program never produces — the spec-literal rule forbids it, and section 44's per-screen entries had already recorded 'by cursor and flag alone, never by colour' for exactly these four screens. The three screens whose programs DO move `DFHRED` (`COACTUPC` and `COCRDUPC` through `CSSETATY`, and the card-detail screen) keep the state-based `fieldErrorClass` they already used, so colour still appears exactly where the source paints it | `aria-invalid` now follows the cursor for every error-line message, including the report screen's confirmation prompt. That prompt is not a rejected value, but this screen publishes it on the error line in red, so marking the control it points at makes the programmatic state agree with what is on the glass instead of contradicting it; the alternative leaves a screen-reader operator with a red screen and no field. Verified across seven cases on four screens: the offending control carries `aria-invalid="true"` and the cursor, a non-offending control on the same screen carries neither, no `.fieldError` element exists anywhere on those screens, the mark MOVES rather than accumulating, and `aria-describedby` appends the message-region id to a pre-existing hint rather than replacing it |
| The whole row-24 legend renders inactive on `/transactions/add` and `/billpay` while a write is outstanding | Disable only the ENTER key; leave the legend active and rely on the synchronous ref latch; render a spinner | The 3270 keyboard was LOCKED from the moment an AID was transmitted until the next map arrived, so NO key was accepted in that window — not just the one that sent it. The ref latch already made a double submit produce a single write, but it did so invisibly: the report measured 0 of 1,112 and 0 of 692 frames with any control inactive, so the operator had no way to see the terminal was busy. The sign-on and report screens already publish `enabled: !submitting`, so this makes the four write screens agree | Two of the keys now refused are the screen's own exits (`F3`, `F4`), so an operator cannot leave while a write is outstanding — which is the legacy behaviour and is bounded by the request timeout. Verified under Slow 3G: all four keys inactive across 128 samples over 2,031ms on the add screen and all three across 126 samples over 2,000ms on the bill-pay screen, corroborated by the legend's rendered brightness falling to the dim tone (bright-pixel count 1,510 → 0 and 1,008 → 0) |

### 51.5 The line-8 rule as a mapset literal, and one dim treatment for every inactive control

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| The transaction-view line-8 rule is rendered as the mapset's own run of 70 dash characters through the existing shared separator rule, NOT as a styled `hr` | (a) Give `hr` a real CSS rule (`width: 100%; margin-inline: 0`) and keep the element — the report's suggestion; (b) `align-self: stretch` on the `hr`; (c) leave it and accept the dot | `COTRN01` line 8 is `INITIAL='------…'` with `LENGTH=70`, so the rule IS a literal the terminal painted as 70 characters — not a drawn line. Four screens already render exactly that construct exactly that way (`.billPay__separator`, `.tranAdd__rule`, `.userDelete__rule`, `.userUpdate__separator` share one rule); the transaction-view screen was the single outlier still using a bare `hr`, which is why it was the single screen that collapsed. Adding a CSS rule for `hr` would fix the symptom while leaving the same construct rendered two different ways on two sibling screens — a solid 1px line on one, a dash run on the other — and would keep a UA-default trap in the stylesheet for the next `hr`. Conforming the outlier to the established pattern closes the defect and the inconsistency together | The `hr` element is gone, so the document has one fewer native separator landmark; the replacement carries `aria-hidden="true"`, which is correct for a decorative rule and matches what the other four already do — the rule is not information, it is ink. Verified: `hr` count 0 app-wide, 70 dashes, and geometry byte-identical to both siblings and to the screen column (768.140625 x 20.796875 at left 255.921875, margins 0px, pairwise deltas 0.000000px) where it had been a 2x2px dot with 383.062px auto margins |
| Every inactive control carries the DIM tone through ONE global `input:disabled, select:disabled, textarea:disabled` rule, and the report screen's private copy of that rule was DELETED | (a) Add the treatment screen by screen; (b) keep the private rule and add a second one for the in-flight screens; (c) dim with `opacity`; (d) leave it and rely on the cursor | A 3270 field the program had not opened for entry was ASKIP or DRK, so it never looked like a field awaiting input. A control that keeps the GREEN entry tone while refusing input tells the operator the opposite of the truth, and green-while-refusing was the state of every screen except the report dates. The treatment did not need inventing: `button:disabled` already declares it for an inactive function key, in the same `--term-neutral-dim` token, and the private report rule was a copy of it whose own comment said "dimmed like the keys" — so this generalises an existing decision rather than making a new one. Deleting the private rule keeps one definition instead of two. `opacity` was rejected because it would fade the field background and border geometry too, and the border is what marks a 3270 field's extent | The dim pairing measures 2.80:1 against the field background, below the AA text threshold. WCAG 1.4.3 exempts inactive components, and this was confirmed empirically rather than assumed: the contrast audit passes with ZERO flagged items on both an authenticated and an anonymous page, both scoring 100. A control that is BOTH faulted and disabled now renders dim rather than red, because `input:disabled` (0,1,1) outranks `.fieldError` (0,1,0) — correct, since a locked keyboard renders the whole screen inert and the mark returns with the next send. Verified across 21,396 samples: one atomic value set while disabled on every screen, and all 105 enabled controls on 11 screens still exactly `rgb(51,255,102)` / `rgb(31,156,64)` |

### 51.6 Bundle shape, upstream header ownership, and start-up DNS resolution

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| The SPA continues to ship as ONE JavaScript chunk; route-level code splitting is deliberately declined and its cost recorded here instead | (a) `React.lazy` + `Suspense` per route; (b) `manualChunks` in the Vite build; (c) both | The measured cost is real and is stated in the Risk column rather than minimised. Against it: a `Suspense` fallback is unrequested observable output on a screen whose legacy analogue transferred by `XCTL` with NO intermediate state, which the spec-literal rule forbids; it reintroduces the layout shift just eliminated (CLS is now 0 on the sign-on screen, and TBT is 0); and it costs an extra round trip on the FIRST visit to each of 17 screens, which on a slow link is about 2 s of blank per screen against a 200 ms p95 online-response target — trading one cold start for seventeen warm ones. `manualChunks` alone changes nothing, because chunks reached through static imports all stay in the initial graph and are fetched on load regardless; it only adds request overhead. The measurements also show splitting is the ONLY lever left: the bundle is already fully minified, with no legacy transpilation and no duplicated modules (three separate audits each score 1 with zero items) | The cost, measured rather than estimated: 55.1989% of the bundle is downloaded, parsed and never executed on the sign-on screen — 64,531 of 116,907 compressed bytes, 212,943 of 385,774 source bytes. On a cold Slow-3G load the viewport carries no content for 6,508 ms, of which the single 117,162-byte chunk accounts for 4,431.6 ms of download; content then appears 43.6 ms after its last byte lands. Confirmed two independent ways agreeing to 1 ms (paint entries vs frame-by-frame analysis of 1,076 recorded frames). What this buys back if ever reversed is 450 ms of mobile and 40 ms of desktop paint time. The stated targets are nonetheless met today: performance 99 mobile / 100 desktop, CLS 0, TBT 0, total page weight 123,046 bytes over 5 requests. Revisit if a screen's dependencies grow the chunk materially |
| A response carries exactly ONE copy of each security header, and for everything under `/api/` the gateway owns ALL of them | (a) Strip the gateway's copies and let nginx own all of them; (b) declare three headers on the location and `proxy_hide_header` the gateway's copies of them, leaving CSP and `Permissions-Policy` to pass through; (c) leave both copies | Two copies of a header make the effective policy a question the client answers, and for `Permissions-Policy` the two copies did not even agree — the gateway denies 8 features, nginx's copy denied 4, so the weaker one was in play depending on how a client reconciled them. Option (b) would work, but it is unnecessary here and hides the ownership: `add_header` is inherited only when a level declares NONE of its own, and this file declares NO security header at the server level — every location that answers FROM THIS CONTAINER declares the full set itself (section 48 recorded that arrangement). `location /api/` therefore declares none and inherits none, so the gateway's copy of each of the five headers is the only one on the wire, and the stricter value survives for all five rather than for two. On `/assets/` the duplicate came from a different source — `expires 1y` and an `add_header Cache-Control` each emit a `Cache-Control` — so one combined `add_header Cache-Control "public, max-age=31536000, immutable"` replaced both; it is declared WITHOUT `always`, so a 404 for a missing asset is not cached as permanent content | nginx depends on the gateway to send all five headers on API responses; if the gateway ever stops, they disappear from `/api` rather than falling back. Both come from the gateway's own filter chain, which is covered by its tests. Verified after the change: every one of the six audited headers appears exactly once on the document, on the bundle and on an API response, read both from the browser and from raw un-collapsed wire capture, with the stricter value surviving in both cases; and 16 API calls across 7 screens returned no 5xx, so hiding upstream headers did not disturb proxying. `/assets/` intentionally carries no CSP — CSP governs a document's own fetching and has no effect on a script response |
| **SUPERSEDED by section 48, which is what ships.** The `/api` upstream is held in a VARIABLE and re-resolved per request, with the `resolver` line written at start-up by an entrypoint hook that prefers `NGINX_LOCAL_RESOLVERS` and otherwise parses `/etc/resolv.conf` — options (a) and (b) combined, with neither address hardcoded. The reasoning below is recorded as the position this checkpoint took before that fix was integrated | (a) `resolver 127.0.0.11` plus a variable `proxy_pass`; (b) template the resolver from `/etc/resolv.conf` in an entrypoint; (c) leave the upstream resolved once at start-up (this checkpoint's own position) | The failure mode is real but confined to local Compose. In the deployment target the gateway is reached through a Kubernetes Service (`k8s/service-api-gateway.yaml`) whose ClusterIP is stable for the Service's lifetime, so a start-up resolution stays correct there however often the pods behind it are replaced. Option (a) hardcodes Docker's embedded resolver, which does not exist in a cluster pod, so it would fix Compose by breaking production. Option (b) is portable but introduces a templated config plus an entrypoint — a new deployment mechanism, and a new failure mode, for a development-only symptom | None as shipped: because the host is re-resolved per request, a gateway recreated with a new container IP is followed without recreating the frontend container — the symptom this row had accepted. The entrypoint derives the resolver rather than hardcoding Docker's `127.0.0.11`, so the same image still starts in a cluster pod. The measurement recorded here — 16 API calls across 7 screens with no 5xx — was re-observed after integration |
## 52. QA Remediation — Documentation Truthfulness, Test-Phase Naming and Seeded-PII Ownership

Raised by the FINAL documentation-and-governance QA checkpoint, which tested
`README-target.md`, this log, `docs/traceability-matrix.md` and the root
`.gitignore` against the running stack and reported 35 findings. The rows below
record the choices that a competent engineer could reasonably have made
differently; the corrections that were purely factual (a wrong artifact
coordinate, a missing migration row, a stale line citation) needed no decision.

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| The reStructuredText field convention every source file already follows is DOCUMENTED — a "Code Documentation" section in `README-target.md` and this row — rather than left as an unstated house style | Leave it undocumented because the code is self-evidently consistent; adopt Javadoc `@param`/`@return` and rewrite 455 files; adopt the convention only for new files | The Docstring and Comments rule (AAP 0.7.2) mandates reStructuredText, and 455 sources implement it with `:purpose:` / `:param <name>:` / `:output:` / `:returns:` / `:raises:` / `:note:` — but the literal string `reStructuredText` appeared in NO file, so a maintainer had no documented basis for the style and no way to tell an intentional convention from an accident. Documenting costs one section; converting to Javadoc would churn every file and lose the `:purpose:` framing the rule asks for | The convention now has to be maintained deliberately in review; a file that drifts is a documented inconsistency rather than an invisible one |
| Code comments carry PURPOSE and BEHAVIOR plus a bare specification citation (`AAP 0.x`); the REASONING for a non-obvious choice lives only in this log. QA-finding provenance (`QA Issue 6`, `QA CR-15`) is not cited in source at all | Keep the QA ids in the comments and add matching rows here so the provenance resolves both ways; strip the AAP citations too and put every specification reference in this log as well | The Explainability rule puts rationale in the log, not in comments. QA ids in source were the worst of both worlds — they pointed at a review artifact that is not part of the delivered repository, so the trail dead-ended for any later reader; they are gone. A short `AAP 0.x` citation is a different thing: it names the requirement a line implements, which is exactly what a reader of that line needs and what the spec-literal fidelity rule asks to be traceable | A reader who wants to know WHY must open this log; the comments deliberately do not answer it |
| Test classes that start a Testcontainers container are RENAMED to `*IT` so Surefire never runs them, instead of documenting that `mvn test` also starts containers | Correct the README to say `mvn test` starts Testcontainers; move the classes into a profile; keep the names and exclude them from Surefire by configuration | The documented phase table already assigned `*Test` to Surefire as unit tests and `*IT` to Failsafe as integration tests, and these seven classes were integration tests wearing a unit-test name — `DocumentedCredentialsSmokeIT`, `SecurityUserRepositoryIT`, `UserCrudLifecycleIT`, `UserRepositoryIT`, `BillPaymentIntegrationIT`, `CardRepositoryIT`, `CardXrefRepositoryIT`. Renaming makes the promise "no Testcontainers integration tests" literally true (measured: 7 containers before, 0 after) and matches the sibling account-service suites, which were already named `*IT`. Documenting the leak instead would have written a convention violation into the contract | The seven classes now run only under `mvn verify` / `mvn install`, so a developer who runs `mvn test` alone gets no coverage from them — which is the documented meaning of the two phases |
| Flyway version `4` is FIXED so it performs the seeded-PII encryption it is credited with; the `SeededPiiEncryptionMigrator` context-initialization pass stays as an idempotent safety net and is documented as such | Re-document migration 4 as a no-op that merely records the version while the runtime bean does the work; delete the runtime bean and rely on the migration alone | The migration decided "already encrypted" by handing the value to `CryptoConverter.convertToEntityAttribute` and treating a non-throw as proof — but that method deliberately passes an unrecognised value straight through for pre-encryption compatibility, so every plaintext fixture value was classified as protected and the tally was always `rewritten=0`. Switching to the envelope-aware `isProtected` predicate makes the migration do the work inside the migration transaction, where a failure rolls back; keeping the runtime sweep still protects a database seeded before that migration existed or restored from an older dump. Deleting the sweep would leave those databases with plaintext at rest | Two components can now encrypt the same columns. Both are idempotent and the sweep no-ops after a correct migration (verified: `customers rewritten=50, cards rewritten=50` at migration time, then nothing left for the sweep) |
| A rejected account-number edit CLEARS the displayed account and customer block on the account-view screen | Leave the previous record on screen with the error banner, as the screen did before; clear only the fields that changed | `COACTVWC` reaches every send through `1000-SEND-MAP`, whose first step `1100-SCREEN-INIT` does `MOVE LOW-VALUES TO CACTVWAO`, and the `INPUT-ERROR` path performs that same `1000-SEND-MAP` — so the mainframe repaints an EMPTY block on a failed edit. Retaining the data also showed one account's balances and masked identifiers beside a different account number in the entry field, which a reader can easily misattribute `[app/cbl/COACTVWC.cbl:L363-L366,L432; frontend/src/pages/AccountViewPage.tsx]` | An operator who mistypes loses the record they were looking at and must re-enter the number — exactly as on the 3270 |
| The README documents the `.env` procedure as "replace EVERY value" and explains why Compose's `${VAR:?}` guard does NOT catch a placeholder | Name the handful of variables a local run strictly needs; make the placeholders empty so the guard fires; add a startup validator that rejects known placeholder strings | `.env.example` ships a readable placeholder for all 17 variables so a reader can see what each one is for, which means every `${VAR:?}` reference is satisfied and the fail-fast guard stays silent. The README previously named seven variables, leaving nine at published placeholder values — including `CARDDEMO_PII_KEY`, whose placeholder is not valid Base64, so PII-touching services crash-looped. Emptying the placeholders would trade a documented trap for an undocumented one (a reader would see `VAR=` and not know the format); the documentation states the trap and the exact failure message instead | A reader who skims still has one way to get this wrong; the failure is loud and its message is quoted in the README |
| A hop that only READ the shared session ACCEPTS that the entry has gone when it writes it back (`RemovedSessionTolerantSessionRepository`, installed over every `SessionRepository` bean by the `BeanPostProcessor` in `SessionRedisConfig`) | Hunt down and remove every component that reads the session on a sign-on request; catch the failure in a filter placed outside Spring Session's; stop rotating the session id | Spring Session writes a session back for EVERY hop that touched it — that is how `lastAccessedTime` is refreshed — and sign-on rotates the session id in `auth-service`, which DELETES the old entry. The gateway had read the caller's session while serving that same request, so its write-back of the vanished entry raised `IllegalStateException: Session was invalidated` while the proxied body was being written, outside any handler that could recover it: a sign-on that had already SUCCEEDED (new session issued, previous ids dead) reached the browser as HTTP 500 for every caller who signed on while holding a session — signing on twice, or switching user without signing out. Rotation cannot be given up, since it is the session-fixation protection. Removing readers is whack-a-mole: a probe found TWO on that route alone (`AnonymousAuthenticationFilter` building `WebAuthenticationDetails`, and `FrameworkServlet.publishRequestHandledEvent`), and a framework upgrade may add another. Accepting the deletion is also the semantically correct outcome, because re-writing the entry would RESURRECT a session that must no longer exist | Only `save` is tolerant, and only of the one `IllegalStateException` whose message is `Session was invalidated`; a differently-messaged `IllegalStateException` is RE-THROWN, `findById`, `createSession` and `deleteById` pass through, and store faults still surface as `DataAccessException`, so a deleted session still fails to authorize on its next use. Matching the message rather than the type keeps an unrelated illegal state from being swallowed; the trade is that a future Spring Session release which reworded it would resurface the 500, which is why `RemovedSessionTolerantSessionRepositoryTest` pins the literal and asserts both branches |
| `DispatcherServlet` is given a store-nothing `FlashMapManager` (`WebHardeningConfig`) | Keep the MVC default `SessionFlashMapManager`; disable it only in the gateway | The default consults the session on EVERY request to look for flash attributes, which made every hop OWN the shared session for the request and write it back at commit. Nothing in CardDemo produces flash attributes — there is no `RedirectAttributes`, `RedirectView` or `FlashMap` anywhere in the tree — so the read protects nothing, and removing it also drops one Redis round trip per request | A handler added later that returns a redirect with flash attributes would find them silently dropped; session idle timeout is unaffected, because every authenticated request still reads the session through `SessionContextAuthenticationFilter`, which is what refreshes `lastAccessedTime` |
| `SessionContextAuthenticationFilter` does not run at all for `/auth/**` | Let it run and read the session as before | The sign-on routes are anonymous (`permitAll`) and authenticate from the request body, so restoring a principal for them grants nothing, and reading the session there is reading the very session sign-on is about to replace. The skip is decided on the path within the application, so it holds behind a context path | A future `/auth/**` endpoint that needs the signed-on principal would have to resolve it itself or move out from under the prefix |
| The escaping-exception access-log record renders the request path ONCE, through the masking helper | Keep the second, unmasked `getRequestURI()` argument and add the missing placeholder | The record had six arguments for four placeholders, so the duration was printed in the exception slot, the exception class was dropped from the message (`POST /auth/signon failed after /auth/signonms: 140`), and the extra argument put an UNMASKED, possibly PAN-bearing path into the log — the very leak `loggedUri` exists to prevent (CWE-532). Rendering the path once fixes both, and matches the success and 5xx records | None; the exception class is still named in the message and its stack trace is still attached |
## 53. QA-Testing Checkpoint — Automated-Test-System Remediation

Decisions taken while closing the QA testing report on the FINAL automated test system (Maven
reactor plus the frontend Jest/`tsc`/build stack). The report was raised against an earlier
commit; the rows below cover only the choices that this pass actually had to make.

### 53.1 Reactor failure behaviour and coverage instrumentation

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| `--fail-at-end` is committed in `.mvn/maven.config`, so EVERY invocation of this repository's build carries it | Add a `ci` Maven profile; pass `-fae` from the pipeline definition only; leave the default halting behaviour | A profile cannot set reactor failure behaviour — it is a session option, not a plugin setting — and a pipeline flag protects only the pipeline: a developer running `mvn verify` locally still saw one red module halt the reactor and hide the integration tests of every module after it (six modules SKIPPED and 19 of 29 `*IT` classes unexecuted in the reported run). `.mvn/maven.config` is Maven's own mechanism for options that belong to the project rather than to the caller, so the guarantee travels with the checkout | The build still fails, but later: a failure now appears in the Reactor Summary at the end instead of stopping the run, so a caller watching only the tail of the log must read the summary. Modules that DEPEND on a failed module are still skipped, which is why the probe used to verify this behaviour was planted in a leaf module |
