| Coverage is measured per module by `jacoco-maven-plugin` 0.8.15 with SEPARATE unit and integration exec files and reports (`jacoco.exec` / `site/jacoco` from Surefire, `jacoco-it.exec` / `site/jacoco-it` from Failsafe) | One merged exec file for both tiers; a single `report` bound after `verify` | A merged file cannot answer the question the checkpoint asks — whether a branch is covered by a fast unit test or only by a Testcontainers integration test — and the answer changes what a reviewer does about it. Two exec files cost nothing because `prepare-agent` and `prepare-agent-integration` write to different destinations by default | Two report trees per module instead of one. `argLine` is contributed by the agent goals and no module overrides it, so the Surefire/Failsafe `systemPropertyVariables` configuration is untouched |
| No `report-aggregate` and no coverage-aggregation module were added; the reports stay per module | Add a `carddemo-coverage` module depending on all ten modules and bind `report-aggregate` there; declare every module as a dependency of the root aggregator | AAP §0.3.2 fixes the module list, and `report-aggregate` collects only from the module's own dependencies — so a repository-wide figure needs either a new module the AAP does not enumerate or a parent POM that depends on its own children. Per-module reports already make every untested branch quantitatively visible, which is what the finding asked for | A single repository-wide percentage is not produced by the build; a reviewer reads eleven module reports (or feeds the eleven `jacoco.xml` files to an external tool). Recorded here so the omission is not mistaken for an oversight |
| The frontend measures coverage with Jest's `v8` provider, not the default `babel` provider | Keep the default `babel` provider; add `babel-jest` and a Babel config alongside `ts-jest` | The suite runs as native ESM through `ts-jest/presets/default-esm`; the `babel` provider re-transforms the sources and breaks that contract outright — measured, not assumed: it failed 25 of 32 suites with `SyntaxError: await is only valid in async functions`. The `v8` provider instruments in the runtime and leaves the ESM transform alone (32/32 suites, 671 tests still pass) | `v8` counts statements and lines identically, so the two figures in the summary are the same number by construction rather than by coincidence |
| Frontend `coverageThreshold` gates are set BELOW the measured figures (statements/lines 95 vs 97.17 measured, branches 85 vs 89.37, functions 88 vs 91.48) | Set the gates at the measured values; set round aspirational numbers above them | A gate at the measured value fails the build on any honest refactor that moves a line, which trains people to raise the gate instead of the coverage. A gate above the measurement is red on the day it lands. The margin is deliberately small enough that deleting a page suite still breaks the build | The gates permit a few points of regression before failing. `npm test` stays fast and gate-free; `npm run test:coverage` is the gated entry point |

### 53.2 Determinism of the PII-key security tests

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| `CARDDEMO_PII_KEY` is BLANKED in every Surefire and Failsafe fork (`<environmentVariables><CARDDEMO_PII_KEY/></environmentVariables>` in the root POM), and the skip guards that used to tolerate an ambient key are DELETED | Keep the `assumeTrue` guards; reverse `PiiEncryptionKey`'s resolution order so the system property outranks the environment variable; pass the fixture key through the environment variable instead of the property | `PiiEncryptionKey.require()` resolves the environment variable before the `carddemo.pii.key` property, so any machine provisioned the way this project's own `docker-compose.yml` and `k8s/secret.yaml` require silently outranked the fixture — and the guards then SKIPPED the wrong-key, tampered-token, missing-key and short-key cases plus the entire seeded-migration suite while Maven still reported success (measured before the fix: 19 run / **13 skipped**, exit 0). Blanking the variable in the fork fixes it where the problem is — the test environment — with NO change to production resolution, and a blank value is already treated as absent by the existing code. Reversing the production precedence would have changed how a deployed service resolves its key, which is not something a test-integrity finding justifies | A run launched OUTSIDE Maven (an IDE) on a machine that exports a real key now FAILS those cases instead of skipping them. That is the intended trade: the finding's own bar is that the cases "run in every environment, or the build fails loudly if they cannot" |
| The crypto suites additionally call `PiiEncryptionKey.clear()` before and after each case | Rely on the system property alone; give the converter a test-only key setter | An installed process-wide key outranks every configuration source, and a Spring context booted earlier in the same fork installs one — so without the clear, the case that sets a WRONG key would have kept decrypting successfully and passed for the wrong reason. `clear()` already exists for exactly this purpose | The fork is left with no installed key, which is the state a fresh fork starts in; the next context installs its own during startup |
| The one remaining skip in `carddemo-common` (`BatchOutputPathResolverTest.nonWritableOutputRootAbortsConstruction`) is left environment-guarded | Force the case to run as root; delete it; run the module's tests as a non-root user | The case needs a directory whose write bit is actually enforced, and root defeats the bit — the assumption is on an OS capability, not on application configuration, so it can never hide an application defect. The containment behaviour the resolver relies on is asserted unconditionally by the sibling cases | One case does not execute when the build runs as root. It is the only skip in the entire backend suite, so a second one appearing is conspicuous |


### 53.3 Schema-contract fidelity, migration inventory and integration-test discovery

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| The four integration classes that overrode Hibernate's schema action now run at `validate` against a MIGRATED database — `TransactionServiceApplicationIT` and transaction `SecurityContractIT` drop their `ddl-auto=update`, `JobLaunchParameterIT` and `TransactionIdGenerationIT` keep their own throwaway container but let the context's own Flyway provision it | Leave the overrides and document them; give the two transaction-service classes their own containers as well; keep `create` and assert the schema separately | An override of `create`/`create-drop`/`update` makes Hibernate manufacture the very schema the class then "validates", so the entity-to-migration contract is asserted against the entities themselves — and `TransactionServiceApplicationIT`'s own docstring claimed `validate` while setting `update`. Two of the four also pointed at the SHARED `MigratedSchemaContainer`, where a schema action is not merely uninformative but destructive to sibling classes in the same fork. Measured after the change: all four classes pass at `validate`, which proves the overrides were pure masking rather than a necessity | The two classes with private containers now pay one Flyway run each (V1-V8 plus the Java migration) instead of a Hibernate `create`, and their fixtures must satisfy the real constraints — which is the point: `TransactionIdGenerationIT` previously inserted a card number no `cards` row existed for, something `fk_transactions_card` (V8) forbids |
| `TransactionIdGenerationIT` binds to the SEEDED card `0923877193247330` / account `2` and empties only the transaction master | Keep inventing customer/account/card rows; delete the seeded parents first | The seeded parents cannot be deleted (`fk_cards_account`, `fk_card_xref_*` reference them), and the previous hand-rolled customer insert supplied 5 of the 19 NOT NULL columns the real `customers` table declares, so it only ever worked because Hibernate had generated a laxer schema. Reusing the seed is both shorter and closer to what a deployment holds | The class now depends on that seeded pair existing, which is asserted explicitly by its own `seededFixtureIsPresent` case rather than assumed |
| `spring.batch.jdbc.initialize-schema=always` is DELETED rather than replaced | Keep it for documentation value; migrate it to a property that still exists | Spring Boot 4.1's `BatchProperties` exposes only the `job` group, so the key is inert — and it was being relied upon in a comment as if it provisioned the `BATCH_*` tables. Those tables come from `V5__batch_metadata.sql` and, on an empty database, from `JdbcBatchConfiguration`. A configuration key that does nothing is worse than none, because it invites the reader to believe the provisioning question is settled | None; the two classes that carried it pass without it |
| A new `SchemaMigrationInventoryIT` in `carddemo-common` pins the applied history: the exact version list in installed order, each description, and `success` on every row | Assert only the count; leave the inventory unpinned; put the test in a service module | Nothing prevented a migration from being dropped, renumbered or re-described, and the absence of a `V4__*.sql` file (version 4 is the JAVA migration `SeededPiiEncryptionMigration`) had already misled several docstrings into describing migrations that do not exist. The test lives next to the migrations it guards, and reads the history that `MigratedSchemaContainer` produced from the committed files rather than a list restated in a fixture | Adding a migration now requires updating two lists in one file, deliberately. `carddemo-common` gained a TEST-scope `org.postgresql:postgresql` dependency, without which its own test-support container is unreachable from its own tests |
| Five of the six classes the finding named were renamed `*Test` → `*IT`; `StatementGenerationJobTest` was NOT | Rename all six as reported; rename none | Measured: `StatementGenerationJobTest` runs 21 cases in 0.17 s and references no container, Spring context or database — it is a pure unit test of the statement formatting helpers, so moving it to the Failsafe tier would slow `verify` and remove real coverage from `mvn test`. The other five each boot a real container or full context (18.6 s, 12.5 s, 5.9 s, 3.1 s and a shared-container case) and belong to the tier `verify` owns | The finding's premise is corrected rather than followed for that one file; recorded here so the discrepancy is not read as an omission |
| Two container-based classes the finding did NOT name — `SecurityUserRepositoryTest` (13.9 s) and `UserRepositoryTest` — were renamed to `*IT` in the same pass | Rename only the enumerated files; leave both for a later finding | They are the same defect the finding describes (a Testcontainers integration test discovered by Surefire), and leaving the two slowest offenders behind would have left `mvn test` carrying container work while claiming a clean partition. Verified that no other `*Test` class starts a container: the remaining slow ones are `@WebMvcTest` slices and context tests with mocked infrastructure | Four class names changed in documentation as well as in code; every reference was updated mechanically and re-checked |
| Stale migration names in test docstrings were corrected to the files that actually exist (`V1__create_security_users_table.sql` / `V2__seed_security_users.sql` → `V1__create_schema.sql` / `V3__seed_test_data.sql`; `V4__create_transaction_id_sequence.sql` → `V1__create_schema.sql`), and the "same V1..V4 scripts" comment in three `application.yml` files now names the real set | Leave the docstrings; delete the references instead of correcting them | Four docstrings and three service configurations named migrations that do not exist, which is what made the migration set look like it had a hole at V4. The traceability matrix already recorded the consolidation; the code comments had not caught up | None; these are comment-only edits, and the new inventory IT now fails the build if the real set changes |

### 53.4 Awaiting an asynchronously launched batch run

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| The two classes that wait for an asynchronously launched Spring Batch run poll with Awaitility (50 ms interval, 300 s budget, zero poll delay) instead of `Thread.sleep` | Keep the sleep loops; use a `CountDownLatch` fed by a `JobExecutionListener`; make the executor synchronous for tests | A fixed sleep loop is wrong in both directions: on a loaded host `JobLaunchParameterIT`'s 300 × 100 ms loop silently fell through and asserted on a still-`STARTING` execution, and on a fast host every poll interval was paid in full. Awaitility returns the moment the execution is terminal and, on timeout, reports the state it was left in. A latch would need production wiring the jobs do not have, and forcing a synchronous executor would remove the very asynchrony these classes exist to exercise | One test-scope dependency added to `batch-service` and `reporting-service`; the version is BOM-managed |
| A new `JobLaunchParameterIT` case asserts that a launch really is carried by the PRODUCTION `asyncJobOperator` and that its terminal status is durable | Rely on the other cases implicitly using the launch surface; assert the executing thread name | The QA finding's substantive point was that the deterministic batch ITs could pass while the production launcher stayed unexercised (`InterestCalculationJobIT` builds its own launcher over a `SyncTaskExecutor`). The new case pins the delegation (`JobSchedulingConfig`'s operator IS the `asyncJobOperator` bean), pins that its executor is asynchronous, and then reads `batch_job_execution.status` back from the database so the COMPLETED it reports is the recorded one rather than the in-memory object's. A thread-name assertion would have been racy and would have proved less | The case launches a real data-management job on every run, which is why the class keeps a private container |

### 53.5 Production session wiring covered per service

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| Each of the five session-carrying services gains ONE production-like `SessionRedisWiringIT` that clears the `test` profile's `spring.autoconfigure.exclude` list and boots the real Spring Data Redis + Spring Session wiring against a shared `redis:8` container | Remove the exclusions from the `test` profile outright; rely on the api-gateway's `SessionRedisRoundTripIT`; assert the wiring with a mocked connection factory | Removing the exclusions wholesale would make every suite in those five modules pay for a Redis container, which is why they were excluded in the first place. Relying on the gateway's single round trip is what the finding objects to: the namespace, the repository filter and the allowlisted-JSON serializer are configured PER SERVICE (each application class must `@Import(SessionRedisConfig)`), so a service that dropped the import or mistyped the namespace would still have passed. A mocked connection factory cannot observe a key name or a serialized payload | Each of the five modules pays for one Redis container in the `verify` phase (measured 10-48 s per class, sharing one container per module). The exclusions stay in place for every other suite |
| The new class asserts four things: the `springSessionRepositoryFilter` bean exists, the repository implementation is Redis-backed, a `SessionContext` round-trips under the `carddemo:session` namespace with its frozen `CDEMO-USRTYP-*` / `CDEMO-PGM-*` values, and the stored payload is `@class`-tagged JSON whose first byte is not the `0xAC` Java-serialization magic | Assert only that the context loads; assert the cookie instead of the store | "The context loads" is exactly the assertion that passed while the wiring was excluded. The cookie is the gateway's concern; what these services must not lose is the STORE contract — namespace, readability and serializer — because that is what makes the COMMAREA equivalent survive a hand-off between services | The payload assertion is coupled to the `sessionAttr:` hash-field convention Spring Session uses; that convention changing would break the test loudly rather than silently |

### 53.6 Shared-container discipline in the account-service integration suites

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| `AccountViewUpdateIT` no longer TRUNCATES `card_xref`; it deletes only the rows of the ONE account it needs unlinked and restores them in `@AfterAll` | Keep the truncate; give the class its own container; recreate the whole seed after each test | Truncating destroyed all 50 seeded cross-references for every later class in the same fork, which is exactly the coupling `MigratedSchemaContainer`'s contract forbids ("tests that mutate seeded rows must restore them"). A private container would double the module's container cost for one unlinked-account scenario, and re-seeding 50 rows per test would make the fixture the test's subject | The class's own two synthetic linkages now coexist with the seeded ones for accounts 1 and 2. The read path takes the FIRST cross-reference by card number, and both candidates name the same account and customer, so the response is identical either way — verified by the class passing in randomized class order |
| `AtomicRollbackIT` writes ONE cross-reference row only when it is absent, deletes exactly that row in teardown, and drops its probe CHECK constraint before adding it as well as after | Keep `deleteAll()` on the cross-reference repository; leave the row behind; add the constraint without a defensive drop | `deleteAll()` removed the seeded linkage every sibling class reads, and the previous teardown docstring claimed to remove the row it actually left behind. PostgreSQL has no `ADD CONSTRAINT IF NOT EXISTS`, so an interrupted run left a name that made the next `@BeforeEach` fail for a reason unrelated to the test | The probe constraint exists on `accounts` for the duration of each case in this class; any sibling case running concurrently in the same fork would see it, which is safe because Surefire/Failsafe run one class at a time per fork |
| Account `SecurityContractIT` no longer creates `card_xref` and no longer overrides `ddl-auto` | Keep the `CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS` as a safety net; keep `ddl-auto=none` | The statement created a FOREIGN-KEY-FREE copy of the table, which is the exact shape that silently disarmed the referential-integrity assertions in sibling classes; against the migrated schema it was a no-op, so it bought nothing and risked everything. Its docstring also mis-stated ownership ("the card-service owns the migration"): the table comes from the SHARED `V1__create_schema.sql` and no service ships per-service migrations. Removing the `ddl-auto` override lets the profile's `validate` assert the entity mappings here too — measured green | None; the class asserts 401s and header policy and touches no data |
| The two PII failure messages are BOTH retained, each asserted in exactly one place: `PiiEncryptionException.MESSAGE` ("Unable to process protected customer data") is the caller-facing frozen text the exception handlers substitute, and "Unable to decrypt sensitive attribute from persistence" is the operator-facing detail on the raised exception, which is logged and never returned | Collapse to one message; assert only the caller-facing one | The two are not drift, they are two audiences: a caller must not learn WHICH crypto operation failed, and an operator must. Collapsing them would either leak the diagnosis into the response or discard it from the log. Runtime-verified: a corrupted at-rest token makes the API answer `500 {"message":"Unable to process protected customer data"}` while the service log carries the decrypt detail | Two strings must stay in step with their two owners; each has exactly one assertion site and one producer |

### 53.7 One authoritative card-list authorization and paging contract

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| The card-list browse stays ROLE INDEPENDENT and the contradiction is closed in the LOG, not in the code: the Q15 row in §11.2 is prefixed `SUPERSEDED (§19.1)`, §11.2 gains a note stating what ships, and the §19.1 row is annotated `SUPERSEDES the Q15 row in §11.2` | Implement the §11.2 row instead (scope a non-admin browse to its session account and return no rows when nothing is pinned); delete the §11.2 row outright; leave both rows standing and let the reader pick | `COCRDLIC 9500-FILTER-RECORDS` filters on the `ACCTSID` the operator typed and contains NO user-type branch, and AAP §0.7.1 requires validation rules and workflow sequencing reproduced exactly while §0.7.6 forbids enhancing business processes — so implementing the §11.2 row would deny requests the legacy grants. Deleting the row would erase the reasoning that was reversed, which the Explainability rule requires to stay auditable; leaving both would keep two mutually unsatisfiable contracts on record. Prefixing plus a bidirectional cross-reference leaves exactly ONE contract in force and keeps the retired reasoning readable | A signed-on `ROLE_USER` can page the whole card base by card number, exactly as on the 3270. Mitigated by the admin role gate on the admin screens, TLS and PII masking per AAP §0.6.7; the residual risk is recorded here and in §19.1 rather than in the code `[app/cbl/COCRDLIC.cbl:L1382-L1390; AAP §0.7.1, §0.7.6]` |
| The unit specification asserts the delivered contract POSITIVELY (`listCards_unfilteredBrowseIsIdenticalForAnOrdinaryUserAndAnAdministrator` compares the mapped rows a `CDEMO-USRTYP-USER` session receives with an administrator's, and `listCards_acctFilter_readsNoSessionFieldToScopeTheBrowse` proves no session field is read) | Keep the two rows it replaces (`listCards_nonAdminUnpinnedNoFilter_doesNotWidenToFindAll`, which asserted the SUPERSEDED contract, and `listCards_nonAdminOwnAcctFilter_isHonoured`, a duplicate of `listCards_acctFilter_scopesBrowseForEveryRole`); simply delete both without replacement | The replaced case was also VACUOUS: it stubbed no window, so the empty row list it asserted came from Mockito's default return, not from any scoping the service performs — it would have passed against BOTH contracts. The two new cases fail the moment a per-principal predicate is reintroduced, which is the only guard §19.1 needs | `verifyNoInteractions(session)` also freezes the fact that `listCards` propagates nothing INTO the session; a future screen field that must be remembered across the list screen has to be published on the response DTO, as the resolved selection already is |
| The production docstrings that still described "admin-versus-user account scoping" (`CardController.listCards`) and a session the browse "propagates the resolved selection into" (both `CardService.listCards` overloads) were corrected to the delivered behaviour | Leave the docstrings and rely on the decision log alone; delete the `sessionContext` parameter from the read path | The Docstring rule requires a docstring to define its parameters, and these three described the reversed contract — the same dead intent the log carried. The parameter itself is kept because the seven-argument overload is the screen-contract signature shared with the detail and update paths | None; no behaviour changed |
| The seven-rows-per-page browse window stays an explicit `offset` / `limit` `Pageable` (`ScreenWindow(offset = (page - 1) * 7, limit = 8)`) rather than `PageRequest.of(page - 1, 8)` | Fetch seven rows with `PageRequest.of(page - 1, 7)` and probe for a further row with a second query; count the whole browse and derive the page count | `PageRequest` multiplies the page index by the PAGE SIZE, so a lookahead-sized page advanced the offset by eight while showing seven — one card was skipped at every boundary and six of fifty were unreachable. One window of `7 + 1` rows keeps `WS-MAX-SCREEN-LINES + 1` (the record COBOL reads to learn a further page exists) in a single query while the offset advances by seven | `ScreenWindow` implements `Pageable` by hand, so `getPageNumber()` reports the SCREEN index (`offset / 7`); the class documents this and `listCards_readsOnlyOnePageWindowPlusOneLookaheadRow` pins it `[app/cbl/COCRDLIC.cbl:L1382-L1390]` |

### 53.8 Frontend test-suite quality: awaited interactions, StrictMode and identifier masking

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| `AccountUpdatePage` publishes its screen chrome from `useLayoutEffect`, not `useEffect` — the same choice §17 already records for `CardUpdatePage` | Leave the passive effect and silence the diagnostic in the suite; publish through a reducer keyed on a save counter; keep a `useRef` latest-value box for the handlers | Passive effects are flushed on a scheduled task, so the message line, the `busy` flag and the PF-key `onActivate` closures reached the shell one frame late: an AID pressed in that frame ran against the previous screen state. It is also what produced every "An update to ScreenFrame inside a test was not wrapped in act(...)" diagnostic — the update landed outside every scope. Measured: the change removes ALL such diagnostics repo-wide (2 of the 4 reported) | A layout effect runs synchronously at commit, so the shell re-renders in the same commit; the effect's deps are per-render stable values, so it cannot loop. `CardUpdatePage` has shipped this way since §17 |
| The sign-on suite presses the AID keys through awaited `act` helpers (`await pressEnterKey()` / `await pressPf3Key()`, each dispatching inside `await act(async () => { … await Promise.resolve(); })`) | Leave the bare `fireEvent.keyDown`; wrap each call site individually; wait on the outcome with `waitFor` at every site | The legend handler calls the page's ASYNC submit, so the validation state lands in a promise continuation and the chrome republish in a passive effect after it — a bare `fireEvent` opens only a synchronous scope, and both settled outside it. Measured: the sign-on suite's two diagnostics both disappear, and its 30 cases still pass unchanged | The helper is `async`, so all nine call sites had to be awaited; the `await Promise.resolve()` inside the callback is what makes the scope asynchronous (and satisfies `@typescript-eslint/require-await`) |
| StrictMode is proven by ONE dedicated suite, `frontend/src/StrictModeMount.test.tsx`, which mounts the REAL `App` at all nineteen routes with Testing Library's `reactStrictMode` render option | Set `configure({ reactStrictMode: true })` globally in `setupTests.ts`; add a StrictMode case to each of the seventeen page suites; wrap a shared render helper (none exists) | Measured on the global option: 11 suites and 61 cases fail, because every entry read fires twice and the suites assert `toHaveBeenCalledTimes(1)`. Double invocation is a DEVELOPMENT diagnostic — a production build runs each effect once — so relaxing those counts would replace a real contract ("one request per entry", which AAP §0.7.1's p95 budget depends on) with a dev-only artifact, and de-duplicating the read would be the caching AAP §0.7.6 forbids. The dedicated suite instead asserts what StrictMode is FOR: the entry read repeats with the SAME url and never a third time, no screen writes from a mount effect, cleanup is symmetric, and the double-mounted screen renders text identical to a single mount | The route/read table is maintained by hand; a new screen with an entry read must be added to it. The doubled read log is asserted explicitly (`[path, path]`), so the suite fails if the render option is ever ignored — it cannot silently stop testing StrictMode |
| The EFT account id joins the SSN and the government-issued id in the backend read-path masking assertion (`AccountViewUpdateIT#getAccount_masksRegulatedCustomerIdentifiers`), and both fixed-width identifiers are asserted to keep their stored length | Assert only the two identifiers the review named; assert the EFT mask in a separate case | `AccountMapper` masks all THREE identifiers on both read paths (L213-216, L274-277) and the frontend suite already asserts all three, so the backend covered two of the three the mapper protects. The length assertion is what distinguishes a mask from a truncation | None; the assertion reads the stored value through JPA and rebuilds the expected mask with `PiiMasker`, so it cannot drift from the production masker |
| ONE React diagnostic is accepted and recorded rather than removed: a single `A component suspended inside an act scope, but the act call was not awaited` from `AccountUpdatePage.test.tsx` | Relax or restructure the four cases whose write rejects; disable Testing Library's automatic cleanup globally (`RTL_SKIP_AUTO_CLEANUP`); unmount inside every case; unmount the session harness probe inside an awaited scope | Measured origin: Testing Library's AUTOMATIC cleanup unmounts from a SYNCHRONOUS `act` it never awaits, and a tree still holding the work a rejected write queued leaves that queue occupied when the scope closes. Running the one case with `RTL_SKIP_AUTO_CLEANUP=true` makes the diagnostic disappear, which is what identified it. React caps it at one per suite (`didWarnNoAwaitAct`), and twelve measured variants — awaited raw-DOM AID dispatch, `waitFor` drains, multi-tick act drains, per-case unmounts in all 34 cases, an awaited harness mount and unmount, an awaited explicit `cleanup()` — each left exactly one. Disabling automatic cleanup globally is the only lever that removes it and it breaks 25 of the 34 cases by leaking the DOM between them | The diagnostic is emitted by the test framework, not by application code, and no production defect underlies it: the 409 path is asserted in three cases and was verified against the running services (a stale `version` returns 409 with the frozen `Record changed by some one else. Please review`). A human wanting zero output can raise it with Testing Library or opt the suite out of automatic cleanup once that no longer leaks |

## 54. Screen-Shell Heading Reset — the Body Region's Row Budget on the Account Screens

One decision taken while verifying the delivered screen shell end to end in a browser. The
routed screen body is deliberately sized to exactly nineteen character rows
(`.screen__body { flex: 1 1 calc(19 * var(--term-line)); overflow: auto }`) so a screen with
more content than the frame can show gives way THERE rather than carrying the line-23 message
row and the line-24 key legend off the device. That budget only holds if every element inside
the body occupies whole character rows, which is what the body's heading reset exists to
guarantee — and the reset was not reaching every heading level the screens actually use.

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| The body heading reset lists EVERY level — `main h1, main h2, main h3, main h4, main h5, main h6 { margin: 0; font: inherit; font-weight: normal }` — rather than the first three | Reset only the levels in use today and extend the list again whenever a screen adds one; give the account screens' sub-section heading a class and reset the class; move the sub-section back up to `h3` | The account view and the account update screen carry a sub-section heading (`Customer Details`) UNDER their `h3` screen title, so it is an `h4`, and an `h4` the reset did not reach kept the user agent's `margin: 1.33em 0` (21.28px above and below at the 16px cell) and its bold weight. Two rows of margin is two rows of the nineteen, and the account view needs all of them: it measured `clientHeight 395` against `scrollHeight 417`, so its last row — `Phone 2`, `EFT Account Id` and `Primary Card Holder Y/N` — rendered OUTSIDE the visible region and had to be scrolled to, on a screen whose whole point is that a 3270 never scrolls. Resetting the level list is the fix the rule's own rationale already states ("a 3270 screen has one uniform character cell: no browser heading size, weight, or margin"); it was simply written before a fourth level existed. Raising the heading back to `h3` was rejected because the heading LEVEL is a tested accessibility contract (`AccountViewPage.test.tsx` and `AccountUpdatePage.test.tsx` both assert `level: 4` for `Customer Details` under a `level: 3` screen title), and a per-class reset would leave the same trap set for the next screen that adds a level | None observable: the reset only REMOVES user-agent margin and weight, so it can only shorten a body region, never lengthen one, and it can only act on levels the previous rule left unstyled. Verified in the browser on the shipped bundle: the account view's body region measures `clientHeight 395` / `scrollHeight 395` (was 417), all three previously clipped fields render fully inside the visible box at `scrollTop: 0` with 20.65px of headroom, the heading is still `<h4 class="neutral">` at `margin 0` / `16px` / weight `400` / one 20.8px row, the page itself still does not scroll (`documentElement.scrollHeight 800` = `innerHeight 800`, `scrollY` pinned at 0), and the key legend still sits at 478–510px. The three list screens are unaffected and measure `395 / 395` bodies with unchanged titles and column counts at both 1280px and 375px |
| The account UPDATE screen's remaining body overflow is RECORDED here, not closed | Enlarge that screen's body region; shrink the editable rows; split the screen | `COACTUP` is an editable screen: its 43 entry fields are border-box inputs 24px tall against a 20.8px character row (+3.2px on each of eighteen entry rows) and it carries twenty logical rows in the nineteen-row region, which together measure `clientHeight 395` against `scrollHeight 474`. None of that is heading margin — with the reset in force both `h4` margins are 0px — so it is a separate matter from the row above, and closing it means changing the entry-row metric or that screen's row budget, which is a presentation change to every editable screen rather than a repair to this rule | The line-23 message row and the line-24 key legend remain on the device (measured at 436.78, 457.58 and 478–510px) and the page still does not scroll, so the property the nineteen-row budget exists to protect is intact; what an operator loses is that the last entry rows of that one screen need the body region scrolled, with no scrollbar gutter drawn to advertise it. Recorded so the next presentation pass has the measurement rather than having to rediscover it |
